#3171. Nasdaq-100 Correction: 2 Proven Winners That Can Buck Historic Volatility

Two non-tech stocks are the perfect buys amid a 15% correction in the Nasdaq-100.

Although the artificial intelligence (AI)-driven bull market over the last four years has sent Wall Street's major stock indexes to several record highs, the last few weeks have served as a reminder that stocks can, indeed, go down. Last week, on Nov. 5, the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +2.74%) logged the largest single-session point decline in its storied history (1,422 points), while the Nasdaq-100 reached a nearly 15% correction in a month.

While a retracement has arguably been long overdue for high-flying tech stocks, several bargains abound within the Nasdaq-100 for patient investors. Two proven winners from outside the tech sector, Intuitive Surgical (ISRG +1.71%) and O'Reilly Automotive (ORLY -0.11%), can buck this historic volatility.

Though you can certainly find cheaper healthcare stocks, based on the price-to-earnings ratio, you'd struggle mightily to find a healthcare company with a sustained double-digit growth rate or with as large a moat as robotic-assisted surgical systems developer Intuitive Surgical.

Intuitive holds a veritable monopoly in robotic-assisted surgical systems, with more than 12,000 of its da Vinci systems installed worldwide. The relatively high cost of these systems, coupled with a lack of competition, often locks hospitals and surgical centers in as long-term customers.

What's arguably even more important than its virtual monopoly status is its revenue mix. In the early 2000s, Intuitive generated the bulk of its revenue from selling its high-priced, but mediocre-margin systems. As time has passed, higher-margin sales channels, including accessories sold with each procedure and system servicing, have grown into a majority of net sales. In short, this is a recipe for Intuitive's profits to grow even faster than its sales.

Innovation matters, too. In addition to developing new generations of the da Vinci surgical system, Intuitive debuted its minimally invasive lung biopsy system (Ion endoluminal system) in 2019. Ion procedures jumped by 31% in the latest quarter.

Intuitive Surgical stock may not be cheap, but there are several reasons it can maintain a healthy premium.

Another proven winner within the Nasdaq-100 that can buck this recent volatility is auto parts retailer O'Reilly Automotive.

Macro trends certainly favor O'Reilly. According to a May 2025 report from S&P Global Mobility (now Mobility Global (MBGL +1.12%)), the average age of vehicles in the U.S. rose to a record 12.8 years, up from an average of 11.1 years in 2012. Though newer vehicles are built to last longer, the aging of America's vehicles suggests drivers and mechanics will be relying on companies like O'Reilly Automotive to keep them in tip-top shape.

O'Reilly's success is also a function of its hub-and-spoke distribution model. The company's 32 distribution centers provide overnight access to more than 156,000 stock-keeping units (SKUs). Nearly 400 larger hub stores can also provide up to 115,000 SKUs on a same-day basis.

ORLY Shares Outstanding (Quarterly) data by YCharts.

But the one factor that's really set O'Reilly Automotive apart, at least from an investment standpoint, is its top-notch share repurchase program. Since the inception of its buyback program in January 2011, O'Reilly has spent just over $31 billion to retire 1.5 billion outstanding shares (OS). The company has reduced its OS by 62% in less than 16 years, meaningfully boosting its earnings per share in the process.

Tech-driven volatility doesn't change the investment thesis for O'Reilly Automotive one bit.

Sean Williams has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Intuitive Surgical and Mobility Global. The Motley Fool recommends the following options: long January 2028 $520 calls on Intuitive Surgical and short January 2028 $530 calls on Intuitive Surgical. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3170. November 2026 Skywatching Guide: Meteors, Planets, Stars and a Full Moon

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

This month, you'll find three meteor showers and a planetary alignment starring four planets.

Cool nights, cool sights. November is the final full month of fall, and the sun sets even earlier now that daylight saving time has ended. That’ll give skygazers more time to see some cool sights without staying up horribly late or waking up unbearably early.

This month is incredibly active for amateur astronomers, with things to see as late as Nov. 30. While there aren’t any major celestial events, a series of smaller ones makes up for it.

Every planet is visible during the month of November, though depending on the time and day, you’ll get a better or worse view of them. Mercury and Venus are the most difficult to see due to their proximity to the sun.

Mercury: Mercury was at its eastern elongation during October, so it starts November a bit too close to the sun to see safely. It reaches western elongation on Nov. 20, when it’ll be easily visible just before sunrise for the rest of the month.

Venus: Venus starts the month just ahead of the sun at sunrise, technically making it possible to see every morning in November. It gets farther from the sun as the month goes on, making it easier and safer to see toward the end of the month.

Mars: Mars is high in the sky all month. It rises out of the eastern horizon shortly after midnight at the start of November and rises earlier as the month progresses. Early morning is the best time to see it, or right around midnight toward the end of the month.

Jupiter: Jupiter and Mars are close pals for all of November, rising at roughly the same time and right next to each other in the sky. The star Regulus joins in to form a trio, which is visible to the naked eye the entire month.

Saturn: Saturn is also visible every night in November. It’s in the eastern sky when the sun sets, and sets on the western horizon shortly before sunrise. Right after dark is the best time to see it, but you’ll need binoculars or a telescope to capture its glorious rings.

Neptune: Neptune and Saturn are teamed up for November, with both of them being very close in the sky and following a similar trajectory. Bring binoculars or a telescope.

Uranus: Uranus is in the night sky every day in November, rising in the east shortly before sunset and setting in the west right around sunrise. It reaches opposition on Nov. 25, which means it’s as far away from the sun as it can get, and will be the most visible. You’ll still want to bring some binoculars or a telescope for best results.

Most of the planets are visible in the night sky almost every night in November, but on Saturday, there’ll be a fun little planet parade. It’ll take place just before sunrise, so it’s mostly for early risers. Mercury, Venus, Mars and Jupiter are set to line up nicely, making them easy to spot. This is especially true for Jupiter and Mars, which should be right next to each other that night.

The show will start around Saturday and will last until around Nov. 25, depending on your location. The tricky part is catching Mercury in the sky before the sun comes up. It’s only about a 30-minute window since the sun closely follows Mercury up over the eastern horizon. The single best day to view it is Nov. 20, when Mercury is at its western elongation — as far from the sun as it can get.

Uranus is also visible at this time, bringing the number of visible planets to three. Saturn and Neptune are below the horizon. Uranus is on the other side of the sky, however, so it’s not technically part of the alignment.

If you miss the Southern Taurids, you only have to wait a week to catch a glimpse of the Northern Taurids meteor shower. This is another long-lasting meteor shower that started on Oct. 20 and runs until Dec. 10. It peaks on Wednesday evening and Thursday morning. The American Meteor Society notes that the moon is only 5% illuminated on Wednesday, giving viewers even better conditions than during the Southern Taurids.

It is otherwise virtually identical to the Southern Taurids. The shower appears to originate from the same constellation, Taurus, which rises and sets at almost exactly the same times as it did a week earlier. The Northern Taurids promise about five meteors per hour under optimal conditions, with an increased chance of fireballs. Both showers are even fueled by the same comet, 2P/Encke. It’s the space equivalent of a sitcom rerun airing just one week later.

Leonids is usually the most active meteor shower in November. It started on Nov. 6 and runs until Nov. 30, making it shorter than both Taurids meteor showers. It peaks on the evening of Nov. 16 and the morning of Nov. 17.

The good news is that the meteor shower is very easy to find. Meteors from it will appear to originate from the Leo constellation, which is right next to Mars and Jupiter, which are visible in the night sky.

The bad news is that this part of the sky doesn’t rise until around midnight, making this a meteor shower for night owls. It should produce about 15 meteors per hour under optimal conditions, though you can expect slightly fewer because the 41% illuminated moon will create enough natural light pollution to obscure fainter meteors.

The Leonids have produced exceptional meteor storms in the past, with hundreds of meteors per hour. That isn’t expected to happen in 2026, but the odds aren’t zero. They may improve after the comet loops around the sun again in 2031.

A micro moon is noticeably smaller and less bright than a supermoon or even a regular full moon.

November’s Beaver Moon takes place just two days before Thanksgiving. It is also the first of a string of four consecutive supermoons that will close out 2026 and begin 2027. This happens when the moon is within 90% of perigee — its closest point to Earth. The phenomenon makes the moon appear about 7% larger than a regular moon and 14% larger than a minimoon — and noticeably brighter than both.

Per The Old Farmer’s Almanac, the Beaver Moon reaches its peak illumination on Nov. 24 at 9:53 a.m. ET, which is after sunrise for most people in the US. That means the best time to see it is either the night before (Nov. 23) or the evening after (Nov. 24). The moon remains over 90% full from Nov. 22-27. If you plan on going skygazing, avoid those days like the plague, since the moon creates a ton of light pollution.

It’s not often that the same event happens twice in a single month, but it turns out Jupiter, Mars and the moon just can’t seem to get enough of each other. The trio closes out the month the same way it started: hanging out very close together in the night sky. And yes, Regulus will once again tag along.

Unlike the prior meetup, this one lasts for only one night before the moon dashes away. Since all four of them are visible with the naked eye and the moon is still mostly full, people shouldn’t have any trouble finding them. Eagle-eyed stargazers may see Algieba nearby as well. It’s the second brightest star in the Leo constellation (behind Regulus), and it’s also visible without binoculars or a telescope.

The constellations continue their summer-to-winter shakeup. Prominent constellations in the sky include Taurus, Pegasus, Capricornus, Aquila, Pisces and Aries. Mainstays such as the Big Dipper and Little Dipper are still out there, but can be difficult to see if you live too far south. As a rule, we recommend using a sky map or a tool such as Stellarium to pinpoint each object.

There are also some lesser-known asterisms to seek out, such as the Pleiades (the Seven Sisters), the Great Square of Pegasus and the Circlet of Pisces. The Summer Triangle is still visible, while the Winter Triangle becomes much easier to spot as it rises above the horizon earlier in the evening.

#3169. You can change Windows settings directly from a search now – how to try it

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Windows > Windows 11

The latest Windows 11 Insiders Preview enhances the Search feature so that you can access specific settings right from a search window.

Microsoft has been on a kick lately trying to improve and enhance Windows 11, including sprucing up Search, which is now simpler, cleaner, and friendlier. And there’s another cool trick that Search has up its sleeve: Available in the Beta channel of the latest Windows 11 Insiders Preview, Search will give you access to specific Windows settings and commands. Known as inline actions, this latest skill is designed to help you directly complete certain tasks. That means you no longer need to know exactly where or how to find the setting you need.

Also: Windows 11 can run better on PCs with 8GB now

Curious about built-in features such as Dark mode, Bluetooth, screen brightness, Remote Desktop, the Windows clipboard, or Windows Update? Just type the name or phrase in the Search field. The results then display an Open button that you can select to go directly to the related setting.

“A new Windows Search experience built on WinUI 3 is beginning to roll out,” Microsoft said on a web page announcing the latest Insiders build. “It’s faster, more efficient, and features a streamlined design that makes results easier to scan. Inline actions let you quickly complete tasks directly. Turn on dark mode or Bluetooth, adjust your screen brightness, mute your device, or arrange windows from Windows Search.”

I tried this with several different Windows settings, and the results window pointed me to the right screens for tweaking them. Though I know where most Windows settings are located by now, there are still times when I forget how to reach certain ones. This new skill promises to be a time saver whenever I face that challenge.

Also: 11 Windows apps I replace on every new PC

Here’s another handy enhancement. How often do you type a word or term in Windows Search only to misspell it? With the latest update, Search will now better understand what you actually mean and match you with the correct results. Type “drak” instead of “dark,” for example, and you’ll still see the results for Dark mode.

Microsoft promises these latest improvements to Windows Search will continue to be fine-tuned over the next few weeks. And there’s more in the new Preview build.

Ever run into trouble using Windows Hello facial recognition in a dark area? Now, your screen will temporarily brighten up to help you sign in under those conditions. To enable this option, head to Settings, select Account, select Sign-in options, and then click Facial recognition. Turn on the switch for “Automatically adjust screen brightness during face recognition.”

The latest Insiders build also tweaks features such as Live captions, Gaming, File Explorer, and Microsoft 365 cloud storage.

Also: How I used ChatGPT to talk me through fixing a corrupted gaming PC

To grab this update, you’ll have to join the Windows Insiders Program if you’re not already registered. You can then sign up for the Beta or Experimental channel, either of which will give you access to the new Search skills and other features. Make sure you’re running the Beta Preview Build 26320.9876 or higher. To check, go to Settings, select System, and then click About. The Windows Info section will indicate the build number.

As for regular Windows users not enrolled in the Insiders Program, the new changes should reach you relatively soon. I’d expect them to roll out with the December Patch Tuesday update arriving on December 8, so that Microsoft can squeeze them in before the end of the year.

#3168. Ring’s Flying Home Security Drone Is Finally Here, and It’s Surprisingly Affordable

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Ring's $250 flying indoor security camera is set to arrive in January. We got a first look at Ring Drone in action.

Ring Home Security has taken flight, and the brand’s tiny home security drone is surprisingly affordable. The $250 mini indoor drone camera was announced on Tuesday as part of the brand’s new arm, Ring Robotics. Ring Drone uses a learned map of your home to zoom around autonomously for up to 8 minutes at a time with custom routes to record places a couple of fixed cameras can’t.

The small drone weighs just 5.8 ounces and houses a pair of 2K cameras mounted on the front and bottom, respectively. The cameras use bright LED lights to enable day or night monitoring, sending a live feed or recorded video to your device. The LEDs are also meant to ward off potential intruders peering in from the outside.

You can program the flying cam to make regular flights, sending it on target missions to check on the dog or see if the stove was left on. The Ring Drone also syncs with other Ring cameras and sensors to automatically respond when windows and doors are opened at certain hours.

If you’re thinking, “wait, didn’t Ring already launch a drone camera?” you’d be right — sort of. The brand “launched” the Ring Always Home Cam in 2020. The $250 indoor drone saw a limited invitation-only program but made it to public release. We’ve been assured this latest iteration is ready for primetime, though it’s still not available for preorder on Amazon.

Ring founder and CEO Jamie Siminoff demoed the drone, which has been in development for more than five years, in an event space set up to replicate a home. During the demo, the small drone lifted gently from the dock and rose to about eye level where it hovered as if to say “hello” to the group of journalists gathered around.

Read more: When Flock Comes to Town: Everything You Should Know About This Tech

Once airborne, the drone turned toward the adjacent room and whirred off to investigate. After a few seconds of scanning, the drone returned to the first room, docked and resumed charging.

While in flight, the Ring Drone made a notably loud buzz reminiscent of a golf cart in reverse. Siminoff was quick to note that the sound was an intentional choice intended to deter potential intruders and alert home dwellers to the drone’s presence.

The 1-minute flight was enough to cover two medium-sized rooms, though the drone can fly up to eight minutes on a full charge before needing about an hour to recharge. Ring reps told me it can monitor a home up to 3,500 square feet – more than enough for most – but Siminoff says they plan to improve the range over time.

If you’re worried about the drone knocking over vases and flying into ceiling fans like I was, Siminoff assured us the preprogrammed coverage zone is tight.

The drone is powered by AI Pilot, “a custom-built system that uses the device’s cameras, specialized vision models, sensors and cloud processing to see, think, and navigate autonomously.” (We’ll see for ourselves once we get a unit in for testing.)

To create your drone’s fly zone, you carry it through the space where it intelligently identifies rooms and suggests patrol points like doors, windows or a nursery. You can add rooms and areas based on your home’s layout and security preferences.

The $250 Ring Drone Camera isn’t yet available for preorder, but Ring representatives at the launch event assured us it will be soon, and units are set to begin shipping in late January 2027. We’ll update this story when it becomes available for preorder or purchase.

#3167. Down 24% This Year, Is MercadoLibre (MELI) an Obvious Growth Stock to Buy in November?

MercadoLibre has been a highly volatile stock, but it has significant growth potential.

MercadoLibre (MELI +1.50%) is often described as a cross between Amazon.com and PayPal, as it encompasses both a dominant online marketplace (MercadoLibre) and a major "fintech" business (Mercado Pago). You might not have heard of it, though, as it's focused on the Latin American market, which is rather... huge. According to one report, the retail market is valued at $2.0 trillion in 2026 and is expected to reach $3.4 trillion by 2034.

So why is the stock down, and is it worth considering for your portfolio? Let's see.

Founded in 1999 and headquartered in Montevideo, Uruguay, MercadoLibre sports a market value near $76 billion (as of Nov. 6). It's active in 18 countries and was recently delivering 3.2 billion items annually. It has 136 million unique buyers and 93 million monthly active users (both numbers as of the third quarter).

The stock has been volatile in recent years:

Source: Morningstar.com, as of Nov. 5, 2026.

That can make plenty of investors skittish, but remember that while the average annual gain over the past three years is just 3.31%, it's 21% over the past 15 years. It can help to be a long-term investor.

Investors worried about MercadoLibre are concerned about factors such as:

Those are quite reasonable concerns, but the company has a lot going for it, as well. And some of those concerns are not as bad as they may seem. For example, the shrinking profit margins are partly due to the company extending more free shipping and credit to shoppers. That can build loyalty and can help it compete against rivals and would-be rivals. It's also due to the company investing in expanding its fulfillment capabilities, which can pay off with better service for customers.

Meanwhile, the stock is appealingly priced at recent levels, with a forward-looking price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 28, well below the five-year average of 50, and a recent price-to-sales ratio of 2.0, well below the five-year average of 4.8.

Long-term investors seeking businesses with solid growth potential, especially those looking for investments based outside the U.S. for diversification, should take a closer look at MercadoLibre.

#3166. Can you still trust Proton?

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The company’s reputation took a major hit two weeks ago, when 404 Media published a detailed report, based on leaked Slack messages and interviews with anonymous insiders. The story detailed a push by Proton CEO Andy Yen to — in his own words — “ruthlessly smash everything that stands in the way of AI velocity.”

Also: Is your browser’s free VPN enough? I tested 4 against Proton VPN

After one employee proposed spending at least a few days on penetration testing before shipping new code, Yen reportedly said, “That’s bullshit and loser thinking. Of course it’s possible to be fast and secure. Stop thinking always of how to be slower, and focus more on how to be faster.” According to 404 Media’s account, Yen’s message for Proton engineers was to “stop lovingly handcrafting all our code and use AI more.”

After the original story broke, 404 Media posted a follow-up story reporting that the company had canceled its “re-interviews” that were intended to assess how developers were adopting AI. A 404 Media podcast was titled “Proton’s CEO Is Putting AI Before Security.”

Reddit has an especially active Proton community with 345,000 weekly visitors. It quickly filled up with so many comments — the majority of them negative — that moderators created a single megathread to help keep track of the different threads, including pages for general criticism, support of the AI usage, and “leaving your goodbyes.”

Also: Best VPN services 2026: Expert tested and recommended

Given the level of interest, it’s not surprising that Yen chose that forum to post a public message to the community. In it, he apologized for “hurtful” remarks, adding that he’s “determined to do better.”

He also addressed the topic of AI-based security reviews:

But most importantly, I need to categorically state that our use of AI will never be at the expense of security. In the past year, contrary to what has been alleged, actually additional layers of security review have been added to meet the threat posed by hostile AIs scanning Proton’s open source code for vulnerabilities. Fast and secure need not be mutually exclusive. We also leverage AI to find vulnerabilities in our own code before attackers do. Our code is open source, and its security remains open for all to verify. [emphasis in original]

Nothing in the 404 Media reporting demonstrates that Proton weakened its encryption or shipped insecure code because of AI. The damage is different: Yen’s remarks undermined confidence that management will consistently respect the security processes its customers rely on.

In a blog post that Proton shared with ZDNET ahead of its publication today, the company followed up on Yen’s promise to “lift the hood on how we use AI internally at Proton.” The post, titled “How Proton keeps your data secure in the age of AI,” was signed by the Proton Team and emphasizes Proton’s secure architecture, with end-to-end, zero-access encryption:

We do not hold the keys to your emails, files, or passwords, so we cannot read them, and no AI tool can, either. Our documented security governance policies have not changed since June 2024, before AI was even a major factor in our work here, and they all still apply.

On Reddit, Yen said, “AI is here, and it will unquestionably change how software is developed forever, and Proton must also change.” Today’s post clarified that code receives the same checks whether it is written by a human, by an AI agent, or by a combination of both. Every change receives automated security scans but also requires human approval; sensitive changes, such as core cryptography work, require review by specialist teams. Major feature designs are also reviewed for security, dependencies, testing, and deployment plans.

Also: I can access my home PCs remotely for free – no VPN needed

Those policies address the most serious technical concerns raised by the current controversy, although outsiders have no way to know from the policy alone how consistently those safeguards are followed in day-to-day development.

Should a modern software company use AI for security? Proton argues that it can’t afford not to.

Because of AI, attacks that once might have required a nation-state can now be executed by almost anyone. A defender relying on manual processes and hand-crafted tools is bringing a knife to a gunfight. An attacker only needs to find one flaw, while a defender needs to close all of them. AI is the only tool that makes that defense possible. Cybersecurity is an arms race, and the teams working on security at Proton have been some of the earliest adopters of AI, because the safety of your data depends on winning that race.

But that argument doesn’t really address the objection. The current controversy isn’t about whether Proton should use AI to find vulnerabilities; it’s about whether pressure for “AI velocity” can override the judgment of engineers responsible for keeping its products secure.

Proton says it uses AI to review code changes, identify vulnerabilities through continuous security reviews, and monitor an attack surface that has “grown far beyond what unaided humans could monitor.” The company uses its own models alongside non-public cyber models from OpenAI and Anthropic.

The modern software development landscape is torn by two opposing forces. On one side, you have AI-based tools that are routinely finding nightmarish vulnerabilities in existing codebases and then exploiting them immediately. On the other side, you have a global movement that’s skeptical of the benefits of AI and outright opposed to construction of the data centers where they run.

Much of Proton’s growth over the past few years has been the direct result of people trying to escape big tech. So it shouldn’t be a surprise that many of Proton’s customers — at least those who are passionate enough to visit a Reddit forum — are deeply skeptical of anything related to AI.

Also: Are free VPNs legit? I asked security experts to learn the true cost

In a traditional software company, that realization would have led management to cautiously roll out AI-based features and reveal its AI-centric security strategy more deliberately. That didn’t happen here, obviously. Those blunt remarks from the CEO exposed a management culture that many employees and customers found troubling. It’s difficult to restore trust, both within the workforce and among customers, after that kind of public display of contempt.

Ironically, one of Yen’s comments from the original 404 Media story describes the company’s current dilemma with devastating accuracy.

In a Slack message, Yen told a Proton engineer, “perception is more important than truth, the truth doesn’t matter if you don’t win on perception.” The implication was that he was urging the employee to lie, or at least shade the truth. In fact, as Yen himself told a random Reddit commenter:

I do not believe perception should be more important than truth. I am stating that unfortunately it often ends up being the case. If you place it into the context in which it was said, it can be understood as, “it doesn’t matter that the truth is that Proton’s PQC implementation is better, this truth is irrelevant as long as it is not perceived to be this way, and we therefore need to correct that perception”

That’s Proton’s challenge now, distilled to its essence. It takes seconds to destroy a reputation. It can take months or years to rebuild the perception that the company and its management can no longer be trusted.

Personally, I will continue to use Proton Pass and Proton VPN, both of which are solid, reliable products. But I’ve moved my secondary email accounts (which I had migrated from Gmail to Proton Mail); they’re now running on Fastmail. I had been considering making the switch for a long time, primarily because Proton Mail’s performance has been too slow for my taste. Current events just hastened that switch.

#3165. CoreWeave credit default swaps hit distress levels as investors sour on AI debt

CoreWeave's credit stress continued to spiral on Thursday amid a broader sell-off in AI-related names.

CoreWeave — a so-called neocloud company — has taken on more than $51 billion of debt and obligations to finance its AI infrastructure build-out. It's now facing a rebellion from stock and bond investors as concerns mount about AI overspending and debt levels.

Yields on CoreWeave debt have continued to climb, with the yield to maturity on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 reaching 15.78%. Bond yields move inversely to prices. Meanwhile, the spread on its five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default, at one point soared above 1,140 basis points. It last stood at nearly 1,038 bps — a 16% week-over-week jump and 164% higher than a year ago.

Credit concerns are spreading into AI equities too. CoreWeave's stock dropped during trading Thursday before recovering and closing 5.94% higher. The moved followed Wednesday's 15.5% nosedive.

Other neocloud stocks were under similar pressure. Nebius also sank before climbing higher to close 1.29% and and Iren ultimately ended the day down less than 1%.

Anxiety about AI overspending is also spreading. For instance, shares of Micron Technology and Advanced Micro Devices sold off following a big sell-off in South Korean chip stocks. Micron closed up 1.87%, while AMD fell 1.5%.

While AI debt worries have been rising steadily, investors have suddenly grown skittish about the underlying finances of companies borrowing heavily to buy chips, build data centers, and rent out computing power.

Coreweave's credit default swaps are one place to see that stress.

Credit default swaps are contracts where buyers pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. The spread widening in CoreWeave's CDS means investors view the debt as riskier.

The market is "basically saying 'no mas' to AI debt without operating cash flow support," the Evercore ISI trading desk said.

A spread level of 1,000 basis points or above is generally a sign of credit distress, said Gavan Nolan, executive director of fixed income pricing at S&P Global Market Intelligence.

No one is questioning whether demand for CoreWeave's services is real, since the company has contracts with all the big hyperscalers, Nolan said. The question is whether the cash flows will ultimately arrive.

"It's obviously heavily negative free cash flow at the moment, and it needs that cash flow to arrive really to justify all the capex that it's spending," Nolan said.

CEO Mike Intrator dismissed the market moves on Wednesday, according to a memo obtained by CNBC.

"When you are building a new type of technology company there will be periods of time when the market misunderstands and acts irrationally," he wrote. "We are confident in our backlog which more than supports all of our debt obligations."

The backlog is over $100 billion, with around two-thirds of it with investment-grade counterparts including Microsoft, Meta, IBM and Caterpillar, Deutsche Bank said in a note Thursday. The firm reiterated its buy-rating on the stock Wednesday, calling the dislocation a buying opportunity.

"The problem is this kind of concern can prove to be a self-fulfilling prophecy, and risks triggering broader issues in other AI-related names," said Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research.

Domino effects may already be happening. Oracle's five-year CDS spread also spiked this week. It was last at 282 basis points, a roughly 10% increase in one week.

"A downgrade from its BBB rating would risk pressuring IG-only investors into forced sales," Reid said.

#3164. How AI security failures are endangering your supply chain – and what you can do

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

New research provides a stark look at the AI supply chain and the risks it brings to businesses.

Artificial intelligence companies worldwide are bringing a new wave of vulnerabilities and security issues to the digital supply chain, researchers warn.

According to a new report published by Black Kite on Tuesday, there now exists a “widespread cybersecurity weakness” across the “rapidly expanding ecosystem of companies developing, deploying, and supporting artificial intelligence.”

AI and smart solutions don’t automatically equate with safe solutions. In a study titled “2026 AI Supply Chain Report: The Risks Behind Your AI Relationships,” Black Kite says that many AI suppliers — the companies that develop and deploy AI services to today’s businesses — are struggling with “basic security practices.”

Also: Rogue AI incidents hit ‘tens of thousands’: Can businesses trust these tools?

According to the research, after examining the companies themselves, along with their software, integrations, and components, researchers found failures to patch critical vulnerabilities, protect credentials, authenticate users and email, and address network security issues.

The study analyzes 18,160 AI companies worldwide, ranging from 0-10 employees to more than 1,000 employees. A company’s AI involvement was determined through reviews of company products and services and primary business activities, covering six broad groups: everyday application development; AI development and implementation; AI applications in specialized sectors such as healthcare, IT, and finance; industrial organizations; and AI foundations working on data, compute, and modeling.

A small percentage of assessed companies are considered “unclassified,” and the study excluded businesses that use AI only incidentally.

In addition, unusual network activity was detected at 44.3% of organizations, and botnet-related activity was recorded at 41.8% of AI companies.

AI’s main business appeal is its ability to integrate with existing software and platforms to handle manual tasks, heavy lifting, and tedious, time-consuming work, freeing employees to focus on work that requires human oversight.

Enterprises are investing billions of dollars in AI. Menlo’s 2026 research estimates that enterprise AI spend reached $37 billion in 2025, with half of overall spend going to AI apps. In total, 76% of enterprise AI solutions are purchased rather than built internally.

However, the moment an AI or automated component is introduced into a corporate ecosystem or operational platform, any security problems it has impact the user — and each additional integration, such as software components or open-source tooling, may also increase the attack surface.

In an accompanying analysis of AI firms’ use of Model Context Protocol (MCP) components, which can link AI apps to business systems, 48.8% of 1,732 logged security findings were rated High or Critical, including code and command injection vulnerabilities.

Also: The new enterprise AI expert every company needs

It’s not just the AI application itself. As noted by Black Kite, AI suppliers often have access to customer data, developer environments, business infrastructure, and enterprise platforms. If the supplier’s credentials are stolen, this could expose its customers to a range of cyberattacks.

The researchers found, for example, that 19.9% of AI companies offering solutions for enterprise AI deployment had corporate login information in infostealer records, and that stolen corporate logins, overall, were linked to 6.8% of AI suppliers in the study.

Visibility matters, too, for businesses that should be monitoring their digital supply chains. However, 33.2% of AI companies assessed have a “limited” digital footprint, including their domains, subdomains, and IP addresses.

The same principles for protecting the digital supply chain apply to AI suppliers, where continual monitoring and assessment are among the best protections businesses have.

Staying informed and aware of the risks to your own business makes all the difference. As recommended by Black Kite, it takes more than a single point-in-time security assessment — frequent monitoring and engaging with suppliers to understand their technologies and dependencies can reveal potential security issues.

Also: Do you need AI agent liability insurance?

If you find weaknesses, manage them by priority, handling ransomware-related exposures and vulnerabilities actively exploited in the wild first.

Also audit an AI tool’s integrations and components, access, what data it can reach, and the potential impact of exploitation. Once you’re armed with this information, it’s possible to weigh risk and reward — and whether the AI tool is worth the potential attack exposure it brings. If you decide to keep it, limit its permissions and access to what it requires.

#3163. Sandisk Stock Has Collapsed by 65%, but It's Still Undergoing an 8-for-1 Stock Split on Nov. 19. Here's Why I'm Still Not Buying Shares Amidst This Nasdaq Market Slump.

P/E ratios can play tricks on investors when it comes to highly cyclical companies.

The Nasdaq Composite index briefly entered correction territory last week, dropping by more than 10% from its peak as the prices of artificial intelligence (AI)-related stocks collapsed. Memory and storage giant Sandisk (SNDK +6.16%) has fallen 64% from its highs set only in June, after rising more than 10 times in value.

Sandisk management has scheduled a stock split to take place on Nov. 19. The flash memory maker trades at a cheap-looking price-to-earnings ratio (P/E) right now, but it just might be the most expensive stock you could buy today. Here's why I am not buying Sandisk stock despite the price dip and the upcoming stock split.

Data storage giant Western Digital bought SanDisk in 2016, but then hived it back off (with a slightly different name) in early 2025. Since then, the rapid pace of the AI infrastructure build-out has driven a massive increase in demand and a deep worldwide shortage of memory chips. Because of that, Sandisk and its peers have been able to raise their prices sharply.

Last quarter, its revenue was $11 billion, up 378% year over year, with a gross margin of 85%. Gross margin was below 30% in the same quarter a year ago. This boom has led to massive earnings growth, with $8.78 billion in earnings last quarter alone, up from barely breaking even a year prior.

That earnings inflection is why Sandisk stock was at one point up by more than 6,000% since Western Digital spun it off. Now, though, it has fallen by more than half from that peak in conjunction with the Nasdaq correction.

Many news items have accumulated since Sandisk's stock peaked in June that have caused investors to become nervous about the AI infrastructure theme.

OpenAI delayed its IPO. Neoclouds such as CoreWeave are reportedly having trouble raising funds in the debt markets. The midterm elections could have sweeping impacts on AI regulation and the pace of data center construction.

The less capital that flows into the AI companies, the less they will have to spend on memory chips from Sandisk. This narrative has taken hold of Wall Street at the moment, and it's why Sandisk stock has been falling.

When Sandisk reported its fiscal 2027 first-quarter results on Oct. 29, management guided for sequential revenue growth of around 10% in its fiscal Q2. This suggests that memory demand continues to grow, but that would be slower than fiscal Q1's 23% sequential growth. Wall Street hates slowing revenue growth.

Sandisk and its peers are all in the midst of building new facilities to manufacture more memory and grab more of the growing market. But eventually, as that additional production comes online in the quarters and years ahead, the supply of memory chips will come back in balance with demand. It may then exceed demand, creating a glut, at which point Sandisk and its cohort will run into trouble.

Sandisk's ability to implement significant price increases across its product lines over the past year and a half has led to massive earnings growth. The sharp shift in its gross margins illustrates the wild cyclicality in the memory chip market. Historically, the memory chip sector has experienced many periods of high demand and prices that were followed by equally deep declines as the supply-and-demand dynamic reversed. I don't think this time will be different.

For investors, the stock split is a meaningless event. It only increases the total number of shares outstanding, but it changes nothing about either the stock's valuation or the underlying business. Never buy shares in a company just because of a stock split.

Right now, Sandisk trades at what looks like a dirt-cheap forward price-to-earnings ratio (P/E) of 3.5. It is possible that Sandisk will generate the expected level of earnings that ratio is based on over the next 12 months. However, over a longer time horizon, a downturn in the memory chip market could significantly affect this earnings ratio.

If Sandisk's annualized revenues fall back to $10 billion (which is what the company was reporting less than a year ago) and its operating margin collapses from 73% to 10% as it loses its shortage-driven pricing power, its earnings will fall to $1 billion. That would give it a P/E ratio of 100 relative to the current market cap of around $100 billion. That makes Sandisk stock look wildly expensive for anyone thinking about holding it beyond the next few quarters.

Brett Schafer has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Western Digital. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3162. Ring’s new drone patrols your home to keep an eye on things – even upstairs

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Smart Home > Home Security

Alongside the Ring Drone Cam, Ring introduced the Rover Cam for businesses. It investigates disturbances and streams 4K footage.

Today, Ring announced the launch of a new subbrand, Ring Robotics, with two new products: the $249 Ring Drone Cam, an indoor “mobile security camera” drone that shoots 2K video, and the $999 Ring Rover Cam, a security robot for businesses that patrols autonomously and investigates disturbances.

Also: Amazon’s new Echo Studio gives Alexa+ an AI camera to see inside your home

Both devices use Ring’s custom navigation software, “AI Pilot,” to autonomously navigate interior spaces and move between set points along a patrol, rather than being controlled directly by the user. AI Pilot leverages the devices’ cameras, sensors, and vision models, along with cloud processing, to create interior maps, then move the devices between predesignated patrol points such as a side door or a window.

I recently saw both new robots in person at a showcase in New York hosted by Ring’s founder and chief inventor, Jamie Siminoff. They certainly do what they advertise but come with some significant limitations. The drone, for example, can only stay in the air for about 8 minutes before it needs to dock and recharge, while the Ring Rover Cam is slow and deliberate, seemingly ideal for routine surveillance rather than rapid response to an event.

The Ring Drone Cam moves around predetermined patrol points in your home, sending you a live 2K video feed whether you’re just in another room or out of town. The user doesn’t actually fly the drone; instead, it moves around autonomously using Ring’s custom “AI Pilot” navigation technology, leveraging its cameras, sensors, and cloud processing to create a map of its surroundings. This allows it to fly through the air at an appropriate height, going from room to room, even up stairs.

In the Ring app, the user creates patrol points they want the drone to move between — likely a window, doorway, or high-traffic room — creating a set path that is both predictable (so you always know where the drone will go) and relevant, so it’s only monitoring the areas you want it to. But here is where its limitations come into play: It needs a finite path to navigate because its flight time is limited to just five to eight minutes in the air before it has to return to its charging dock.

That makes the dock both the start and end point for the drone’s routine trips, and it’s designed with this in mind. When it goes to land, the drone hovers over the dock and lowers itself down, dropping into place so it can start charging automatically. I asked about its potential to land elsewhere, and Ring told me it would only make an emergency landing outside of its dock in case it didn’t have enough power to get home.

Also: Amazon’s new Echo Dot can turn into a Wi-Fi extender – how it works

There’s one other very important caveat: This thing is loud. There’s no sneaking up on anyone with the Ring Drone Cam, as the entire household is likely to hear it the second it undocks. The four propellers emit a constant, high-pitched whirring while in the air that’s loud enough to announce its presence to anyone in the vicinity: intruder or resident.

During the product briefing, Siminoff insinuated that this was part of its design to dissuade home intruders snooping around your house at night (especially when paired with the drone’s front-facing spotlight), but it’s likely just the result of its engineering, especially considering this is a first-gen product. It’s so loud that it makes me think it’s better suited to navigating the home when no one’s home (although whether or not your pets go bonkers trying to catch it is a whole other issue).

The Ring Drone is designed to work out of the box with the existing Ring ecosystem, including the 2K Video Doorbell and Indoor Cam, and is also compatible with Amazon’s Map View.

The Ring Drone Cam will start shipping on Jan. 27, 2027, and will cost $249.

The other product Ring announced is the Rover Cam, a mobile robot with a 4K camera designed to monitor large spaces like warehouses, offices, or businesses. Ring calls it a “security guard that never clocks out,” but it’s more like a slow-moving droid that goes on set patrol paths. It leverages the same AI Pilot technology as the Ring Drone to map its surroundings and navigate around shelving, furniture, and obstacles in real time.

It’s much quieter than its airborne sibling, traveling at low speeds and offering mobile surveillance instead of wiring every square inch of a large space. If the technology here looks familiar, it’s because it resembles the Amazon Astra, a smaller robot designed for home monitoring but with a very similar build.

The idea here is to deploy the robot to certain areas of a workplace via the app, then get 360-degree views once it’s there. Like the drone, it can also set out on automated routes at select timing intervals — like right after a business closes or before it opens. During working hours, it chugs along with its camera, capturing everything in its view.

Also: The 4 biggest Amazon smart home announcements from today

If another Ring device is triggered, the Rover Cam autonomously goes to investigate the disturbance, providing 4K footage in real time via the app. It also features a periscope camera that can extend to see over obstacles like shelving, counters, or furniture, providing a high-level view if needed. In case you lose it in a large warehouse or office, it has a “light tower” that projects light upward onto the ceiling, notifying users of its location.

Even when docked, it uses motion-sensing technology to start recording the second it senses any kind of movement, just like Ring doorbell cameras. In case of an emergency, the Rover features a siren and red flashing lights to dissuade intruders.

The Ring Rover Cam is available for preorder now and will start shipping on Dec. 16, 2026. It will cost $999.

#3159. The United Explorer Card Is Great if You Fly Basic Economy

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Basic economy tickets come with plenty of restrictions, but holding the right credit card is my top trick for avoiding some of them.

As airfares remain high, that basic economy fare might look more tempting than ever. Saving $100 on a round-trip domestic flight could mean more money for hotels, dining or other experiences at your destination.

I’ve flown my fair share of basic economy flights, which come with restrictions like being unable to select a seat, earning no award miles and boarding last. You also can’t make changes or cancel your itinerary without paying a hefty fee, and — specific to United Airlines — you can’t bring a full-size carry-on on board.

But as a United frequent flyer, I have a secret to share with you: Holding the United℠ Explorer Card (annual fee: $0 intro for the first year, then $150) lets you avoid several of those basic economy restrictions.

Right now, new United℠ Explorer Card applicants can receive this welcome offer: Earn 70,000 bonus miles after you spend $3,000 on purchases in the first 3 months your account is open. Plus, earn 10,000 bonus miles when you add an authorized user in the first 3 months your account is open.

Most major airlines no longer let you earn points or miles on basic economy fares. United is an exception, though — assuming you hold the right credit card.

Eligible cardholders, including those who have the United℠ Explorer Card, earn at least 3 miles per $1 spent on United basic economy tickets, excluding taxes and select fees. That’s half of what a standard economy ticket earns for cardholders (6 miles per $1), but it’s better than zero. If you have elite status, you’ll earn more.

Those miles are in addition to the 3 miles per $1 you earn on all United purchases when using your United℠ Explorer Card.

With the card, your miles can become more valuable. That’s because cardholders get at least 10% off award tickets that are operated by United or United Express. If you have United status, that discount gets bumped up to at least 15% off.

With the credit card, you have access to priority boarding in Group 2. That means you’ll be able to board earlier compared to the rest of the basic economy passengers in Group 6, making it more likely you’ll find space for your carry-on bag.

Now, you might be thinking: Wait a minute… Basic economy doesn’t allow you to bring a carry-on bag. Let me explain how that works.

United enforces its full-size carry-on restriction based on your boarding group. Group 6 passengers can't bring a full-size carry-on.

Because the credit card gets you Group 2 boarding, you essentially skip that restriction.

Premier members — who board in Group 2 or earlier — are also exempt from the full-size carry-on restriction when flying basic economy.

If you don’t want to deal with the hassle of a carry-on bag, you can opt to check it instead.

As long as you pay for your ticket using your United℠ Explorer Card, you’ll be able to check your first bag — and your companion’s first bag — for free when traveling on United. (If you’re booking an award ticket, make sure to use your card to pay for the taxes and fees.)

Keep in mind that if the first flight on your itinerary is operated by another airline, that airline’s baggage rules would apply and you would have to pay for your bag.

Of course, holding the right United credit card only gets you so far.

With basic economy, you’ll still need to pay a fee to change or cancel your ticket, and you won’t be able to select a seat for free.

Ultimately, whether basic economy is a good deal depends on a few factors.

If your plans might change, pay up for standard economy. Otherwise, the penalty to change or cancel will end up costing you more than you saved.

Another thing to consider is whether you’re willing to sit in a middle seat. You might be fine with it for a few hours, but what about a long-haul flight to Asia? Everyone’s threshold is different, so you’ll need to decide whether the middle-seat gamble is worth it.

If your plans are firm and you don’t mind a potential middle seat, booking a basic economy ticket can be a solid way to save money. Just book with a United card like, the United℠ Explorer Card, to avoid the biggest downsides.

#3158. Adobe Is Giving Its AI Agents Even More to Do

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Across Firefly and Creative Cloud, apps are getting even deeper into creative work.

Would you use an always-on AI agent such as Claude Duet or OpenAI’s Dot to edit your photos? At Adobe Max on Tuesday, the company announced it’s expanding its local MCP integrations with more third-party companies to enable more autonomous creative work.

MCP, or Model Context Protocol, is an open-source protocol for connecting AI systems to external apps and databases. AI agents and MCPs act as a two-way street. Agents, or fancy bots, can complete tasks independently.

For example, if you wanted your OpenAI agents (or Dots, whatever you call them) to edit a project in Photoshop, the MCP pathway would allow ChatGPT to run agents to edit the design in the Photoshop beta desktop app — no need to search through the toolbar if you’re not familiar with it.

The new MCP integrations are in preview now. The idea is to give people more choice by letting them use their preferred AI app to help them work in select Creative Cloud apps.

Over the past year, Adobe has been working with AI companies to bring its creative knowledge and tech to ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and Copilot as Adobe plugins. It’s also brought external models from AI companies into Firefly, Adobe’s AI hub. Now, Adobe is deepening its relationships with third-party AI vendors to adapt to the agentic AI era.

Bringing your own agents offers a degree of user control, according to Shilpa Anand, vice president of global strategic partnerships. “These new models allow me to actually do things faster, experience the full power of the application and really make it more accessible to a broader set of users who are not experts in our workflow,” Anand told me.

Agentic AI has been a huge focus for tech companies this year. It’s the next step for companies that want to move beyond mere chat and information-gathering AI capabilities.

Adobe has been driving toward this vision for a while. It released AI assistants in Photoshop and Adobe Express that set the foundation for June’s official launch of Adobe creative agents.

But AI agents from frontier labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic caused trouble this summer when they escaped testing environments and hacked real websites. Anand told me that safety and security are “table stakes” for Adobe and that there haven’t been any issues aligning with other AI companies on safety precautions.

When considering partnerships, the company also considers which AI models will be most useful for Adobe’s creative customers, which is why Firefly includes models from big players including Google, as well as more specialized AI companies, such as ElevenLabs for AI audio tech. (Adobe acquired Topaz Labs, which makes AI photo editing software, earlier this year.)

Those creative AI agents are getting new capabilities across Creative Cloud apps, too. In Firefly, the company’s AI hub, its AI assistant should now be better at multistep or more complex tasks, and it has a new “plan mode” in preview that lets you review the agent’s plan before it starts running.

New “always-on” Firefly AI agents do exactly what the name implies: They’re always working and can handle predefined tasks, such as assessing image quality in new asset uploads. They’re in preview now, too. And across Illustrator, InDesign, Premiere, After Effects and Frame.io, those AI assistants are getting upgraded capabilities as well.

Adobe is also expanding its partnerships. Microsoft Photos will have a built-in Adobe Express integration, and Microsoft’s Arm-based Windows PCs are getting “optimized” Adobe apps. Disney is also deepening its relationship with Adobe, bringing Adobe’s Firefly AI tech to its creative teams.

Partnerships with AI companies and the integration of agentic capabilities will continue to be a focus for Adobe next year, Anand told me. “Agentic software is really going to enable us to reach more users and empower everyone to create, which is our mission.”

#3157. Energy Transfer’s EPS Missed by 20%. Enbridge’s Profit Nearly Doubled. Here’s Why I’m Not Worried About Either Dividend Even Though Both Still Fell Short.

Energy Transfer missed estimates, and Enbridge’s profit nearly doubled, but neither headline tells you whether the dividend is safe. Here’s what the cash flow shows for each pipeline giant.

Energy Transfer's (ET +0.90%) third-quarter earnings per unit came in about 20% below analysts' expectations. A few days later, Enbridge (ENB +1.09%) reported a profit that nearly doubled from a year ago. If you judged these two pipeline giants by those headlines alone, you'd probably think one had a strong quarter, while the other did not. That would be a mistake because neither number tells you the real story, especially when it comes to the safety of their high-yielding dividends.

Here are the numbers that show why I'm not worried about these high-yielding dividend stocks.

One common mistake many investors make when analyzing pipeline stocks is putting too much emphasis on their reported earnings per share. That number isn't a true representation of the underlying earnings these companies generate. That's because pipelines and other energy infrastructure assets are capital intensive. As a result, pipeline companies often record significant depreciation charges that lower reported earnings. Additionally, gains and losses on asset sales or commodity hedges can meaningfully distort reported earnings.

A better measure of pipeline profitability is distributable cash flow (DCF). This is the cash generated by these businesses that they could distribute to investors via dividends. Here's the math for these pipeline giants

Enbridge reported GAAP earnings of 1.3 billion Canadian dollars ($920 million), or CA$0.59 per share ($0.42), in the third quarter, nearly double the year-ago period. Despite nearly doubling, Enbridge's reported earnings were still below the CA$0.97 per share ($0.69) it pays in dividends each quarter.

Enbridge's DCF, on the other hand, is more than twice the size of its reported earnings at CA$2.7 billion ($1.9 billion), a nearly 4% year-over-year increase. That puts its dividend payout ratio at 79%. It's an even more comfortable 66% based on the midpoint of the company's 2026 guidance range, well within its 60%-70% target range. That puts its nearly 6%-yielding dividend on a much firmer foundation.

That conservative payout ratio enables Enbridge to retain excess cash to reinvest in the growth of its operations. The company generates CA$5 billion to CA$6 billion ($3.6 billion to $4.3 billion) in post-dividend free cash flow each year. Enbridge can fund over CA$10 billion ($7.1 billion) in new investments each year by adding its additional balance sheet capacity while remaining within its targeted leverage ratio range. This robust investment capacity supports its expectation of growing its DCF per share by around a 5% annual rate, which should support similar dividend growth. Enbridge has increased its payout for 31 straight years (in Canadian dollars).

We see a similar story at Energy Transfer. The master limited partnership (MLP) reported GAAP earnings of $1.1 billion, or $0.33 per unit, missing the analysts' consensus estimate of $0.41 per unit by nearly 20%. Like Enbridge, Energy Transfer's reported earnings were below its current distribution level ($0.3425 per unit).

However, its DCF was much higher at $2.5 billion, a 33% increase from the prior year. That was more than double what it distributed to unit holders ($1.2 billion), putting its more than 7%-yielding payout on a much firmer foundation.

Energy Transfer's robust cash flow supports its continued expansion. The MLP plans to invest about $5.5 billion into expansion projects this year and another $7 billion in 2027. These investments should support 3% to 5% annual distribution growth. Energy Transfer has raised its payout for 20 straight quarters.

Reported earnings can make the high-yielding dividends of pipeline stocks such as Enbridge and Energy Transfer seem at risk of a reduction. However, those numbers don't show the true underlying cash-generating capabilities of these companies. When you look at their cash flows, their payouts are on much more sustainable foundations. It's why I'm not worried about either of their high-yielding dividends.

Matt DiLallo has positions in Enbridge and Energy Transfer. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Enbridge. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3156. The New Adobe Firefly AI Is All About Video Editing

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Adobe's AI platform is doing all the things now, not just beginners' basics.

Adobe has been on a journey for years to turn itself into an AI behemoth, and its latest round of Firefly AI updates brings its dreams one step closer to fruition. At Adobe Max on Tuesday, the company announced a slew of Firefly AI updates focused on building out its video-editing capabilities.

Firefly is getting an upgraded video editor. Like other creative AI companies, Adobe has been building up its AI video capabilities over the past year, with video models made both in-house and by third-party AI companies. Now, the new interface incorporates the Firefly AI assistant, which is in beta, in a bigger way. There’s a dedicated ideation panel and more ways to storyboard ideas.

It’s a more feature-packed alternative to beginner or everyday video editors like CapCut, without overloading creators with all the features of professional-grade Adobe Premiere. Part of that push is integrating agentic AI, or agents that can handle editing tasks for you autonomously. And there are a lot of AI agents coming to Adobe’s programs.

That beginner-to-intermediate design is, well, by design — Adobe is aiming to lure in folks without decades of video-editing know-how with its generative AI features. It fits with Adobe’s larger goals; it wants to build Firefly for video and image editors the same way it built Photoshop — to be the “career partner” for creators.

Another new project is Firefly Design, an AI-heavy version of a graphic design program that incorporates print, motion graphics and 3D. The intelligence assistant is a big part of the interface, where you can use text prompts to come up with variations of different scenes.

There are a few new Firefly AI image tools, and their names describe their uses well: relight, retouch and edit object. These are more precise controls, keeping with Adobe’s goal of giving AI enthusiasts more ability to edit their video content. There are also new quick actions, or shortcuts, including a video thumbnail generator that’s based on your video.

And if you remember Project Graph from last year’s Adobe Max, it’s back in a public preview as Firefly Graph. This is a node-based editing system, where you can create individual “nodes” and connect them to create an entire system, or ecosystem, of content. It won’t be for everyone, but it’s aimed at creative teams who want to visualize large workflows.

#3155. Breakfast News: Nvidia’s Governance Moment

Here’s what worries us most about the unexpected Huang news.

When unexpected news breaks about a leader we’re invested in, we at The Motley Fool try to slow down and separate the person from the business. That’s how we’re approaching this weekend’s news from Nvidia (NVDA -4.27%).

As we know, CEO Jensen Huang has admitted to an affair with Alice Huang, a former executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer. And U.S. officials have expressed concerns that she was mindfully gathering sensitive information on Nvidia’s technology and strategy for Chinese intelligence.

Nvidia’s board is now investigating. So far, the board says it has no reason to think any national security information or company secrets were compromised.

Meanwhile, Nvidia’s business looks the same as it did on Friday. Many Wall Street firms have not altered their price targets, and Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives & Co still thinks Nvidia leads China by two to three years in artificial intelligence chips.

However, we do expect substantial fallout in Washington.

Huang has spent years lobbying to sell advanced chips in China. Lawmakers from both parties now want a closer look at those sales, and the incoming Congress could move to tighten export rules again.

We’ve always viewed Nvidia’s chance for China sales as upside to our core thesis. Losing that opportunity would not alter our long-term view.

Huang has led Nvidia since 1993. Around 60 executives report to him directly. That structure leaves no obvious successor were he to step aside, even for a short leave. The board has publicly backed Huang, and we think his departure is unlikely, but shareholders will obviously want more clarity on succession now.

All told, we’re staying the course with Nvidia, a stock that our Rule Breakers team recommended at 16 cents per share in 2005 and never sold. Our Hidden Gems team bought NVDA at $5.29 in 2018 and never sold.

But the views across our teams are subject to change (not surprisingly) if the investigation shows Huang shared secrets or let Nvidia’s chips reach restricted buyers. For Fools with a position size in Nvidia greater than 15% of their net worth, we don’t see reasons to react urgently. But you might consider making some incremental sales.

Finally, if Nvidia’s board handles this well, the company should come out of it with stronger governance paired with a public succession plan. Some silver lining to a grey grey cloud.

Team Hidden Gems has a five-year price prediction of $321.42, implying an annualized return of about 12.5%.

Team Rule Breakers has a five-year price prediction of $562.47, implying an annualized return of around 26%.

Our five-year price prediction for Twilio (TWLO +2.29%) is $400, implying 14% annualized gains from today’s price. Twilio’s software lets businesses text, call, and email customers. The stock is close to 600% since its entry onto our Rule Breakers scorecard in 2017, ahead of the S&P 500 by over 300 percentage points. At that time, it was a founder-led business. CEO and co-founder Jeff Lawson did a good job steering the company through the IPO process. But the start-up mentality lost its luster. Investors started to question the

Then came Khozema Shipchandler. Though not a founder, he has held Twilio’s trifecta of top roles: CFO, then COO, and CEO since January 2024. Shares have nearly tripled since he took over.

Twilio’s investments in AI are paying off. Thanks to strong leadership and a clear vision, we think there’s still plenty of opportunity for Twilio to grow.

Which other founder-led stocks do you think carry the same “one person” risk as Nvidia, and how would losing that founder change your thesis?

#3154. We Write About Saving Money; Here’s How We Trick Our Brains Into Doing It

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Even personal finance writers and editors need psychological hacks to save money. Here are the tips we use, and why they work.

While staring at the Container Store cart on my phone at 11 p.m., I did something for the first time that I’d read about a hundred times. Instead of spending $200 on more organizational boxes that I categorically did not need, I transferred that amount to my savings account.

Normally, my willpower is no match for the very real psychological phenomenon called present bias: Our brains light up more when we’re rewarding our current selves than our future selves.

Research has also shown that you imagine your future self almost like a stranger — it’s tougher to care about that stranger than about who you are right now. But that night, I saw near-future-me using that $200 on next summer’s vacation. I wanted the vacation more than the boxes. So I abandoned my cart.

I had managed to use a psychological savings trick: Find a goal that helps you envision your future self. With this epiphany in mind, I asked some of my fellow Nerds to share the savings habits they’ve put to work in their own lives. Perhaps reading these can give late-night you some saving inspiration.

Kimberly Palmer, senior writer and spokesperson: I let nonessential items sit in my online shopping cart for at least 24 hours before buying them. It definitely makes me realize I don't need a lot of them! I also informed my children of this rule, and it has helped cut down on their requests — or at least they know they have to be patient.

Karen Gaudette Brewer, head of content: Fellow let-it-sit-in-the-cart person here. I wait for notifications that something's at least 30% off before I make any moves. Ideally, 40% to 50% off.

Julie Myhre-Nunes, managing editor: When I browse online, I add an item to my shopping cart and wait 24 to 48 hours. After that, I either decide it’s a waste of money/an impulse purchase and delete it from my cart (or forget about the cart altogether), or I decide I want it and make the purchase — it’s the former 99% of the time.

Why it works: “Waiting 1 to 2 days allows me to take the emotion out of purchasing. I no longer succumb to marketing or sales, and instead make spending decisions based on what my family and I actually need or can use.” — Julie again, eloquently making the point for me.

Caitlin Constantine, managing editor: My one real shopping weakness is books, and I’ve found I can get a very close approximation of that feeling without spending an outrageous amount of money by going to the library, wandering around the stacks to see what books might look interesting, and checking out a bunch of them. Even if I don’t end up reading them, I’m still helping the library with circulation numbers, so I see it as a win-win.

Spencer Tierney, senior writer: Plus one to Caitlin Constantine’s “my one real shopping weakness is books.” If I want an older or smaller book, I'll try to find it at one of four libraries I belong to on Libby, the library e-book reader app. (The Libby waitlist for new books can take months, so I have to account for that.)

Why it works: There’s no secret here: It’s nice to get free stuff. You might be surprised what the library has to offer, like movies, magazine subscriptions, games, classes and theater tickets.

Pamela de la Fuente, managing editor: We always tell people to automate savings, and I do in my high-yield savings account, but Dasha Kennedy, the woman behind “The Broke Black Girl”, told Elizabeth Ayoola on the Smart Money podcast that you should invest, even a little bit every week or so, for future you. So I just set up a small recurring deposit to my robo-advisor.

Why it works: You only have to make this decision once for your future self, not over and over again. And if you do it early enough, you could avoid being part of the 41% of Americans who wish they had started investing earlier, according to a 2026 NerdWallet survey.

Athena Cocoves, managing editor: To cut down on food delivery, I started cooking when I feel like it instead of when I’m supposed to. I exercise free will and make soup at 10 p.m. It sounds more obvious than it feels, and it's made a big difference in my budget. And it'd make an even bigger difference if I wasn't also rewarding myself with fancy spices and vinegars, but a girl's gotta splurge somehow.

Ryan Brady, lead writer: My wife takes advantage of a T-Mobile freebie that gives us a discount for DoorDash deliveries. She uses it to buy groceries from Aldi instead of takeout. Aldi is far from us. Publix is right across the street, but more expensive. So this saves us money on the actual items and also helps us not impulse buy (as we often do when we're in-store shopping).

Why it works: You don’t need to call on willpower if you can avoid the situation that tempts you to spend money.

» Read more: Strategies to save on groceries that actually work

Kenley Young, managing editor: I use credit cards for most purchases to get those sweet, sweet rewards, but there's a lot in this article that I've also taken to heart.

I've started using library resources more, and it's saving me money on things like monthly subscriptions.

I've negotiated down several monthly bills, too: refinanced my car loan, got cheaper homeowners insurance, opted for a little less auto coverage in exchange for a lower premium, and haggled online with everyone from Xfinity and SiriusXM to my local newspaper until they all gave me a discount.

Plus, I've opted for some home improvement projects with the expectation that my utility bills would be lower. A plumber finally fixed our toilet, which has led to lower water bills, and I even installed solar panels.

Lastly, my analog skill (playing live music) has become a steady side hustle. I play eight to 10 gigs a month, where (on top of my baseline rate) I also get tips, free food and beer.

Why it works: Because you’re Kenley and you put in a lot of effort.

But you don’t need to be Kenley to find a savings habit that works for your personality, your financial situation and even your musical talents. You just need to experiment. Pick the strategy that speaks to you most, and test it out. Which one are you going to try first?
