#3153. Brian Sullivan: Why the 'real' price of oil is likely $30-$40 higher than where it is now

Oil, in reality, is $125 per barrel or higher.

That's the real cost of a barrel of oil because buying the physical commodity is a very different story from a number on traders' screens.

Oil futures are just that: paper contracts indicating a price today, tomorrow, next month or further out.  If you want to buy the physical commodity, it may be a totally different story — and price.

As I've reported, prices for oil delivery have skyrocketed. Last week, a VLCC (aka a "supertanker" in non-shipper parlance) was chartered from the U.S. Gulf Coast to China for $76 million. Another very large crude carrier went for $77 million from the U.S. to South Korea. That's 10 times more than the price of the same trip less than one year ago.

Assuming these ships hold about 2 million barrels of oil, that charter price means each barrel on the ship costs about $38 over the price of the oil itself.

I'm certainly not saying to avoid looking at oil futures prices, but you need to know that the price on the CNBC screen or CNBC.com isn't always the full price being paid. These days, any company that needs oil shipped by sea, rail or truck will pay a hefty premium over the price of the oil itself. So, while the volume of oil moving through Hormuz is closing in on pre-war levels, costs are not. And there's no sign that they're going anywhere but up.

You know what else is up? The stocks of the shipowners. Look at their moves this month and this year.

On a more serious note, I want to express my condolences for the awful attacks on the airport in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia. The cowardly strike on civilians—some of whom were religious pilgrims—took the lives of at least 14 people and injured more than 300.

The airport was attacked at least three times as travelers and energy industry professionals from around the world were flying into Riyadh for the World Petroleum Council conference. I was scheduled to fly in on Saturday, but the trip was canceled because of the strikes. The WPC went on as planned — here's one panel that took place Tuesday — and I look forward to seeing all my global energy friends again soon.

Thanks for reading—and watching. Be safe in these crazy and often scary times.

The supercap oil and gas stock to watch right now is Shell (SHEL). Shell is not a shell of its former self. It's back to being what energy investors wanted: a major player in global energy, focused on what it does best. Shareholders are being rewarded.

Shell hit a new high this week. While analysts remain bullish, the stock is getting close to its target price.

Shell may be underappreciated for its huge liquefied natural gas business. RBC analyst Biraj Borkhataria writes: "Without a sustained resumption in Qatari volumes and with European storage levels below seasonal lows heading into winter, European gas prices continued on an upward trajectory in 3Q26. With HH broadly unchanged throughout the summer, export margins have increased to the widest levels seen since 2022, on our estimates nearly double the level in 2Q26 (~$13/mmbtu vs. $7/mmbtu)."

Borkhataria expects Shell and other companies with huge U.S. LNG positions — TotalEnergies (TTE) and BP (BP) — to benefit from wider margins on exports to Europe.

Speaking of RBC and energy, the firm lists ConocoPhillips as one of its top 30 global stock ideas. The company is covered by analyst Scott Hanold, who believes it has a "returns-focused value proposition, a strong balance sheet, and peer-leading distributions." He loves its "global and diverse" asset base and management's clear "investment propositions." Hanold has a $152 target on COP. That's more than 20% upside.

In oil and gas, refiners and big energy stocks are generating cash for investors, and Morgan Stanley is optimistic more is coming.

The firm says the diesel shortage is now spilling over into gasoline, potentially marking another leg of the "golden age" for refiners. Morgan Stanley has been revising its earnings estimates higher for companies including ExxonMobil (XOM), Valero (VLO), Phillips 66 (PSX) and PBF (PBF).

Fun Fact →  While nearly every other company calls Texas home, refiner PBF is headquartered in a suburban office park just off a highway in New Jersey.

Two big calls to quickly highlight in power production.

First, Truist says you have to buy First Solar (FSLR). The analyst upgrades it to Buy with a $270 target. Tariffs should help the Phoenix-based company, giving it pricing power for at least four more years. The Section 232 tariffs—which are too complicated to detail here—should help First Solar raise solar module prices and boost earnings. We agree, having written about the company and stock back in July.

Another call on the Power Insider radar: TD Cowen is starting coverage of TeraWulf (WULF) with a Buy rating and a $21 target. That indicates about 50% upside for the stock, but it's actually one of the Street's lower price targets. TeraWulf and other AI power companies have seen their stocks hit hard following months of big gains, but Cowen analyst Michael Elias says the valuation is now attractive and a likely lease in the second half of 2027 at a Kentucky location could be a catalyst.

In the wake of the terror attacks on Saudi Arabia, we spoke with RBC Capital Markets' Helima Croft about the attacks, energy and more.

The entire energy world is watching Europe's natural gas storage levels. The question is whether the continent will have enough gas to make it safely through the winter. Power Insider wrote about the topic in the last issue.

The fact that JPMorgan highlights expected weather patterns should tell you how much temperature matters when it comes to gas storage. The early forecast calls for October and November to be average or slightly warmer than the 10-year average. The red line represents the 10-year average.

Europe has been largely "saved" by mild temperatures over the past few years. Natural gas storage levels are lower this year than in years past, so the thermometer will matter a lot when it comes to how well countries can, ahem, weather the winter. Random, but interesting.

#3152. Polestar has a new look with its Formula 2030 concept

The shape looks like a Saab Aero-X, but the nose previews the next Polestar 2.

Perhaps only a small percentage of Ars readers will care about the Polestar 2030 concept, debuted today by the electric automaker. Spun out of Volvo—once upon a time, Polestar was a racing team—the brand has leveraged the resources of parent-automaker Geely to create electric vehicles that marry a modern minimalist style with rewarding driving dynamics. But you now can’t buy any Polestars in the US, since Geely is Chinese, and banning Chinese-owned car brands enjoys remarkable bipartisan support in Congress.

The Formula 2030 is about the same length as a Polestar 2 but with a very low roofline. If anything, the two-box shape reminds me of the Saab Aero-X, one of the final concepts from the then-terminal Swedish automaker. (In many ways, Polestar has adopted Saab’s mantle.)

Polestar says the concept fits into its own segment somewhere between the next Polestar 2 and the Polestar 7—it numbers its cars chronologically (mostly), not based on size—although it’s not saying if the hatchback form factor will go into series production. Successfully packaging the rear seat passengers and a lithium-ion battery pack is something a concept needn’t worry about, after all. Rather, it’s a styling exercise, or more than one.

The nose is a good preview of the next Polestar 2, and the rear light signature will show up on production Polestars. “We wanted to evolve, while respecting, the core design values we demonstrated with previous models that define the Polestar brand in such a recognizable way,” said Nahum Escobedo, head of exterior design at Polestar.

“And with Formula 2030, with its clear Polestar design attributes, like the Dual Blade headlights, wraparound visor-like windshield, and powerful haunches, we have done just that. But we’ve pushed perceptions, preconceptions, and the expected as well, with our considered choices in form language, amplification of the performance topic, and new detailing, like the Single Blade rear lights,” Escobedo said.

Polestar says the four-seat interior pushes its usual minimalism even further. But the end result has avoided the bland fate suffered by the recent Range Rover GT concept, for example. Even though Polestar is committed to developing autonomous driving technology, for manual modes it has added physical controls—rocker switches and rotary controllers, with a user-configurable UI. That’s a new thing for Polestar.

You can’t really tell from the photos, but the Formula 2030 concept explores some sustainability ideas as well. Like BMW’s I Vision Circular, the Formula 2030 was designed using monomaterials and with recycling in mind where possible. The interiors of the doors are fully recyclable, there’s low-emission steel in the chassis, and biocomposites in place of traditional plastics.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#3151. Prediction: Here's How Much SpaceX Will Be Worth by the End of 2027

It's been a wild ride for the stock since its historic blockbuster IPO earlier this year.

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +5.81%), or SpaceX for short, pulled off a historic $1.77 trillion initial public offering just months ago. There have been some ups and downs early on, but today shares trade at $134, almost a bullseye on its $135 IPO price.

The company recently posted impressive growth with its Q3 2026 earnings. As a result, I predict that SpaceX stock could run 65% from here, closing 2027 at around $250 per share.

A word of caution: the artificial intelligence landscape is changing quickly, and some storm clouds could be on the horizon.

For all the attention SpaceX receives for launching rockets into space, the company operates three distinct business segments. Beyond the Space segment, investors will also find the Connectivity business, which holds Starlink, as well as the Super Intelligence (AI) segment, which houses xAI and Cursor.

SpaceX is selling compute to other AI hyperscalers, and business is booming. Management guided for Q1 2027 revenue of $33 billion to $37 billion, implying the size of the business as a whole could more than double over the next six months.

SpaceX's fundamentals are changing quickly, making it that much more challenging for investors to gauge how attractive the stock is today. Suppose SpaceX hits the high end of its Q1 2027 guidance. Annualize that, and you get to roughly $150 billion in 2027 revenue.

At 20 times this hypothetical 2027 revenue, shares would trade at that $250 target. SpaceX's market cap would come in around $3.4 trillion. In other words, it would be one of the world's most valuable companies. It's worth noting that no other company, save Nvidia, is growing so quickly at such a large size.

There's also suddenly some political risk here, too. CEO Elon Musk has been a close ally of President Donald Trump for most of the past few years. Naturally, Musk has a target on his back as the world's wealthiest individual. Democrats reclaimed control of the U.S. House and Senate following the recent midterm elections.

Voters came out in force against various data center proposals, and some politicians have already made it clear they intend to scrutinize AI companies more closely. This could become a big challenge for SpaceX, which is now counting on its AI computing business to achieve the blistering growth it's guiding for.

Despite all this, Wall Street analysts remain bullish on SpaceX. There are 47 analyst ratings on CNN Business, with a median 12-month price target of $245 -- right around my prediction. But don't forget that things can change quickly.

Owning SpaceX is essentially a vote of confidence in Musk's ability to lead the company through this increasingly complex AI revolution while simultaneously delivering on lofty growth goals. There might not be a riskier stock at its market cap, but the upside is there if SpaceX delivers.

#3150. Trade Desk: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Co-founder Dave Pickles is returning to daily platform work, a move management ties to faster product releases.

A 15% headcount reduction and a shift in decision-making at major brands show a business adjusting its cost structure while trying to capture larger budgets. Long-term shareholders must determine whether these internal shifts can rebuild operating leverage as ad-spending choices multiply. Three things management said stand out.

Co-founder Dave Pickles stepped down as CTO in September 2023 but remained an advisor. Management is bringing his technical guidance back into daily platform development to accelerate product releases.

A 15% reduction in global headcount caused $44 million in restructuring charges during the third quarter. Management plans to direct those resulting cost savings into core engineering, artificial intelligence, and customer relationships.

Ad-buying choices now reach C-suite executives, prompting The Trade Desk to secure long-term joint business plans (JBPs)--agreements that frame shared objectives -- rather than relying on one-off campaign orders. Clients operating under these agreements spend more money and expand their platform commitments faster.

Operating expenses ran high due to platform infrastructure investments and artificial intelligence tools, but management expects cost pressures to drop. Trade Desk is leaning on its independent buying platform and long-term customer relationships while watching software efficiency and expense discipline.

Capital allocation priorities focus on disciplined product development and offsetting share dilution through a refreshed $1 billion repurchase authorization.

#3149. The Austin Powers steamroller: How to trade CoreWeave's slow moving crisis

In Austin Powers: International Man of Mystery, a Virtuocon guard famously stands in front of a commandeered steamroller, screaming "No!!" three times before being run over. The joke, of course, is that the hazard moved so slowly he had ample time to step out of the way.

For months, equity traders holding CoreWeave (CRWV) have played that guard. Despite credit markets flashing warnings, equity holders essentially just stood there. Now, after a shocking 27.5% drop from last Friday's close to this Thursday morning's 52-week lows, followed by a whiplash 17% rebound, equity investors, if not squashed like Dr. Evil's henchman, are dazed and confused. Shares are down another 5% today.

With earnings just a few weeks away, massive leverage, a heavily split sell-side, and 15.4% short interest, the stock remains primed for explosive volatility.

The hazard approaching CRWV equity wasn't hidden. The AI cloud infrastructure platform essentially didn't exist five years ago and has accumulated $51.6 billion in debt (~$46 billion net of cash) against a backdrop of zero historical profitability. That was well known, but the CEO's comments that the company aspired to be investment grade so it could borrow "unlimited sums", even as investors already anticipated nearly another $60 billion in debt in the next 18months, spooked folks. (Trick or Treat!) Especially since the debt issued only in June had already fallen 20% since.

From an options trader's point of view, it was hard to reconcile deteriorating credit and rising leverage with falling options premia; as recently as last week, they were in the 6th percentile, 1.5 standard deviations below the one-year mean. At 72%, options weren't cheap in absolute terms, but given everything going on, they should have been higher. The stock also looked somewhat complacent, with the equity chopping around historical support levels dating back to May 2025. The credit side was on tilt:

Because CRWV is levered by more than 2:1, a 10% move in enterprise value translates to a nearly 22% swing in equity value.

Despite the dire credit picture, equity sentiment remains fiercely divided, driving this week's 17% bounce from the lows. For example, HSBC maintains a Sell rating with a $39 price target, essentially calling for the stock to be cut in half. Conversely, banks like BNP and Cantor Fitzgerald (my former employer) suggest these fears are overblown and the stock should more than double from here.

It's not just bankers defending the company either. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang downplayed systemic concerns, and ARK Investment stepped in to buy 192,000 shares. BofA argues that CRWV's ability to bring active power online at scale, combined with higher pricing from Nvidia's upcoming Vera Rubin deployments, will drive faster-than-expected revenue growth. They described execution risk as a bigger concern than financing; I'm not sure those who bought the 2032 8 1/2s at par would agree.

With CRWV's credit heavily distressed but its equity heavily defended by high-profile dip-buyers, going outright long or short into earnings carries excessive directional risk. A November/January Call Diagonal, currently trading for just under $6 per spread. Almost 9% of the current stock price. Normally, that's a lot, but given this stock has fallen 27.5% and then gained 17% in the past 5 sessions, it isn't.

#3148. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, November 6: A Little Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates dropped today, but inflation data could shake things up next week.

With the midterm elections behind us, mortgage rates are continuing their gradual slide this month. One factor is a sell-off in tech stocks, which can send investors toward the relative safety of bonds and help push mortgage rates lower.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 6.96% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is five basis points lower than yesterday and 11 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Meanwhile, a stronger-than-expected jobs report could slow that slide by reinforcing expectations for another Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

And a reminder: While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

The recent slide in mortgage rates might reverse course if next week’s inflation data comes in hot.

This week, the Nerds are watching the October Consumer Price Index, which comes out on Tuesday, Nov. 10. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at the other data set, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Right now, the labor market is looking pretty darn healthy: The October jobs report, released today from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, showed U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, far more than economists expected, and unemployment edged down to 4.1%.

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, but rate hikes are only on the table when the labor market is strong enough to handle it. Right now, it’s looking fairly likely that the Fed will approve a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Still, a jump in mortgage rates isn’t a sure thing. October’s inflation data will capture the effect of the Fed’s last rate hike, which came in the middle of September. If that worked as expected and inflation shows signs of slowing, mortgage rates will be less affected.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.46% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3147. CME vs. S&P Global: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Two financial giants, one a derivatives powerhouse and the other a ratings and data leader, with very different risk profiles for 2027.

Choosing between CME Group (CME -0.45%) and S&P Global (SPGI +0.70%) involves weighing the world's leading derivatives marketplace against the preeminent provider of credit ratings and financial data. Which is the better buy today?

Both companies are infrastructure giants in the financial world. CME processes trillions in trades, while S&P Global provides the ratings and analytics that keep capital flowing. Investors often look to these financial stocks for stability, but their business models offer different exposures to market volatility and economic growth.

CME Group operates the world's leading derivatives marketplace, enabling clients to trade futures and options across asset classes like energy and agriculture. The company serves various participants among financial stocks, including central banks. It maintains a technology partnership with Google Cloud and a joint venture with Flutter Entertainment (FLUT +0.35%). In 2025, one company accounted for 12% of CME's clearing and transaction fee revenue. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $6.5 billion, representing growth of roughly 6.4% over the previous year. Net income was close to $4 billion. The net margin was approximately 62%, which shows the percentage of revenue remaining after all operating and non-operating costs are paid.

As of CME's December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is roughly 0.1. This ratio measures total debt relative to shareholders' equity, indicating a very low reliance on borrowed funds. The current ratio, which compares short-term assets to short-term liabilities, is approximately 1. Free cash flow reached nearly $4.2 billion, representing the cash remaining after the company pays for its capital expenditures.

S&P Global provides essential credit ratings, benchmarks, and analytics that facilitate global capital and commodity markets. Its business is highly diversified, with segments ranging from market intelligence to mobility and energy data. The company serves a global customer base including asset managers and automotive manufacturers through both third-party and proprietary distribution channels. This broad reach ensures its ratings and indexes remain standard tools for investors worldwide.

In fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $15.3 billion, representing growth of close to 7.9% over the prior year. Net income was approximately $4.5 billion. The net margin was roughly 29.2%, indicating that the company retains nearly $0.30 of profit for every dollar of revenue generated.

On S&P Global's December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was approximately 0.5. The current ratio is roughly 0.8. Free cash flow for the period was close to $5.5 billion.

CME Group faces risks from global economic and political conditions that can significantly impact trading volumes. The company is subject to intense competition from other exchanges, over-the-counter markets, and digital asset platforms. Regulatory and compliance risks are also high, including potential new transaction taxes and oversight of prediction market activities. Operational risks include heavy reliance on third-party providers such as Google Cloud and the potential failure of clearing firms.

S&P Global faces significant cybersecurity and technology risks as a central provider of market data. The business is sensitive to interest rate fluctuations and market volatility, which can reduce the volume of new debt issuance. Competitive risks come from both traditional and non-traditional data providers, while regulatory oversight of credit rating agencies remains intense. It also faces operational risks from its reliance on third-party infrastructure providers such as Amazon (AMZN +2.94%) and the complexity of its planned segment separations.

CME Group has a lower forward P/E based on future earnings estimates, while S&P Global maintains a lower P/S ratio, which measures market value against sales over the past 12 months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

CME Group's revenue growth is pretty uninspiring, but S&P Global's isn't significantly higher. Furthermore, CME pays a higher-yielding dividend. But I think the most attractive thing about the derivatives specialist is its net margin. CME's 62% net margin is basically twice what S&P Global reported in fiscal 2025.

Past performance is no indication of future results, of course. But CME Group stock has trailed S&P Global by more than 100 percentage points over the past 10 years. Indeed, CME hasn't even kept pace with the S&P 500.

An impressive net margin and a modest dividend are nice, but ultimately I want a stock that will increase in value. (Pretty core investing concept!) If CME Group hasn't beaten the market over the past decade, why should I have any expectation that trend will reverse itself now? S&P Global gets my vote here.

Erin Kennedy has positions in CME Group. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, CME Group, and S&P Global. The Motley Fool recommends Flutter Entertainment. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3146. Bill Ackman and Warren Buffett Both Have the Same Warning About the Stock Market

The billionaire investors both see the stock market behaving more like a casino of late.

Some of the smartest, most experienced investors in the world are concerned about the markets right now. While the S&P 500 (^GSPC +2.35%) has continued to do well this year, rising by 10% and hitting new records along the way, it hasn't been an entirely smooth ride. Plus, with stock valuations remaining elevated, there's the risk that there may still be a large pullback looming.

Bill Ackman and Warren Buffett are among the most well-known and successful investors in the world, and both are seeing cause for concern in the stock market. They've also issued similar warnings about the market this year, suggesting there's too much risk-taking and gambling going on.

In a recent interview with CNBC, Ackman pointed to the nonsensical prices that many stocks trade at, stating that, "stocks trade at prices that make no sense to me" and that "we're back to the casino-type markets when there was 95% leverage."

This sounds remarkably similar to the warning Buffett issued months earlier about the difficulties in finding stocks to buy given their egregious valuations. "It's tough to find values when everybody is preferring gambling." Buffett, the former Berkshire Hathaway CEO, has in recent years mentioned the inability to find good deals in the markets and not seeing a whole lot to buy. Now, with the S&P 500 on track to deliver a 10% gain or better for the fourth consecutive year, it may be more difficult than ever to do that.

Many stocks have been soaring to incredibly high valuations even though they don't appear to be justified given their underlying financials. Space Exploration Technologies Corp with its roughly $2 trillion market cap, stands out as an obvious example, as does Palantir Technologies, which trades at more than 100 times its earnings.

Buying these types of stocks at mammoth premiums opens investors up to the risk of corrections later on. It's important for investors to be prudent and disciplined when investing in stocks to ensure they aren't just chasing hot trends that may end up fizzling out later on.

For long-term investors, the safest move may be to simply track the S&P 500 through index funds these days. Ackman and Buffett's warnings, however, shouldn't be ignored, as there are definitely reasons for investors to exercise caution these days.

David Jagielski, CPA has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Berkshire Hathaway, Palantir Technologies, and Space Exploration Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3143. Gaining Financial Control Requires More Than Just a High Income

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Income is one factor to feeling financially in control, but your approach to managing your money is crucial, too.

Earning more money can boost your feeling of financial control.

Nearly 9 in 10 (89%) Americans with a household income of $100k-plus say they feel in control of their day-to-day finances compared with 62% of Americans with a household income of less than $50k, according to NerdWallet’s October 2026 Financial Resilience Index survey conducted by The Harris Poll.

But getting a raise or a higher-paying job isn’t a surefire way to feel in control. Pairing your paycheck with good financial habits is a more robust approach.

Nearly 4 in 5 (78%) Americans feel in control of their day-to-day finances, according to the survey. That’s up from 73% in September.

There’s no single external measure for what makes someone feel in control of their finances. If you swap financial positions with someone else who feels in control, for example, there’s no guarantee you would feel the same way.

That said, a person who lives comfortably with what they have and who has the ability to withstand unexpected expenses has a good chance of feeling in control.

Americans with lower household incomes are much more likely to feel a lack of control in their day-to-day finances compared with those with higher household incomes.

That may seem intuitive. Someone with a high income is more likely to be able to cut back on luxuries, such as dining or vacations, when the costs of groceries and healthcare rise. Someone whose income is lower might not have expenses to cut.

However, that narrative is an overgeneralization.

If feeling in control of your finances depended entirely on having a high income, you might expect to see a much higher share of Americans with low incomes who feel they aren’t in control of their day-to-day finances.

But in fact, the majority (62%) of Americans with a household income of less than $50k do feel in control of their day-to-day finances. At the other end of the income spectrum, it’s clear that money doesn’t automatically result in feeling in control: About 1 in 10 Americans (11%) with a household income of $100k+ say they don’t feel this.

A high income alone won’t offset spending more than you earn, and it isn’t a substitute for an emergency fund. Good financial habits are an important component of financial control, too. They may look like:

Preparing for an emergency: Car repairs, medical bills, broken appliances: There are plenty of expensive emergencies that can pop up, even if you’re doing everything right. One-third (33%) of Americans don’t have enough cash on hand to cover an unexpected $1,000 expense if it popped up this month. Those who have an emergency fund can avoid relying on credit.

Managing debt: Monthly expenses that exceed income indicate a shaky financial footing — not great for developing a feeling of control. Nearly 2 in 5 Americans (38%) say they’ll have to rely on credit to manage expenses in October. Managing debt goes beyond paying your credit card bill each month. For example, if you get the maximum car loan or mortgage that lenders approve, a large portion of your paycheck is committed to debt payments. Keeping your overall debt load low gives you flexibility if your situation changes in the future.

Avoiding expensive mistakes: Saving money takes time. Spending it can happen quickly. A separate recent NerdWallet study found that 3 in 5 Americans (60%) say they’ve spent money on something expensive and later regretted it. Luxury items, gambling and vehicles topped the list of regrettable purchases. Pump the brakes on avoidable expensive purchases. For any big buy, take a day or two to mull over your options, especially if you have outstanding debt or if your emergency fund is low. Considering spending decisions carefully is another way to feel more in control.

The feeling of control is likely to emerge over time. Build momentum by making progress on one tangible financial goal — saving money or paying down debt, for example — before adding another.

Yes, Americans across a range of incomes report feeling in control financially. But it would be glib to suggest that this demonstrates that the feeling of financial control can be flicked on like a light switch, if only people tried harder.

There are plenty of reasons why the road to feeling in control can be arduous. Family obligations, medical concerns, affordability issues and limited opportunities can all present real obstacles, even for those who strive to practice sound financial habits along the way.

If you’re struggling and need assistance, start with these resources:

Call 211 or visit 211.org. They’ll help connect you to the relevant resources in your area.

Can’t pay your bills? Zero in on your options using this quick-help tool.

#3140. Coca-Cola's Dividend Has Grown 64 Years in a Row. I'd Buy It in November and Never Sell.

The same formula for success has worked for more than six decades, and it should continue to do so.

If you want to know why so many investors, including the great Warren Buffett, love Coca-Cola (KO +0.38%) stock, look no further than the company's impressive dividend history. The global beverage giant has raised its dividend for 64 consecutive years.

A streak that long doesn't happen by accident. Coca-Cola has battled through mistakes, recessions, up-and-down markets, and even a global pandemic over the past six decades.

That's ultimately why Coca-Cola is precisely the type of stock an investor can buy in November and hold indefinitely without losing a wink of sleep at night.

Coca-Cola's empire stretches well beyond its namesake soda, including 32 brands with at least $1 billion in annual sales. It sells 2.2 billion servings of its products each day across more than 200 countries.

All those microtransactions add up to a size and scale that other companies struggle to compete with. It has an unmatched distribution network, putting Coca-Cola products on the best shelves at countless stores, in venues, and in vending machines.

The company has various paths to the steady growth it seems to produce year in and year out, decade after decade.

Coca-Cola has responded well to changing consumer preferences over the years, leaning into zero-sugar brands to appease health-conscious consumers. It launches new flavors and acquires up-and-coming brands to expand its portfolio. Of course, it can simply raise prices directly or through creative packaging and serving sizes.

And don't forget that population growth means more potential customers. Add it all up, and Coca-Cola doesn't have down years because it's a highly diversified business with a laundry list of ways to make more money, and it wields one of the world's most recognizable and beloved brand names.

Coca-Cola's revenue grew by 7% in Q3 2026, including global volume growth of 5%. Management now anticipates the company will grow comparable earnings by 11% in 2026 and generate over $13 billion in free cash flow. That puts the dividend at about 70% of guided 2026 cash flow, leaving a solid cushion and room for growth.

Analysts estimate that Coca-Cola will grow its earnings by an average of 7%-8% over the next three to five years. It's almost boring, but that's what this stock does. It delivers steady growth each year, with very little risk of catastrophe.

Perhaps the best news yet is that Coca-Cola still has plenty of room to continue expanding in a vast and fragmented global market. Management estimates that Coca-Cola still only holds a 14% share in developed markets and 6% in emerging markets.

It's generally a bad idea to buy and forget any stock, but Coca-Cola is arguably an exception. That's because the stock does its best work over the course of years, even decades. To maximize your return on Coca-Cola stock, you'll want to reinvest its famous dividend.

Reinvesting dividends means immediately pumping that dividend back into the stock for more shares that, you guessed it, also pay dividends. It adds an entire new compounding effect that makes a tremendous difference over time.

When you start measuring in decades, reinvesting the dividend can literally multiply your total returns. There's no reason to believe this formula can't still work, making Coca-Cola a no-brainer for any buy-and-hold dividend investor.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates went up today, but they're still in a much better place than this time last month.

Mortgage rates are higher today, but only by a little. And they're still hovering below 7%, which is much better than what we saw around this time last month.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked up to 6.98% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is two basis points higher than Friday and five basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Mortgage rates have more room to rise, as a stronger-than-expected jobs report reinforced expectations for a Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds are watching the October Consumer Price Index, which comes out on Tuesday. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at a different inflation measure, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Right now, the labor market is looking pretty darn strong: The October jobs report, released Friday from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, showed U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, far more than economists expected, and unemployment edged down to 4.1%.

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, but rate hikes are only on the table when the labor market is strong enough to handle it. Considering Friday’s jobs data, it’s looking fairly likely that the Fed will approve a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Still, a jump in mortgage rates isn’t a sure thing, and market sentiment could shift over the next month. October’s inflation data will capture the effect of the Fed’s last rate hike, which came in the middle of September. If that worked as intended and inflation shows signs of slowing, mortgage rates might remain around 7%.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.48% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3136. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#3134. CME vs. PayPal: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Two exchange-style rivals -- one a derivatives powerhouse with a 62% net margin, the other a payments giant trading at a far lower multiple -- and a tougher choice than it looks.

While digital payments reshape retail, global financial markets rely on stable exchanges. Deciding between CME Group (CME -0.45%) and PayPal (PYPL +1.37%) requires weighing high-margin stability against massive scale and a value-oriented valuation.

CME Group operates major derivatives marketplaces, benefiting from volatility in interest rates and commodities. PayPal remains a leader in digital wallets and branded checkout services. We're comparing these two companies because they represent different corners of the financial world, offering distinct risk-reward profiles for investors looking toward 2027.

CME Group operates the leading marketplace for derivatives, which are financial contracts that derive value from underlying assets. As a prominent player among financial stocks, it serves professional traders and global institutions across asset classes like interest rates, energy, and metals. The company relies on a member-based model in which exchange members drive 85% of derivatives volume, and it maintains a strategic partnership with S&P Global (SPGI +0.70%) for index-based products. In addition to traditional assets, the company offers products like Bitcoin (BTC -1.16%) futures and prediction markets through a venture with FanDuel, a subsidiary of Flutter Entertainment (FLUT +0.35%).

In fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $6.5 billion, representing growth of approximately 6.4% compared to the prior year. The company generated net income of roughly $4 billion. This resulted in a strong net margin of about 62%. Customer concentration is a factor to watch, as one clearing firm accounts for nearly 12% of CME's clearing and transaction fee revenue. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio, which measures total debt relative to shareholders' equity, was approximately 0.1. The current ratio, which gauges the ability to cover short-term liabilities with short-term assets, was about 1. During fiscal 2025, the business generated free cash flow of nearly $4.2 billion. Free cash flow is the cash a company generates after accounting for the money needed to maintain or expand its asset base.

PayPal operates a massive two-sided network with nearly 439 million active accounts across approximately 200 markets. The company provides a wide range of services, including Venmo for person-to-person transfers and buy now, pay later solutions for shoppers. It generates revenue by providing payment processing and fraud prevention tools to millions of merchants globally. By maintaining a lead in branded checkout, the company remains a central figure in the global digital economy.

In fiscal 2025, PayPal reported revenue of nearly $33.2 billion, an increase of approximately 4.3% from the previous year. The company achieved net income of close to $5.2 billion. These results produced a net margin of roughly 15.8%. While revenue growth has slowed compared to earlier years, PayPal continues to generate significant profits from its vast transaction volume.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio was approximately 1.3. The debt-to-equity ratio was roughly 0.5. PayPal generated free cash flow of close to $5.6 billion during fiscal 2025. This strong cash generation provides the company with flexibility to reinvest or return value to shareholders.

CME Group is subject to risks from global market and geopolitical volatility, which can negatively impact trading volumes and revenue. The company also faces intense competition from traditional exchanges like Cboe Global Markets (CBOE -0.14%) and Deutsche Börse (DBOEF +0.60%). Furthermore, the business carries heavy regulatory burdens and operational dependency on third-party data centers, where any disruption could lead to financial loss.

PayPal operates in the highly competitive global payments industry, facing pressure from larger tech-enabled players and nimble start-ups. The company must manage significant cybersecurity risks, including potential data breaches and system outages that could harm its reputation. Additionally, PayPal faces material legal exposure from ongoing securities fraud class action litigation and evolving global regulatory scrutiny regarding consumer protection and digital assets.

PayPal appears significantly more attractive on a valuation basis, as it trades at a much lower multiple of both sales and future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

The P/S ratio measures a company's market value against its sales over the past 12 months. The forward P/E ratio compares the stock price to future earnings estimates for the next year.

PayPal's valuation is clearly lower than CME Group's; investors evidently don't see the former growing much from here. While I don't think historical stock performance necessarily dictates future performance, PayPal's returns over the past 10 years don't compare favorably with CME Group's:

Over the past decade, CME Group has outperformed PayPal by more than 140 percentage points. In fact, PayPal's returns barely outpaced inflation during that period. The payments titan has been a pretty disappointing investment.

However, that chart also reveals something unflattering about both companies' share performance: Neither PayPal nor CME Group managed to keep pace with the S&P 500 over that time frame.

That said, this article is about comparing two financial stocks, not two stocks and an index. Between PayPal and CME Group, I think the derivatives specialist looks like the more attractive investment. CME Group has slightly stronger revenue growth and a significantly higher net margin, and that's why it's getting my vote today.

#3133. The New Economics Of Data Center Delay

Francesco Iorio is co-founder and CEO of Augmenta, a company automating building design for the construction industry.

Data center owners and developers are under enormous pressure to bring capacity online quickly. Every month a 60 MW facility sits idle costs its owner an estimated $14.2 million in lost revenue, financing costs and contractual penalties, according to STL Partners’ research.​

Experienced project teams already spend enormous effort identifying schedule exposure and squeezing time from procurement, sequencing, inspections, field execution and other familiar levers. Yet, some of the forces affecting energization remain difficult or impossible to control.​​​

Other forces are within an owner or developer’s control. Late design changes are a good example: Owners may deliberately change requirements to accommodate newer technology such as higher-performance GPUs with different rack, power or cooling requirements, because the economic benefits justify the disruption.​​

The challenge is therefore not always just preventing change, but building a delivery process capable of absorbing disruption.​​

That requires looking beyond the traditional places teams go to recover time. Some parts of the project workflow have historically been treated as relatively fixed because there was little practical ability to accelerate them or make them more responsive.

As technology reaches deeper into design, modeling and coordination, that assumption is beginning to change.​ Rather than replace proven schedule controls, the opportunity is to expand the field of view and to identify where technology has made previously difficult-to-influence parts of the workflow newly actionable.​​​​​​​

Design, building information modeling (BIM) and coordination are examples of areas where technology is beginning to change what is possible.

For instance, AI can now automate time-consuming modeling tasks, generate and evaluate routing options and identify potential conflicts earlier in the coordination process, reducing the manual effort traditionally required to produce and revise complex building designs.​

At Augmenta, where we’re developing spatial AI technology to automate building systems design, we’re seeing firsthand how these capabilities can give skilled practitioners more flexibility to refine designs and respond to project changes. While our work naturally informs my perspective, the broader opportunity extends well beyond any one solution.​

This is particularly relevant in data centers, where owner-driven changes to technology requirements can affect electrical, cooling and other systems. The faster teams can revisit and revise work already completed, the more room they have to accommodate those changes.​​

To identify these opportunities, a useful starting point is to talk to the people performing these workflows. Where is progress constrained by available skilled capacity? Which processes have historically been difficult to accelerate? Where does a change trigger significant downstream rework? And where has technology created options that didn’t exist a few years ago?​​

The answers may reveal constraints that teams previously had to accept but are now worth revisiting, not because they were managed poorly, but because the available tools have changed.​​

Identifying opportunities for acceleration is only part of the equation. Technology only reduces schedule risk if teams can deploy it without introducing greater disruption elsewhere.

The potential impact of any new technology must be balanced against implementation time, integrations, training and change management requirements.​​

This means prioritizing technologies that work within existing processes and give practitioners greater leverage rather than requiring wholesale workflow changes. A tool that accelerates one part of the project but creates additional work or delays elsewhere may simply relocate risk rather than reduce it.​​

To distinguish between the two, teams should start with the specific workflow constraint that a technology addresses and its potential schedule impact. They should also consider the time to value, adoption and training requirements and integration with existing workflows. Equally important is whether the technology can respond to changing project conditions and whether its benefits extend downstream.​​

The goal is to identify technologies that create meaningful flexibility within the delivery process without introducing new constraints that offset the gains.​​

One important consideration is where in the project workflow those gains occur. Not all time savings have the same effect on the overall schedule. Improvements in upstream workflows like design, modeling and coordination can create margin that carries into multiple downstream activities.​​

Accelerating these workflows can create flexibility and optionality later in the schedule, which greatly reduces risk. The value becomes especially apparent when requirements change: time saved upstream gives teams more room to revise, re-coordinate and respond without immediately affecting downstream milestones.​​

I’ve seen this shift firsthand on a large-scale data center project, where an electrical contractor used spatial AI design technology to accelerate the initial modeling process. Rather than spending weeks manually populating the BIM model, the team was able to generate an initial model much faster, freeing skilled virtual design and construction professionals to focus more of their time on reviewing the design, addressing constructibility considerations and preparing for downstream coordination.​

The takeaway goes beyond time saved to how the workflow changes when teams are no longer constrained by the same volume of repetitive manual modeling. That creates additional room to revisit design decisions, coordinate with other trades and accommodate evolving project requirements before they affect downstream activities.​

This is where “speed” becomes a risk-management tool rather than simply a productivity metric.​

Owners and developers cannot eliminate every threat to a data center schedule, nor should they abandon the proven controls they already use to manage risk. ​

But now, there is an opportunity to expand the field of view. As technology reaches deeper into design, modeling, coordination and other parts of the project lifecycle, processes that once offered little room to maneuver may become new sources of schedule margin.​​

By digging deeper into workflows that have historically been difficult to accelerate or influence, teams may find new opportunities to create flexibility before they need it and make the overall schedule more resilient when the inevitable disruption arrives.​​

#3132. Brookfield Infrastructure: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Long-term investors should weigh whether the AI infrastructure pipeline can keep pace with the stronger demand management describes.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 6, 2026, Brookfield Infrastructure (BIPC +0.53%) reported funds from operations, a cash flow metric for infrastructure assets, of $717 million. Predictable cash generation allows the business to fund large capital projects without straining its balance sheet. Three things management said stand out.

Tech giants can't expand artificial intelligence capabilities without massive power and compute capacity, creating a lucrative growth avenue for infrastructure operators. Long-duration contracts ensure full capital recovery while shielding cash flows from technology risks.

Rising borrowing costs can erode infrastructure returns when corporate balance sheets are tied to floating rates. Management limits this volatility by staggering debt maturities and locking in fixed interest rates across the vast majority of obligations.

A unified corporate structure makes equity units easier for major institutional funds and passive indexes to hold. Closing this transaction expands liquidity and supports broader ownership over the coming quarters.

Demand for long-duration infrastructure capital outpaces supply across data, energy, and transport markets. Platform scale provides a distinct edge when negotiating multi-year power contracts, while management tracks capital scarcity across global debt markets.

Organic project execution across the capital pipeline underpins operational momentum through year-end.

#3131. ​The Two-Headed Hydra: How AI Is Changing Software Development And Cyber Risk

Ken Ammon is CEO of CodeHunter, a serial entrepreneur who exited OPĀQ and NetSec, and former U.S. Air Force captain at the NSA.

​It sounds like a security nightmare, but it is quickly becoming operational reality: The same AI capabilities helping enterprises build software faster now help adversaries attack it faster.

AI coding tools and development agents are now part of the software delivery process. They help developers write scripts, update dependencies, troubleshoot builds, generate tests and move code through workflows with less manual effort. Attackers are using the same class of capabilities to build malicious software more efficiently.

They can automate reconnaissance, generate exploit code, test evasive variations and tailor malware to a target environment with ease. AI is now accelerating enterprise software development and software-based attacks at the same time.

Meanwhile, AI-driven malware undermines the signals security teams have historically used to make trust decisions. Source, signing, provenance, dependency scans and reputation remain important, but become unreliable when attack code has never been seen before, is short-lived and designed to change.

These indicators do not prove what code will do when it runs. Post-execution detection has the same limitation with worse timing. By the time suspicious behavior is visible, the artifact may already have accessed credentials, modified system settings or established persistence.

The more durable signal is behavior, and the most important decision point is whether that behavior should be allowed before execution.

​We are already seeing why. Recent npm supply chain campaigns show how quickly malicious packages can move into developer environments and CI/CD workflows. Some packages are designed to steal cloud and build credentials. Others profile developer machines or abuse dependency confusion to blend into normal package resolution. The attack path is familiar to any security team: A package looks useful, gets pulled into a workflow, runs during installation or build and inherits the permissions of the environment that executed it.

​AI raises the pressure on traditional security models because it reduces the cost of variation. Attackers can generate more package names, alter payloads, test different install behaviors and adapt code faster than a team can manually review each artifact.

​The same dynamic applies inside the enterprise. AI coding tools and development agents can generate scripts, modify files, recommend dependencies and issue commands as part of normal software workflows. In many cases, a human still approves the pull request or reviews the output. In other cases, the review is shallow because the pipeline is moving fast and the AI-produced change looks routine.

​That is where the risk concentrates. A generated script may complete the requested task while also introducing access the organization did not intend. A dependency update may solve a build issue while bringing in a package with dangerous install-time behavior. A container may pass through a pipeline while carrying logic that should never run in production. The issue is not whether the code was created by an employee, an AI assistant or an attacker. The issue is what the code is capable of doing when it executes.

​The form of an attack can keep changing while its objective stays the same. A package may be renamed, a payload may produce a new hash, and a script may be rewritten each time a model is prompted. But code still has to act to achieve its purpose. If the goal is credential theft, persistence, external communication or unauthorized system change, those behaviors eventually have to show up. That is why security teams need the ability to make an enforceable decision before execution.

​The goal is not to slow development by forcing manual review of every artifact, but to identify where code gains meaningful execution rights and put an enforceable decision point in front of those paths before it runs.

​A good starting point is developer workstations, CI/CD runners, package managers, build identities, deployment scripts, container promotion paths and automation jobs. This is where code often inherits trust from the person, tool or service account that launched it. A package running on a developer laptop is one level of risk. A package running in a build system with access to signing keys, cloud credentials or production deployment rights has a much bigger security blast radius.

​The control point should focus on behavior before execution. If an artifact is about to run in a sensitive environment, the organization should know what it is capable of doing. Will it attempt to access secrets, create persistence or make unauthorized system changes? When the behavior falls outside policy, code execution should be prevented, restricted, isolated or the artifact sent for review before it is allowed to run.

​This approach is not new. Zero trust is already used to assess users, devices, sessions and workloads before access is granted. By moving the trust decision closer to execution, we can apply the same discipline to code.

#3130. 3 Things You Need to Know Before Buying SpaceX

Since the opening trading price at its initial public offering in June, shares in this innovative business have fallen by 18%.

Anthropic's upcoming initial public offering is getting a lot of the market's attention right now. But it wasn't that long ago when Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +5.81%) was the talk of the town. Now valued at $1.7 trillion, making it the world's eighth-most-valuable business, there's certainly a lot of excitement surrounding the company.

Given his track record, Elon Musk seems to automatically get the benefit of the doubt from capital markets. As a result, investors might not hesitate to immediately add this stock to their portfolios in the hopes of achieving massive returns. However, this is a good place to step back.

Here are three things you need to know before buying SpaceX.

Three segments drive SpaceX's financial performance. It generates revenue from its space (launch infrastructure), connectivity (Starlink internet service), and super intelligence (artificial intelligence solutions) operations. All are dependent on the company's ability to continue increasing payload capacity while lowering costs, as this supports the economic and strategic flywheel that enables overall success.

During the most recent quarter (Q3 2026 ended Sept. 30), Starlink collected $5.2 billion in revenue, rising 69% compared to the year-ago period. This was driven by 89% growth in consumer subscriptions, now totaling 13.6 million. Enterprise and government sales for this segment climbed 91%.

Starlink is the profit engine, and it's not even close. Its operating income was $2.4 billion in the most recent quarter, representing the bulk of SpaceX's profit. This resulted in a division-level operating margin of 45%.

The company's scaled connectivity platform brings in high-margin, recurring revenue with low marginal costs once the satellites are deployed. The financial windfall this produces supports SpaceX's other projects.

The major hyperscalers are Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, and Oracle. They're investing incredible amounts of capital, expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2027 in total, to build data centers that power artificial intelligence (AI) model training and inference. It's these businesses that sit right at the center of the AI boom.

SpaceX might deserve to be in this conversation. This segment's capital expenditures totaled $40 billion on computing capacity during the last nine months. And it rents this out to customers. Notable clients include Anthropic and Google Cloud.

In Q3, super intelligence posted an 830% year-over-year revenue surge to $7.6 billion. It now accounts for more than half of SpaceX's total top line.

The business eventually wants to deploy AI data centers in space, where abundant solar power is available. Musk said in January that "the lowest cost place to put AI will be space, and that'll be true within two years, maybe three at the latest."

To say that Musk works on ambitious projects might be underselling it. But nothing is as out-of-this-world as what SpaceX is ultimately trying to do. It's about "making humanity multiplanetary."

This strategy depends on the Starship project, which is the next rocket platform undergoing testing and waiting to become fully operational, being successful. This would serve as the main vehicle to conduct space missions. The business is betting on these to be reusable with minimal maintenance, handle heavy payloads, and drive lower launch costs.

Put aside whether you believe that colonizing Mars is even remotely realistic, and consider how it would impact SpaceX's economics.

The company would surely burn massive amounts of capital building what is essentially an interplanetary transportation network, not to mention the uncertainty around feasibility, progress, and timing. Additionally, there's a lot we still don't know about Mars.

The bull case is that SpaceX becomes a monopoly that controls all the digital infrastructure for a hypothetical Mars colony. And the business would act like a toll collector on anything moved between Earth, Mars, and deep space.

This is a complex enterprise. Investors now have some background knowledge of SpaceX.

Neil Patel has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, and Oracle. The Motley Fool recommends Space Exploration Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
