#3128. How Reading The Room Can Help Prevent ERP Go-Live Delays

Komal Goyal is CEO of 6e Technologies, a global IT advisory firm specializing in ERP implementation for commercial and government clients.

​Should a date decide when an ERP system is ready to go live?

ERP go-live dates are often tied to hard-to-move commitments, such as a fiscal year-end or a merger and acquisition (M&A) event. As the date approaches, teams focus on what dashboards can measure: testing, defects, integrations and training. But those measures do not capture every readiness risk.

Take Rhode Island’s payroll as an example. It went live as planned. Within weeks, unions reported missing wages, unpaid overtime and incorrect deductions. Root causes await an independent review, but the vendor cited the complexity of moving from fragmented legacy systems to a unified platform. However, technology rarely fails on its own; how people adopt, use and work around it often shapes the outcome.

Across more than 30 successful ERP implementations, I have found readiness gaps often appear first in behavior. Reading those cues early allows time to address problems while protecting the go-live date.

This is an important principle of ERP implementation assurance: independently testing whether an organization is ready to operate a new system, rather than relying solely on whether an implementation is on schedule.​

Reading the room starts with signals that never appear in test results or sign-offs. Here are five I watch for:

I have been left guessing in requirements discussions because only part of a process was shared. That under-sharing may be oversight or reluctance to expose informal work-arounds. Either way, missed details become missed requirements, which often surface after go-live as defects, delays or operational problems.

When a stakeholder stops discussing the future-state process to defend the current one, I pay attention. The objection may be valid or reflect concerns about changing responsibilities, authority or job security. I have seen people make a process sound more complex than it is or insist the new system cannot support it.​

Left unread, that defensiveness can lead to customizing around a problem that may not exist, adding effort and risk until go-live slips.

If a user engaged in testing suddenly gets frustrated, it’s easy to assume that perhaps the workflow is clunkier or a feature they rely on is missing. But sometimes the “missing feature” is lost authority, because a step they considered critical is no longer needed.​

If that goes unread, they may disengage from testing, and issues they would have caught surface later on.

Silence is harder to read. When someone who used to speak up goes quiet, I want to know what changed. They may feel speaking up is pointless, or fear what it could cost them. The concerns people stop raising do not disappear. They arrive at go-live.

Every go-live has a list of items to fix later. The list itself is not the warning sign; it is how difficult issues are handled. I pay attention when a question makes the room uneasy and is quickly deferred until after go-live without a clear owner or plan.​

Sometimes, a failed test is dismissed as minor because the launch date is approaching. Without assessing how it could affect daily operations, the team risks discovering the consequences after launch, when resolving them may be more disruptive.

Recognizing these patterns gives teams a chance to act early.​

I’ve seen readiness issues get harder to fix once go-live pressure builds. That’s why a strong change management lead should work closely with stakeholders to act on these findings in time.

However, more time does not inherently solve the problem. Idaho’s Luma rollout was delayed twice, yet an independent review found its change management left agencies and employees underprepared to work differently.

When a cue surfaces, look beyond the behavior to its cause.

If details are withheld, broaden the conversation until the full process, including informal work-arounds, is clear. If someone defends the old process or reports missing functionality, determine what need lies behind the objection before customizing or deferring a solution. If a workflow feels harder, assess whether the issue is design or user capability, then provide the necessary changes, training or support.

Where authority is changing, clarify decision rights. Retire work-arounds only when the new system meets the needs they served. If users go quiet, speak with them privately to understand why, then explain what concerns were addressed and what remains unresolved.​​

​​Importantly, while a change lead can drive this work, appointing someone outside of the program to independently challenge whether people are ready can help to make sure the pressure to meet the deadline does not overshadow unresolved readiness risks.​

As go-live nears, deadline pressure takes over. System integrators focus on delivery, internal teams manage execution, and leaders balance competing business priorities. Generative AI can accelerate this push, cutting implementation effort by up to 40% and testing time by 70%.

But faster delivery does not answer whether the organization is ready. That question needs someone far enough from delivery to challenge readiness. A dashboard or even GenAI can show participation dropped, but rarely why a respected user went quiet. Here, human judgment still has the edge over AI.

Emotional intelligence helps read that cue, and ERP experience tells whether it’s ordinary friction or a real readiness risk. Having the strategy in place to say “not yet” is how the date stops deciding the system change.​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3126. This Janus Henderson fund manager owns CoreWeave debt — here's why he's sticking with it

CoreWeave can overcome the market's skepticism about its debt.

That isn't the consensus view — some Coreweave debt remains deeply distressed with yields above 15%. But Coreweave should power through this tough stretch, according to John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson.

"It's a little bit of overreacting," Lloyd tells CNBC, referring to the sell-off in Coreweave bonds, which have tanked along with its equity.

Lloyd owns Coreweave 9.75% bonds due in 2031 in the firm's Multi-Sector Income Fund. The yield to maturity on the debt is now 15.97%, a more than 70% jump since its issuance in April. Bond prices and yields move in opposite directions.

"Bond prices are lower, spreads are wider, yields are wider, and I don't see a huge fundamental change in the ecosystem right now that would warrant that, other than their higher cost of financing, which I think they can get creative with," Lloyd added.

CoreWeave has taken on a massive amount of debt to fund its data center buildout. The company has roughly $51.6 billion in total debt, including about $35 billion in notes, bonds and loans.

The market is getting nervous about how CoreWeave will be able to keep financing its data centers, and at what cost. Its stock has tumbled 20% over the past week, while the spread on its five-year credit default swaps is nearly 1,081 basis points — lower than earlier in the session but up 13% week over week. Spreads of 1,000 basis points and above are generally a sign of credit distress.

CoreWeave shares moved roughly 2% higher on Tuesday, after tumbling over the past several days and hitting a 52-week low earlier in the session. Its bond yields and spreads remain highly elevated.

Lloyd believes the selloff is a bump in the road for the neocloud company.

The market has been spooked by the jump in Treasury rates, which pushes up funding costs for companies, he explained. The focus largely turned to companies like CoreWeave that have refinance risk when rates move higher, he said.

CoreWeave leverage is high with a debt-to-equity ratio of 702%, up from 290% a year ago. Moreover, CoreWeave will spend an estimated $36.4 billion on capital expenditures this year against Ebitda of $7.5 billion, according to consensus estimates.

Revenue and Ebitda will ramp up next year, hitting $26.5 billion and $16.7 billion respectively, analysts estimate. But capex will hit $47.9 billion, indicating the company may need to finance its data center buildout with more equity issuance and/or debt.

Lloyd isn't necessarily worried about CoreWeave's ability to finance its capital expenditures, despite the fact that its capex outweighs its free-cash flow.

"Next year … they're going to be generating operating free-cash flow that'll support some of that capex need," he said.

The company can also turn to other methods of funding, like more GPU financing, which will allow it to borrow against its graphics processing units or rental contracts, he noted. In addition, CoreWeave could ask its biggest customers — including Microsoft, Meta Platforms and OpenAI — to increase their prepayments, he added.

"They can probably get creative enough where they don't even need to come to the high-yield market," Lloyd said.

The company also doesn't have any near-term maturities, which means it isn't facing a refinancing risk anytime soon, he added.

Meanwhile the supply/demand dynamic should remain tight across the AI compute landscape. Overall adoption rates within the ecosystem are running above consensus demand curves, he said. Add to that a bottleneck in supply, with chips and memory power sold out, and it's a nice set up for CoreWeave.

"All data point to the business model functioning very, very well," Lloyd said. "CoreWeave is one of the best-in-class at getting data centers up and actually running them," he added, noting that CoreWeave's financials have been improving throughout the year.

Another turning point could come if Anthropic goes forth with an IPO, expected in November, followed by OpenAI. Successful IPOs for the frontier labs could soothe market nerves and help support the neocloud operators.

"You just need more of those data points where people become more comfortable with the AI demand side," Lloyd said.

CoreWeave still needs to convince markets it can handle higher market rates and get through the financing bottlenecks.

Lloyd sees the company making it, albeit with some bumps along the way. The bonds should recover, he says, "just not in that straight line."

#3125. If I Had $1,000 to Invest Today, I'd Buy This Trillion-Dollar Stock Instead of SpaceX

This stock looks like a bargain compared to SpaceX.

Elon Musk founded Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +5.81%) in 2002 to drive down the cost of space exploration, but the company has since expanded into the satellite internet connectivity and artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure businesses. SpaceX went public in June, and while its stock quickly soared to a high of $225, it has since lost 40% of its value (as of the market close on Nov. 6).

But SpaceX still has a hefty market capitalization of $1.8 trillion. The company has generated just $32 billion in revenue over the last four quarters, so its stock is trading at a hefty price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of 56.8, making it almost 10 times as expensive as the technology-heavy Nasdaq-100 index.

As a result, more downside might be ahead, which is why if I had $1,000 to invest today, I'd probably look elsewhere. Here's why Microsoft (MSFT +2.75%) could be a better buy for the long term.

AI labs like OpenAI and Anthropic have built some of the world's most powerful AI models, but their long-term success will be determined by how many enterprise customers they can attract, because businesses typically spend far more on software than consumers.

Microsoft has a distinct advantage over those start-ups, because it owns legacy platforms like the Windows operating system, Edge internet browser, and 365 productivity suite (Word, Excel, Outlook, and more). Windows alone has over 1.6 billion active users, so the company has an enormous existing customer base to which it can market its AI products. Copilot is Microsoft's flagship AI assistant, but in September, the company expanded it to become an entire AI operating system.

The new Copilot aggregates all of its previous capabilities, placing it at the center of the entire enterprise experience. From a single home screen, users can now ask the assistant to draft a document or build a presentation, using context from recent meetings or email chains. Moreover, Copilot Code allows practically any employee to create an app or a piece of software, even if they have limited technical expertise.

During Microsoft's recent fiscal 2027 first quarter (ended Sept. 30), Copilot's daily active users soared fivefold year over year, and the number of enterprise customers paying for at least 50,000 licenses for their employees jumped eightfold. Simply put, Microsoft is at the front of the enterprise AI race.

But then there is the Azure cloud platform, which offers hundreds of services enterprises need to thrive in the digital age. It's also home to Foundry, where businesses can access the data center capacity, ready-made foundation models, and other tools they need to develop and run their own AI software.

Azure's revenue increased by 48% year over year during the third quarter, and it was the third-straight period in which that growth rate accelerated. That included Foundry revenue, which more than tripled -- and it's still in the early stages of adoption with just 120,000 customers so far.

Microsoft stock is trading at a P/S ratio of 11.4, so while it's more expensive than the Nasdaq-100 which has a P/S ratio of 5.9, it's substantially cheaper than SpaceX. Moreover, Microsoft is highly profitable, so we can also value its stock using the more traditional price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio.

Based on the company's trailing 12-month earnings of $19.15 per share, its stock is trading at a P/E ratio of just 27.7, which is below its 10-year average of 32.2.

As a result, even though Microsoft is trading near an all-time high, it still looks like a more attractive buy than SpaceX based on valuation alone. It's worth pointing out that Elon Musk has put forward some ambitious forecasts, suggesting SpaceX could reach somewhere between $500 billion and $1 trillion in annualized revenue by the end of 2027, which makes the stock look like a bargain on a forward basis.

SpaceX's annualized revenue was $58.3 billion in the recent September quarter, so generating up to seventeen-fold growth in the space of a year will be no easy feat. Plus, Musk doesn't have a perfect track record when it comes to offering guidance -- after all, he has been promising fully unsupervised self-driving capabilities in Tesla's electric vehicles for over a decade, and most customers are still waiting.

Therefore, if I had $1,000 to invest today, I think Microsoft stock would be the safer long-term buy.

Anthony Di Pizio has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Microsoft, Space Exploration Technologies, and Tesla. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3124. The Homebuying Advice Service Members Need (but Don’t Always Get)

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

VA mortgage borrowers are quietly navigating a knowledge gap. Here’s how to close it.

If your understanding of a VA home loan starts and ends with “it’s for the military,” you’re not alone. For many eligible veterans and service members, the intricate workings (and benefits) of this U.S. government-backed mortgage are obscured behind lackluster guidance.

While there are classes and resources available, the military is broadly wary of steering members in a specific direction, says Alex Rodino, a real estate agent and U.S. Army veteran who served 10 years as an infantry officer and military advisor. “A lot of the course material is just very generic,” he says.

And that’s not a surprise. Considering the sheer size and scope of the military, providing personalized advice on VA loans to all its members would be no easy task.

That leaves real estate agents and lenders as the last line of defense, which is where a big part of the knowledge gap exists. Rodino purchased his first home in 2020 with a VA loan and says he didn’t like how ignorant he felt during the process.

“The reason I actually got my real estate license to begin with was because of that experience I had,” Rodino says. “Looking back on it, I had a lot of questions that didn't really get answered.”

But how can VA borrowers navigate this knowledge gap without having to become mortgage experts in their spare time? Here are some of the most important things to know when considering a VA loan, according to the financial experts and borrowers who have lived it.

“My mortgage broker said, hey, you might qualify for this, look it up,” says Elizabeth Valente, a real estate investor and former U.S. Army officer who bought her first home using a VA loan in 2021. When it came to the application process, she felt crucial information wasn’t readily available to her or fully explained.

In hindsight, Valente says, working with an agent or lender who had detailed VA loan experience would have been beneficial. Not only would it help untangle the specific paperwork and appraisal requirements, but that expertise offers an important layer of support for military borrowers.

“For some processes in the military, it's very hand-holding,” Valente says. “I'll tell you what to wear, what to bring, when to be there.” But that’s not always true when transitioning back to civilian life, and parsing specifics of a loan process can be challenging. “I don't think a typical Realtor would understand what that person's going through,” Valente says.

Finding professionals with the right expertise involves asking questions — and lots of them.

“If I'm the smartest person in the room, we have an issue,” Valente says. “Don't be afraid to tell someone if something doesn't make sense or if you don't understand the numbers.”

Before agreeing to work with a lender or mortgage broker, ask how their process works, what companies they work with, and how familiar they are with VA loans. Treat the initial conversation like an interview — you’re hiring them, not the other way around.

Knowing where to find your Certificate of Eligibility (COE) and how much you might be eligible to borrow is the responsibility of the person applying, Valente says. “All that information — it is available in the [VA] website but you really have to dig for it.”

And this information isn’t recreational reading — your COE lists your "basic entitlement," which is a crucial figure in VA lending. If you have full basic entitlement, the U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) generally guarantees 25% of the loan, which in most cases lets you put 0% down.

Why? With a quarter of the loan guaranteed by the VA, the lender is more protected and thus more willing to forgo a down payment.

So how do you know if you have full basic entitlement? Look for the figure $36,000 on your COE. This dollar amount represents full entitlement. Technically, basic entitlement is for loans up to $144,000, but then your bonus entitlement kicks in and you can go higher. The VA doesn’t set loan limits when you have full entitlement — though your lender will still decide how much you can borrow based on your income, debts, credit and the home’s appraised value.

Let’s say you want to buy a home in Fayetteville, North Carolina, and you need a $200,000 loan. In order to put no money down, your entitlement needs to cover at least 25% of the loan amount — in this example, that’s $50,000. After you purchase the home, your COE will show your basic entitlement as $0 and the entitlement charged to a previous loan as $50,000.

“Serving in the military is a unique investing opportunity that most people don't get,” Rodino says. “I wish someone had told me to buy a house when I got to my first duty station.”

If he’d known it was possible or understood how, Rodino says he would have been able to buy in 2017, move in 2020, rent out his first home and then repeat the process. “I'd have three by now and the first one would probably be paid off.”

This particular plan is partly based on the unique schedule of many military members. “Generally, you're moving anywhere from two to four to six years,” Rodino says. Owning multiple properties also leverages two big (and often misunderstood) perks of the VA loan: It’s possible to have more than one active loan at the same time, and after one year, you may be able to convert your primary residence to a rental.

Let’s go back to Fayetteville, North Carolina. After two years, you decide to rent out the home because you’re moving to Bell County, Texas. The home you want to buy in Bell County is $300,000, and in order to make no down payment, you need the VA to guarantee 25% of the loan ($75,000).

Because you no longer have full basic entitlement — remember, you already have an active VA loan elsewhere — your entitlement is capped at 25% of the conforming loan limit in the county where you plan to buy, minus what’s already being used on your first loan ($50,000 in our Fayetteville example).

Now for the math: The current conforming loan limit in Bell County is $832,750. Multiply this figure by 25% and subtract the $50,000 already in use: You have $158,187.50 of entitlement available to you — plenty to guarantee the $75,000 needed on the new loan.

If all of this sounds overwhelming, you’re not wrong. There are resources out there to help you figure all of this out, Rodino says. “The problem is, they're not always practical or they're not always apparent.”

The VA has a website that can walk you through entitlement calculations, but you should also ask your lender, broker or agent. If you’re working with experienced folks, they’ll be able to explain these details and help you get the most out of the VA’s guarantees.

The key takeaway is this: While each VA loan must initially be for a primary residence, borrowers can leverage their entitlement to build an investment portfolio without needing to cough up large down payments.

But these examples don’t consider all of the factors that go into approvals. Like most mortgages, your income, debts and assets dictate your eligibility. And although the VA doesn’t impose credit score minimums, most lenders do — NerdWallet's analysis shows 620 is a common floor.

It’s also important to note that a VA loan’s 0% down isn’t free. VA loans carry a funding fee based on how much you’re putting down and whether it’s your first or subsequent use (certain disabled veterans are exempt). You can pay this up front at closing or roll it into the loan amount; again, your team of professionals should be able to walk you through your options.

“I think there's the assumption that this is what we do: We get our VA home loan and we go buy a home,” says Julie Soares, financial readiness director for the Wounded Warrior Project. When we’re given access to a benefit that offers discounts and perks, it can be hard not to take advantage of it. But this is why financial awareness is critical.

There are a lot of conversations happening right now around homeownership and whether it’s the right choice, especially as affordability remains challenging. So if you haven’t heard it recently, here’s a reminder: Buying property is not the only way to build wealth or create financial security.

“The financial readiness program at Wounded Warrior Project is really leaning into getting further upstream in those conversations,” Soares says. Having a clear understanding of your benefits before you need to use them allows you to make more informed choices.

While it's helpful to understand your entitlement and what you may be eligible to borrow, you don't have to know every little detail about mortgages, or navigate the process alone. Sometimes, the most important thing to know about VA loans is where to turn for help.

If you are unsure of your eligibility or want more information about home loans, you can call the VA's home loans hotline at 877-827-3702.

“Please reach out to Wounded Warrior Project, reach out to the VA, reach out to any service organization,” Soares says. “Not one organization is gonna be able to fix or solve all of these problems. We have to do this as a group.”

#3123. The Biggest AI Agent Risk Is The Workaround Nobody Approved

Enterprises are learning that approving an AI agent and its intended task does not guarantee a secure outcome. How the agent completes the work is proving to be one of the biggest AI visibility and security gaps in the modern enterprise.

OpenAI’s review of unexpected model behavior during training and evaluation has now led it to notify more than 100 organizations. Its earlier disclosures showed how agents could depart from intended methods while pursuing a task. Models uploaded files to public services, communicated across separate training samples and searched public repositories for leaked API keys. In one case, collaborating agents made a workbook publicly downloadable after the intended local handoff failed. Similar behavior was also evident in the Hugging Face incident, where agents found workarounds when they encountered resistance.

That these incidents occurred in testing environments under close observation makes the enterprise lesson more urgent. If agents can find unauthorized routes in a lab designed to study their behavior, organizations should assume similar behaviors will surface in production environments where the execution path is harder to observe.

The common pattern is persistence. When one route fails, the agent uses the access, tools or communication channels still available to finish the task. The problem arises when the actions taken are not authorized.

That path can also be hijacked rather than chosen. In prompt-injection scenarios, malicious instructions embedded in a file can steer an agent to move data through an approved service, even when the sandbox operates as designed. The final output may look legitimate while the route to it has been compromised.

For security teams, this means that oversight must extend beyond the prompt and the final output.

Security teams already collect much of the evidence they need. The difficulty is seeing how it fits together.

An agent may receive an instruction in one application, continue through an employee’s authenticated session and hand part of the work to another agent. The prompt, subsequent actions and handoff are recorded in different places, leaving no clear record of how the original request became the final outcome.

Identity, endpoint and application tools record individual events, but investigators are left to reconstruct the execution path connecting them.

This also makes attribution difficult. Activity performed by an employee and activity performed by an agent can appear under the same identity. Investigators may know who owns the account but still be unable to determine who or what launched a process, handled a file or made an external connection.

Viewed alone, each event may appear routine. The risk shows up in the order of events, particularly when an agent encounters resistance and changes course. If that sequence is lost, security teams cannot tell whether the agent remained within the authority it was given. They may see a file transfer without seeing the failed attempt that prompted it or know that a task succeeded without knowing that the agent changed the destination.

​Organizations need a continuous record linking the person who initiated the task with the agent’s activity and the data affected. That record must follow the work across systems and show when the agent changes its approach.

Permissions still define where an agent can operate. They cannot demonstrate that every subsequent action remains appropriate. When an agent moves sensitive information outside an approved location or reaches beyond the task’s established purpose, the organization needs enough context to recognize the change and intervene.

That also means defining what an agent should do when an approved route fails. Some alternatives may be acceptable within the task’s existing authority. A workaround that changes who can access sensitive data should require approval before the agent proceeds.

This changes approval from a single decision into an ongoing assessment. The organization defines the agent’s authority before work begins, then evaluates whether its behavior remains consistent with that authority.

As agents gain more discretion, security teams will need to examine the path between instruction and outcome. The immediate risk is not an agent refusing its task but completing it through actions the organization never approved.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3122. Trump Media & Technology: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

#3121. Andy Jassy Just Delivered Great News for Amazon Stock Investors

AWS is growing at its fastest pace in years, and a $578 billion backlog suggests Amazon's huge AI spending is chasing real demand.

Amazon (AMZN +2.94%) has been spending an extraordinary amount of money building artificial intelligence infrastructure, and investors have been waiting to see whether those investments will pay off.

CEO Andy Jassy just gave them a pretty compelling answer.

During Amazon's third-quarter earnings call, Jassy revealed that Amazon Web Services (AWS) is growing faster than it has in nearly five years, with customers consuming new computing capacity almost as quickly as Amazon can install it. "[O]ur plan is working," he said.

That's encouraging news for a company planning to spend approximately $220 billion on capital expenditures this year. There's also plenty more to like about what Jassy had to say.

Amazon CEO Andy Jassy. Image source: Amazon.

Amazon's Q3 revenue was $201.9 billion, up 12% from a year earlier. Operating income increased 61% to $28.1 billion, giving the company a record operating margin of 13.9%.

AWS was the real standout. The cloud computing business generated $46 billion in revenue, an increase of 39.3% year over year. That's the sixth consecutive quarter of accelerating growth, putting AWS on an annualized revenue run rate of approximately $184 billion.

The business is becoming increasingly profitable. AWS operating income reached $17.8 billion, up 55% from $11.4 billion a year earlier.

Jassy told analysts that AWS has been installing an AI accelerator approximately every 14 seconds. "[C]ustomers are gobbling it up," he said. "Our core compute and storage revenue grew at their fastest rates in several years."

That's an important distinction. Amazon isn't simply building enormous data centers and hoping customers eventually show up. It's adding capacity to meet existing demand, and the company has the contracts to demonstrate it.

Jassy said AWS ended the quarter with a whopping $578 billion in backlog, representing triple-digit growth from a year earlier. Backlog represents future revenue associated with customer commitments, although the timing of revenue recognition depends on when Amazon delivers the contracted services.

For investors worried that AI infrastructure spending has gotten ahead of demand, that's a meaningful number.

Jassy also said AWS's traditional computing and storage businesses are growing at their fastest rates in several years. So while AI is attracting much of the attention, Amazon's existing cloud services are contributing to the acceleration.

There's another encouraging development. According to Jassy, profitability in AWS's AI business is improving rapidly, even with higher memory costs and depreciation expenses associated with its infrastructure investments.

That matters because Amazon needs more than growing revenue to justify its enormous spending. It needs those investments to generate attractive returns.

Amazon expects approximately $220 billion in capital expenditures during 2026, much of it directed toward AI infrastructure. That's an extraordinary commitment, even for a company of Amazon's size.

The effect is already showing up in its financial statements. Amazon reported negative free cash flow of $42.4 billion over the trailing 12 months, compared with positive free cash flow of $14.8 billion a year earlier. The company is generating substantial operating cash flow, but its investments in property and equipment are consuming even more money.

Investors also need to remember that backlog isn't guaranteed profit. Amazon must deliver the computing services, absorb the associated operating expenses, and earn enough to justify its investment.

Jassy believes the demand will support continued expansion. In fact, Amazon projects AWS revenue growth exceeding 40% in both 2027 and 2028. But that's management's expectation, not a guaranteed outcome.

Amazon's Q3 results offer something investors have been demanding from the companies spending heavily on AI: evidence that customers are actually paying for the additional computing power.

AWS revenue is accelerating, operating income is climbing, and its backlog suggests there's substantial demand still waiting to be served.

The company's capital spending remains a legitimate concern, particularly with free cash flow under pressure. A backlog of future business doesn't eliminate the possibility that customers will eventually slow their spending.

But Jassy's comments make a persuasive case that Amazon's AI investments are addressing real demand rather than speculative expectations. For investors willing to accept the near-term spending pressure, that's a compelling reason to remain interested in Amazon stock.

Patrick Sanders has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3120. First Look: Inside United’s New Club at Washington Dulles

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The airline’s newest lounge will be the second-largest in the network, offering flyers an upgraded experience at Dulles.

You’ll no longer have to go to the basement to get into a United Club at Washington Dulles International Airport.

The brand-new United Club in the new IAD Concourse E spans nearly 40,000 square feet across an upstairs floor and can seat 630 passengers, making it a significant upgrade to the carrier’s existing lounge portfolio at the airport. It’s the second-largest United Club after its newest lounge in Houston, which opened just weeks earlier.

The new lounge by gates E14 and E16 opens to customers Nov. 10, 2026. United invited me to a media preview of the lounge, and here’s what you can expect inside.

The lounge features several nods to D.C.’s iconic landmarks and monuments, as well as the region’s natural beauty.

When you first enter the lounge, you’ll need to scan your boarding pass and then head up the escalators (stairs and elevators are also available) to get to the lounge.

One of my favorite design features as a former D.C. resident is that escalator ride up, which has been recreated to feel like you’re inside a Metro station.

Thankfully, the escalator ride is not as long as at some stations. Looking at you, Dupont Circle!

Once you're upstairs, you’ll walk into the heart of the lounge, with two buffet stations, a centerpiece bar and plenty of seating options, whether you’re traveling as a group or by yourself. (More on food and beverage options later.)

It’s impossible to miss the soaring ceilings in this lounge, especially compared to the bunker-like atmosphere at some of the existing United Clubs.

Don’t forget to look up, where you’ll see a cherry blossom sculpture, an homage to one of the region’s most popular attractions.

There are also two additional lounging areas on both sides of the central area. One is inspired by the Blue Ridge Mountains, which you can see in the distance.

The other has a cherry blossom theme, with light fixtures that branch out from the ceiling.

Massive windows run throughout the lounge, allowing in tons of natural light and offering fantastic views of the airfield. United has a major presence at IAD, so expect to see plenty of United planes.

The United Club has a full kitchen, allowing it to offer upgraded food options compared to its existing lounges at IAD.

You can expect to find sandwiches, salads and hot protein dishes. The lounge also had a ramen bar and a chili bar set up during my visit.

The two buffet stations are located right next to each other, with a dessert station in between.

If you want something to drink, you can head to the bar or one of the hydration stations throughout the space.

There’s plenty of seating at the bar, which has a double archway design. There are also large TVs if you’re trying to keep up with one of Washington’s many sports teams.

The United Club has eight phone booths for those who need to make a call. They’re split between the two lounging areas in the club.

You'll also find a single-stall private restroom, a wellness room, and dedicated men’s and women’s restrooms.

There are no showers in this lounge. To access a shower in a United lounge, you’ll need to head to the Polaris Lounge, reserved exclusively for eligible business class passengers flying on United and select partner airlines.

You can access the United Club by holding elite status, a lounge membership, the right credit card or an eligible premium cabin ticket.

If there’s availability, United sells day passes at the door for flights departing within three hours. You’ll also need a valid same-day boarding pass on a United or Star Alliance flight.

Holding the right credit card is probably the easiest way to get in. United’s most high-end card comes with a United Club membership, while others might offer one-time passes.

If you’re looking for a United lounge, the newest club in Concourse E is where you want to be. It offers more space, lots of light and elevated food options compared to other United Clubs.

Its massive footprint could also ease overcrowding, a common complaint with other lounges.

One thing to consider is location. The concourses at IAD are spread out, and you may need to take a mobile lounge or train to get there. If you want to check out the lounge, make sure you give yourself enough time to get to your gate.

#3119. PRA Group: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Management expects legal collection costs to peak in 2026 and fall in 2027, a shift that could free up cash for investors weighing PRA's long-term payoff.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, PRA Group (PRAA -1.63%) announced that disciplined cost management helped drive trailing 12-month adjusted EBITDA, a measure of cash-oriented operating profit, up 9% year over year. Debt buyers rely on tight expense controls and selective portfolio purchasing to maintain returns across shifting credit cycles. Three key takeaways from management explain how these operational moves position the business for the long term.

Management is prioritizing net returns by redirecting capital away from a competitive U.S. market and toward European debt portfolios. European cash collections exceeded internal expectations by 3% during the quarter, supporting the strategic pivot.

Operational restructuring has eliminated more than 215 corporate roles since October 2025, trimming overhead staffing by over 25%. These workforce reductions cut third-quarter compensation and benefits expenses by 11% year over year, establishing a lower cost base for future operations.

Upfront investments in court-based debt recovery expanded third-quarter U.S. legal cash collections by 19% year over year to $148 million. As teams clear the remaining backlog of accounts, management projects annual legal collection costs will crest in 2026 and decline in 2027, freeing up future cash flow.

Multimarket flexibility allows the business to maintain buying discipline while expanding digital debt recovery capabilities. Deleveraging, or reducing debt relative to earnings, brought net leverage down to 2.6 times earnings for an eighth consecutive quarter.

Cloud infrastructure upgrades and automated workflow tools continue to enhance agent efficiency. Management expects these structural operational changes to compound long-term returns.

#3117. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#3116. AI Agents Behaving Badly: Four Security Rules For The Agentic AI Age

Srinivas Shekar, Founder and CEO, Pantherun Technologies.

​Bots now generate over 57% of all web traffic, compared with less than 43% of human activity. This is only set to expand as agentic AI proves its mettle in online shopping, research, cloud administration, customer service and more. But enterprise security systems were designed to give approved human users access while blocking human and machine intruders.

It isn’t just about overcoming the “prove you’re an approved human” paradigm that legacy security systems were built on. It’s also about dynamism. After all, humans have stable identities and permissions that can be established and recognized. But AI agents create subagents and change their behavior according to the task they’re executing. They can communicate and interact like humans, but they can also make API calls, move data, consume machine-readable streams, interact directly with enterprise infrastructure, create software tools on the spot and perform thousands of operations simultaneously.

In a world where malicious bots and benevolent agents co-exist, how do we tell them apart? And what happens when we can’t?

Malicious AI agents are already getting into places they shouldn’t be and executing actions. In July 2026, the agentic ransomware attack known as JadePuffer exploited a flaw in the AI development tool Langflow to conduct reconnaissance, steal credentials, encrypt files and demand a bitcoin ransom for release. Once inside, the agent determined what opportunities to exploit, attacked several avenues simultaneously, generated attack code dynamically and adapted its own code in real time, until it achieved success. The agent also created software on the fly, allowing it to move ransomware from a compromised container onto the host. It even wrote its own ransom note!​

And then there’s the speed element. Ransomware attacks traditionally required coordination among a team of highly skilled hackers. But now, autonomous agents can collapse the cost, skill and time required to execute an entire intrusion. The time it took for JadePuffer to destroy the production database was about 30 minutes.

This introduces a range of new security concerns specific to the age of AI: How do you verify agent identity? What data do you allow agents to access? How do you identify and intervene when an approved agent begins acting beyond its approved mission? And how do you ensure that today’s permissions don’t become tomorrow’s liabilities? Here are a few ideas:

It’s tempting to think about agents as mere extensions of the humans they represent. But humans and agents have different capabilities and different senses of discernment. Don’t expect your security systems to safeguard against bad agent behavior if you treat them as their respective trusted human handlers. Instead, give every agent a distinct machine identity that can be logged and analyzed. Microsoft’s Entra Agent ID, for example, now treats agents as unique user identities.

Just because a human has a set of permissions doesn’t mean that their agent should wholly inherit the same. Normalize limiting agent permissions by task, data access and timeframe. Subagents should face even more stringent restrictions. Within their limited sandboxes, agents should be allowed to access just the data they need to execute their tasks. To get there, they must pass strict, deterministic limiters on access and permissions.

And everything should have an expiry date. For any action that exceeds that limited scope, a human should be summoned to make the call. Always remember: An AI agent behaving badly doesn’t need to “hack” anything if it already has permission.

When your human staff are at home sleeping, what they know about your company is inaccessible by thieves. But agent memory becomes a new data repository that can be exploited by bad actors. Consider carefully what agents may remember and for how long they may remember it. Highly sensitive information like passwords and decryption keys, and even login paths to sensitive systems, might be better off forgotten by agents between sessions.

Imagine that an agent requests access to an encrypted database. The system decrypts it, with permission. But then the agent steps out of line, converting the underlying data into plain text and storing it somewhere else, where it’s vulnerable to unauthorized access. In this case, your own agent is “innocent” but your data still gets breached.

Here’s another scenario: An agent is given cryptographic keys for one mission, but you fail to limit its permissions and wipe its memory. When it runs another unrelated mission, it decides to use those keys again to access data that shouldn’t be used in this case.​

To avoid these kinds of scenarios, be very, very careful about what cryptographic keys you put in agents’ hands, and even more careful about letting agents retain persistent keys. Use keyless encryption if you can, so that agents don’t carry the keys to the castle around their virtual necks. Make decryption policy-aware, so that the ability to decrypt data doesn’t automatically confer the right to do so. And, whatever you do, maintain a zero-trust stance, so that humans and agents must always reconfirm their identity and permissions for every session.

The rise of agentic AI makes “zero trust” more important than ever. In the agentic era, identity, authority, memory and access all need to be treated as temporary and continuously reassessed. The goal isn’t to “keep bots out.” It’s to let the “good bots” in, without handing them the freedom to behave badly.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3115. Qualcomm vs. Western Digital: Which Tech Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Qualcomm's chip empire faces a customer concentration question, while Western Digital's storage margins look almost too good to be true.

As mobile connectivity and data storage demands intensify, Qualcomm (QCOM +3.66%) and Western Digital (WDC +9.86%) stand as pivotal players in the tech landscape. Investors must decide which business model offers better potential.

Qualcomm provides the essential chips for smartphones and connected vehicles, while Western Digital manufactures the storage hardware that anchors global data centers. These companies represent different corners of the tech ecosystem, making them an interesting comparison.

Qualcomm is a central figure among semiconductor stocks. It designs the chips that power high-end smartphones and is moving into the automotive and Internet of Things sectors. In its latest annual report, filed in late 2026, the company highlighted key customers like Apple (AAPL +2.43%) and Samsung (SSNLF +0.00%). Since these mobile handset customers account for a large share of sales, customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In fiscal 2026, revenue reached nearly $43.1 billion, a slight decline of roughly 2.6% from the prior year. Despite the dip in top-line growth, the company reported robust net income of approximately $13.6 billion. This equates to a net margin of close to 31.6%, which is a measure of how much profit is kept from every dollar of sales.

As of Qualcomm's September 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was roughly 0.5. This ratio measures total debt relative to shareholders' equity, with lower ratios indicating a more conservative financial structure. The current ratio, which shows assets available to cover short-term liabilities, stands at nearly 1.9. Free cash flow was close to $8.7 billion for the year, though note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 31% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Western Digital provides essential data storage solutions using hard disk drive technology for cloud and consumer markets. According to its latest annual report, filed for the fiscal year ending in 2026, the company noted a massive reliance on cloud providers. This cloud end market accounted for nearly 89% of revenue in fiscal 2026. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business, as three individual customers each accounted for 10% or more of total revenue.

For fiscal 2026, revenue reached approximately $12.9 billion, a jump of nearly 35.7% over the previous year. The company delivered net income of close to $9.4 billion. This resulted in an exceptionally high net margin of roughly 72.9%, indicating the company is keeping a large portion of its revenue as profit.

Based on Western Digital's July 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.1. This indicates a very conservative use of debt relative to shareholder equity. The current ratio stands at close to 1.3, meaning the company has $1.30 in short-term assets for every $1.00 in short-term debt. Free cash flow for the year was nearly $3.5 billion, and the company maintains a healthy financial position for its future projects.

Qualcomm faces significant revenue concentration in a small number of mobile handset customers. If key customers like Apple transition to their own internally developed solutions, the company could lose a major revenue stream. Additionally, high sales volume in China exposes the business to geopolitical tensions and trade restrictions. Competition is also intense, requiring constant innovation to stay ahead of rivals like Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSM +3.51%) in manufacturing and other chip designers.

Western Digital is heavily dependent on a limited number of cloud customers and suppliers for critical components. Any disruption in global demand or a shift in technology standards could hurt its market position. The company is currently navigating a transition to new storage technologies like HAMR. Furthermore, macroeconomic volatility and potential product defects or warranty claims represent ongoing concerns for the hardware manufacturer as it competes with companies like Micron Technology (MU +6.25%).

Western Digital appears more attractive based on its forward P/E ratio, while Qualcomm carries a lower P/S ratio relative to its total sales.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I think we've established I am a skeptic with some stocks (even a hater, perhaps). Western Digital has posted impressive growth recently, and yet. The data storage and memory markets are super cyclical, and right now data storage is trading at a wild premium. This long-term chart gives me pause, because WDC was plodding along for ages:

Furthermore, as you can see above, Qualcomm's and Western Digital's long-term returns aren't that far apart. In fact, Western Digital only really ran away with it in the last year or so:

I sometimes buy small positions in spicy, growth-y stocks just to expand my horizons. I don't feel inclined to think Western Digital is attractive in that way. Data and memory storage demand are so closely linked to AI demand right now that I think it's all just toeing the line between a boom and a bubble. Sign me up for boring smartphone chips; Qualcomm gets my vote.

Erin Kennedy has positions in Apple and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Apple, Micron Technology, Qualcomm, Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing, and Western Digital. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3113. Prediction Markets Are Arriving in Your Investing App, and That Makes Us Nervous

NerdWallet, Inc. is an independent publisher and comparison service, not an investment advisor. Its articles, interactive tools and other content are provided to you for free, as self-help tools and for informational purposes only. They are not intended to provide investment advice. NerdWallet does not and cannot guarantee the accuracy or applicability of any information in regard to your individual circumstances. Examples are hypothetical, and we encourage you to seek personalized advice from qualified professionals regarding specific investment issues. Our estimates are based on past market performance, and past performance is not a guarantee of future performance.

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. And while our site doesn’t feature every company or financial product available on the market, we’re proud that the guidance we offer, the information we provide and the tools we create are objective, independent, straightforward — and free.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us. This may influence which products we review and write about (and where those products appear on the site), but it in no way affects our recommendations or advice, which are grounded in thousands of hours of research. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews of their products or services. Here is a list of our partners.

Prediction markets are booming, but that doesn’t make them a sound investment strategy.

The investing information provided on this page is for educational purposes only. NerdWallet, Inc. does not offer advisory or brokerage services, nor does it recommend or advise investors to buy or sell particular stocks, securities or other investments.

Around this time every year, we conduct NerdWallet’s annual broker review process. We assess what’s changed in the industry, discuss which brokerage features matter most (and which ones now matter less), and finally, we do the work by opening accounts to test them.

Prediction markets, which have made their way into some of the most popular investing apps, came up more than we expected in testing this year.

At first, we decided to simply collect information on who does and doesn’t offer access to them, without intending to reward or penalize a broker either way. It would be a neutral presentation of facts, not something that would factor into how we scored a broker.

But one afternoon, all together in the office, we confronted an uncomfortable question: “Do prediction markets, and such easy access to them within an investment app, go against NerdWallet’s editorial house views?”

After careful consideration, we all agreed: they do.

So we started to configure our 2027 rubric with a firmer stance against this trendy feature, penalizing brokers that actively market sports and entertainment event contracts, and whose prediction markets come with few, if any, guardrails.

We’ve distilled our discussion down to three main points:

These products are being marketed alongside investments.

We fear they could be discouraging long-term investing.

Let’s start here: Many aspects of trading could be seen as gambling-adjacent. Options, futures, highly leveraged single-day equity bets and even crypto can all easily fall into the “highly speculative” category. So why are we picking on prediction markets in particular?

In our view, a key differentiator is a much lower barrier to entry with few in-app guardrails in place.

Most of the products we mentioned above require some level of fluency — and at times even permission from the broker — to dabble in. To trade options, for example, you need to answer questions about your investing proficiency, your finances and more. And even after approval, the terminology required to trade can be so intimidating that the language itself can become an indirect deterrent.

Event contracts, which form the backbone of prediction markets, are largely presented in the vernacular, and our testing showed approval to trade them is completed with a few taps from the home screen. Moreover, their content is often unrelated to finance — according to a May 2026 report by the Pew Research Center, around 80% of volume on the prediction market platform Kalshi was for sports events[1]Pew Research Center. Trading Volume on Prediction Markets Has Soared in Recent Months. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources .

Answering a binary question (yes/no; this/that), posed in everyday language about a familiar topic — something like, “Will the Knicks beat the Spurs on Sunday?” — can be far less daunting than figuring out what an options chain is or understanding forex pairs.

In other words, what makes prediction markets accessible to a wider audience is also what makes them problematic, especially when housed within an investing account. Which brings us to our next point.

On the platforms that feature them, we found that prediction markets almost always appear alongside stock and ETF trading. We think this is a mistake. What has made these mobile-first investing apps so good for so long (making it easy to move, manage and invest money through a beautiful interface) is what’s now making us nervous.

For example, most investing platforms let users do any number of smart things with idle cash. They could open an Individual Retirement Account (IRA), a custodial account or a robo-managed portfolio all through a single app. But now, they can dip into that same pot of money to bet on whether Beyoncé or Alicia Keys will play the Super Bowl halftime show.

But is anyone actually trading these event contracts? Let’s use data from publicly traded brokers that offer prediction markets to explore this.

In Q3 2026, Robinhood’s revenue from event contracts rose more than 600% year over year to $173 million, while the number of event contracts traded grew more than sevenfold to 16.4 billion[2]Robinhood. Robinhood Reports Third Quarter 2026 Results. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources . This was the second-highest source of transaction revenue behind options trading. In a forecast reported on by Cointelegraph, Bernstein analysts projected Robinhood’s prediction markets could drive $1.7 billion in annual revenue by 2028[3]Yahoo Finance. Bernstein Sees Robinhood Prediction Markets Reaching $1.7 Billion in Revenue by 2028, Topping Crypto. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources .

Coinbase's Q1 2026 quarterly report called prediction markets “one of its fastest-scaling products ever, driven by strong retail interest[4]Coinbase. Coinbase Q1 Financial Results Show Resilient Financial Performance Driven by New All-Time High Crypto Trading Volume Market Share. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources ."

In a call discussing Webull’s Q2 results, U.S. CEO Anthony Denier noted trading volume in Webull’s nascent prediction market product grew about 70% quarter over quarter[5]Gloom. Webull (BULL) Q2 2026 earnings call transcript. Accessed Nov 3, 2026. View all sources .

Gemini’s Q2 report shows prediction market volume rose 93% quarter over quarter[6]Gemini. Shareholder Update Q2 2026. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources .

So, now that we’ve established that these markets are growing in popularity — and investing platforms appear to have a large incentive to lean into them — how are all those event contract traders faring? That brings us to our final point.

Is all this even worth it? Several studies show how hard it is to actually turn a profit in prediction markets. Here are a few:

One study, conducted by researchers at ESSEC Business School, HEC Montréal and the University of Toronto's Rotman School of Management, covered 2.4 million Polymarket accounts across $67 billion in trading volume between November 2022 and March 2026, and found that 69% of users lost money[7]SSRN. Who Wins and Who Loses In Prediction Markets? Evidence from Polymarket. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources . Moreover, the top 1% of users who made a profit accounted for about 76% of all gains. Translation: Even if you did turn a profit, there’s a good chance you didn’t make much.

A Bloomberg analysis supports this: From January 2025 to April 2026, more than 100,000 Polymarket accounts lost at least $1,000[8]Bloomberg. Most Prediction Market Traders Are Losing Money While Bots Rack Up Gains. Accessed Nov 3, 2026. View all sources . Of the 2 million active wallets studied, almost half made or lost less than $10 during the period, while far fewer accounts collected larger profits.

A separate Wall Street Journal analysis echoed the same trend: 67% of Polymarket profits go to 0.1% of accounts, and more than 70% of Polymarket users lost money[9]The Wall Street Journal. Why Almost Everyone Loses—Except a Few Sharks—on Prediction Markets. Accessed Nov 3, 2026. View all sources .

While there is far less data available on Kalshi trades (Polymarket runs on a public blockchain, making it easy to extract and analyze data), a Kalshi spokesperson confirmed in the Wall Street Journal analysis above that one month of data in 2026 showed there were 2.9 unprofitable users to each profitable user[10]Wall Street Journal. Why Almost Everyone Loses—Except a Few Sharks—on Prediction Markets. Accessed Nov 10, 2026. View all sources .

The first study above discusses how event contract trading is binary and zero-sum: If one trader wins, another loses. This means traders allocating money to these instruments could be missing out on aspects of equity markets that have helped create long-term wealth for previous generations.

“Unlike equity markets, there is no equity premium, no dividends, and no passive investment options. Their short maturities naturally encourage short-horizon trading, which is precisely what the behavioral finance literature has suggested is most destructive to wealth accumulation. Given the rapid growth of these platforms and their role as a potential entry point for young investors, understanding who bears the losses has direct implications for investor welfare and market regulation.”

As a reminder, the vast majority of Polymarket users bore the losses.

At NerdWallet, our mission has always been to help people make smarter financial decisions, so we’re sticking by that. Starting with our 2027 ratings, brokers that offer easy access to sports and entertainment event contracts will lose points in our scoring system because we believe this new product has the potential to harm long-term wealth building and doesn’t belong in investing platforms.

So, what’s a better way forward? Bring back the friction and make prediction markets a separate app. Financial institutions, businesses and individuals can still explore legitimate use cases for prediction markets, such as forecasting and hedging. And we’re not here to stop anyone from trading event contracts for entertainment with extra money. We just don’t want these markets so easily conflated with the legitimate investing vehicles these apps already offer. And our reviews will reflect that.

#3112. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.
