#3111. From FinOps To TokenOps: The New Economics Of Enterprise AI Infrastructure

Gouri Sankar Dash, a technology leader at TCS with 20+ years driving enterprise data, AI platforms and multimillion-dollar transformations.

For years, enterprise infrastructure leaders have focused on optimizing compute, storage, networks and cloud consumption. FinOps introduced financial accountability to cloud infrastructure, helping organizations manage spending and improve efficiency.

AI is introducing another economic dimension. As enterprises adopt GenAI and autonomous agents, infrastructure costs increasingly depend on model selection, inference workloads, token consumption and agent interactions.

The next frontier of infrastructure optimization isn’t simply managing the cost of computing but understanding the cost of intelligence.

This article begins a series on enterprise AI infrastructure economics, examining how AI consumption is reshaping enterprise infrastructure economics, architecture and operations. I believe this evolution calls for an expanded operating model: TokenOps.

Traditional infrastructure optimization measures CPU utilization, memory, storage and cloud spending. AI introduces additional variables: input and output tokens, context windows, model selection, retrieval operations and inference infrastructure.

Consider an AI agent investigating an infrastructure incident. It may analyze monitoring alerts, retrieve historical tickets, examine configurations, consult runbooks and coordinate with other agents before recommending remediation. Every interaction consumes resources. Without proper orchestration, organizations risk creating another form of infrastructure sprawl through unnecessary AI interactions and repeated reasoning.

FinOps remains essential, but enterprises must also understand how AI consumption translates into operational value. A million tokens consumed is a cost metric. An incident resolved accurately and efficiently is a business outcome. Understanding that relationship is where TokenOps begins.

A recent walkthrough with a large regulated enterprise reinforced an important lesson: Don’t start with the AI agent. Start with the operational foundation, knowledge and controls required for reliable decisions.

The enterprise prioritized integrating telemetry, service management data, access controls, automation, runbooks, incident history and engineering knowledge. Its technology ecosystem included ServiceNow for workflows, Amazon Bedrock for AI capabilities, Dynatrace, Datadog and Amazon CloudWatch for operational signals and Ansible for controlled automation.

Specialized agents supported event correlation, incident diagnosis, change-risk assessment, remediation planning and engineering assistance. Each agent had a defined responsibility. Low-risk activities followed approved automation paths, while high-impact changes required engineering authorization.

The architectural lesson wasn’t the number of agents deployed but how effectively operational knowledge, intelligence and controlled execution worked together. Well-structured knowledge and reusable workflows can reduce redundant investigations, repeated retrieval and unnecessary AI interactions.

This is where intelligent operations and token economics converge.

I view TokenOps as an operating discipline that extends financial accountability into AI consumption, infrastructure efficiency and measurable business outcomes. Five dimensions form its foundation:

1. Consumption Visibility: Track token usage, model costs, agent interactions and infrastructure consumption across enterprise workflows.

2. Intelligent Workload Optimization: Match model capabilities to task complexity. Routine classification may require smaller models, while complex incident analysis may justify advanced reasoning capabilities.

3. Knowledge And Context Efficiency: Optimize retrieval, caching and context management to avoid repeatedly processing unnecessary information.

4. Governance And Controlled Autonomy: Establish access boundaries, approval thresholds, auditability and rollback mechanisms. Expand autonomous execution only when operational evidence supports it.

5. Outcome-Based Economics: Measure cost per resolved incident, resolution time, repeat incidents, engineering effort and operational reliability.

The objective is maximizing the value that AI-enabled workflows generate.

Enterprises can begin applying TokenOps principles without redesigning their entire infrastructure.

• Start with operational friction. Target recurring incidents, alert noise, slow diagnosis and excessive handoffs.

• Build a reliable knowledge foundation. Integrate telemetry, configuration information, service models, historical incidents and runbooks.

• Earn autonomy incrementally. Begin with AI-assisted investigation and recommendations before introducing approved remediation.

• Redesign workflows, not tools. Embed AI capabilities into existing engineering and service management processes.

• Measure outcomes alongside consumption. Evaluate AI costs against reliability, resolution efficiency and engineering productivity.

These practices help prevent AI investments from becoming another unmanaged operational expense.

Enterprise infrastructure economics is evolving through three interconnected stages:

• Traditional Infrastructure Economics: Optimizing physical assets, capacity and technology investments

• FinOps Economics: Managing consumption-based cloud spending and financial accountability

• TokenOps Economics: Optimizing AI consumption, intelligent workloads and measurable business outcomes

TokenOps complements FinOps rather than replacing it. Its implications extend beyond token pricing. Model placement, GPU utilization, caching, network latency, data gravity and hybrid-cloud architecture can significantly influence AI operating costs.

A workload that appears economical under traditional infrastructure metrics may become expensive when repeated inference, extensive retrieval and multi-agent orchestration are considered. Infrastructure leaders must evaluate not only where applications run but also where intelligence should be generated.

The future of enterprise AI will depend less on how many agents organizations deploy and more on how efficiently those agents operate.

Should every AI request use the same model? When should inference run on-premises rather than in the public cloud? Can intelligent caching eliminate unnecessary processing? How will token economics influence data center migration decisions?

These are becoming infrastructure strategy questions.

How can organizations connect AI agents, observability, model routing, knowledge systems and financial controls into a practical operating architecture? I plan on exploring that in the next part of this series.​ The ultimate goal is building infrastructure that delivers the right intelligence, in the right place, at the right time and at a justifiable cost.​

#3109. Chevron Is Doubling Venezuela Production. ExxonMobil Says It's Not in a Rush. Who's Right?

ExxonMobil may be right to stay out of Venezuela.

Chevron (CVX -0.86%) plans to invest more than $7 billion in Venezuela over the next five years, potentially more than doubling its oil production there to about 600,000 barrels per day. ExxonMobil (XOM -0.69%), meanwhile, isn't interested in rushing back into Venezuela. And quite frankly, I think ExxonMobil may have the better strategy.

Chevron isn't starting from scratch. The company has operated in Venezuela for more than a century and maintained a presence, even as political instability and economic turmoil have driven other international oil companies away. In fact, in September, Chevron announced agreements providing improved fiscal, commercial, and legal terms for its Venezuelan operations. It also secured additional acreage in the Orinoco Belt.

Management believes it can expand production while keeping total costs below $20 per barrel. Indeed, that's a pretty attractive proposition.

Chevron already has infrastructure, local relationships, and operating experience. Expanding existing production is considerably different from building an entirely new business in a country with Venezuela's history. And if oil prices remain favorable, Chevron could generate substantial additional cash flow without taking on the same level of risk as a newcomer.

Exxon has good reasons to be skeptical. The company has had its Venezuelan assets seized twice, most recently in 2007 under former President Hugo Chávez. CEO Darren Woods has indicated that Exxon would consider returning, but only with stronger legal protections and investment terms that justify the risks. I don't blame him.

Venezuela's oil infrastructure needs substantial investment, political uncertainty remains high, and there's no guarantee future governments will honor today's agreements. And there's also reason to consider long-term oil demand.

There are varying projections on this. The IEA projects that global oil demand could plateau around 2030. Wood Mackenzie has that plateau hitting in 2032. TotalEnergies (TTE +0.20%) has it between 2035 and 2040, while OPEC suggests "robust global oil demand growth prospects to 2050, with no signs of oil demand peaking over the forecast period." But even with OPEC's optimistic scenario, 2050 is only about 23 years away.

Of course, even if demand levels off, existing oil fields will continue declining. The industry will still need replacement production. But if demand growth slows while producers keep expanding capacity, oil prices could face additional pressure. Under these conditions, spending billions on politically risky projects becomes harder to justify.

Chevron's expansion isn't necessarily a mistake. Its existing operations, relatively low production costs, and improved contractual terms could make Venezuela a profitable source of additional oil production. But ExxonMobil doesn't have the same advantages. It would potentially need to commit substantial capital to rebuild operations in a country that has already confiscated its assets.

The company has other places to invest, including Guyana and the Permian Basin, where it has established operations and greater confidence in its ability to generate returns. Overall, ExxonMobil's strategy just makes sense. Yes, Chevron could make plenty of money in Venezuela. But ExxonMobil is demonstrating that having the financial resources to pursue an opportunity doesn't mean you should. Sometimes the better investment decision is knowing when to walk away.

Jeff Siegel has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Chevron. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3103. Why The Future Of Materials May Be Biology-Enabled

Kim Thompson, Founder and CEO, Kraig Biocraft Laboratories.

​For much of the past century, materials innovation has been driven by chemistry, engineering and increasingly sophisticated manufacturing. Those disciplines will continue to shape what we build. But another field is becoming an important part of the conversation: biology.

Advances in genetic engineering and biotechnology are giving us new ways to produce fibers, proteins and other materials using living systems. In some cases, the goal is to recreate properties already found in nature. In others, it is to take what biology does well and use it as the starting point for something entirely new.

This shift is becoming increasingly important as industries look for materials that deliver better performance while reducing dependence on petrochemical-heavy production. Several forces are driving that shift at once. Manufacturers are being asked to improve durability, strength, weight and functionality, while also considering supply chain resilience, resource use and the long-term environmental impact of the materials they choose.

Biology-enabled approaches will not solve every one of those challenges, but they give engineers and product developers a broader set of options than they have had in the past.

Nature has had millions of years to develop structures with unusual combinations of properties. Spider silk is an obvious example because of its combination of strength, toughness and flexibility. Wood provides strength with relatively low weight and low cost. Bone is both rigid and dynamic. Shells achieve remarkable durability through layered structures built from relatively simple components. These examples matter not because we should copy them, but because they show what biological systems can create.

Until recently, our ability to translate those capabilities into useful products was limited. A naturally occurring substance might be difficult to harvest, available only in small quantities or produced by an organism that cannot realistically become part of an industrial supply chain. Biotechnology gives us a different way to approach that challenge. Instead of asking how we can extract more of a material from nature, researchers can increasingly ask what genetic information gives the material its desired properties and whether that information can be adapted to a more practical production system.

The sustainability case for biology-enabled materials gets a great deal of attention, particularly as manufacturers look for alternatives to petroleum-derived fibers and other traditional inputs. That matters, but sustainability alone will not determine which technologies succeed. Economics and performance will.

A new fiber or advanced material has to do something useful. It may need to be lighter, stronger, more flexible, more durable or capable of performing under conditions where conventional alternatives fall short. It also needs to be made consistently enough that manufacturers and customers can rely on it. That creates possibilities well beyond consumer products.

Technical textiles, protective equipment, transportation, aerospace and industrial applications all place demanding requirements on the materials they use. Even relatively small improvements in weight, strength or durability can have a meaningful impact in those settings. “Authored biology” will give developers another way to pursue those improvements. Authored biology is an approach in biotechnology and genetic engineering that enhances existing, time-tested natural systems, proteins and genetic structures rather than building synthetic biological components entirely from scratch.

One of the most interesting possibilities is that biology may allow us to think about materials less as fixed substances and more as platforms that can be tuned. Traditional material development often begins with an existing substance and asks how its properties can be modified through processing, additives or changes in structure. Bioengineering adds another layer.

As the biological instructions responsible for a protein or fiber are understood, it can become possible to adjust characteristics much earlier in the process. Rather than accepting the properties nature provides, scientists may, and in some cases are able to, influence those properties at their source. We are still early in that evolution, and there is a considerable difference between what is theoretically possible and what can be currently manufactured economically at scale. Even so, the potential is significant. Advances in molecular biology can allow developers to design materials around a particular use case rather than search for an existing material that is close enough.

There is also an important reality that anyone working with emerging materials has to acknowledge: Novelty does not guarantee adoption.

Manufacturers operate within established supply chains, equipment, standards and cost structures. A material can be scientifically impressive and still struggle commercially if it requires customers to redesign too much of what they already do. The technologies most likely to gain early traction will be those that combine meaningful performance advantages with a realistic path into existing markets.

That means thinking beyond the material itself. How will it be processed? Can it work with existing manufacturing equipment? Is supply reliable? Can quality be maintained? Does the cost make sense for the intended application? Those questions may sound less exciting than the underlying science, but they are ultimately what turn a promising technology into something useful.

Biology-enabled materials do not need to replace conventional fibers, polymers or composites to have a meaningful impact. Their importance may come from expanding the range of choices available to engineers and product designers.

Chemistry gave us synthetic materials that transformed entire industries. Advances in composites created new combinations of weight and strength. Biology now adds another set of tools. The most exciting outcome may not be recreating what already exists in nature, but rather using biological systems to develop materials with properties and applications we have not yet considered.

While the future of materials will certainly still involve chemistry, physics and traditional manufacturing, a new door is opening. Authored biology will supply options and opportunities. Those options are just now beginning to open up.​

#3101. 1 Overlooked Dividend Stock With a 54-Year Winning Streak Worth Buying Now

PepsiCo has raised dividends annually for decades.

It's not often that a stock that consistently raises dividends trades at a discount. But that's the case with PepsiCo (PEP +0.38%).

Despite the company's long dividend history, investors have been dismayed by the company's recent earnings. However, that creates a buying opportunity for investors looking for steadily rising dividends and potential capital appreciation.

Here's a closer examination of why PepsiCo's dividend-raising streak doesn't look likely to end anytime soon.

PepsiCo's dividend-raising streak has reached 54 years. There's a special group for companies that have increased payments for at least 50 straight years: Dividend Kings.

The board of directors hasn't been approving nominal, small increases, either. PepsiCo raised June's payout by 4% to $1.48 per share. This latest boost looks sustainable, based on the company's earnings. PepsiCo's payout ratio, or dividends compared to earnings, is 73%.

The shares also have a much higher dividend yield than the overall stock market, as measured by the S&P 500 index, which investors often use as a proxy for large-cap stocks. PepsiCo's stock yields 4.2% versus 1.1% for the index.

Consistently increasing dividends, secure payments, and a higher-than-market dividend yield sound good. Yet investors haven't been impressed with PepsiCo's results. This year, through Nov. 6, the share price dove 1.5%, vastly underperforming the S&P 500's 7.8% gain.

The company has struggled with product volume for a while due to a tough economic environment and competitive pressures. There has been some progress on that front, but it's been uneven.

Its fiscal third-quarter revenue (ended Sept. 5), adjusted to remove foreign-currency translation and acquisitions/divestitures, grew 3.1% year over year. However, higher volume only contributed 0.5 percentage points. Also, cost pressures weighed on the bottom line, with adjusted earnings per share (EPS) increasing just 1.5%.

For the year, management now expects EPS to grow 1% to 2%. That's down from its previous guidance of 4% to 6%.

However, PepsiCo should recoup higher costs over time. Notably, management has been focusing on product innovation to win back customers, add volume, and expand the company's margin. These changes take time, though. However, it's positive that the company is taking steps to adjust to consumer tastes.

Aside from the benefits of higher dividends and an attractive yield, the shares appear well-valued. The stock has a price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 18 compared to the 10-year median of 26. It also has a lower valuation than the S&P 500, which has a P/E multiple of 25.

The multiple should expand over time as PepsiCo's sales and earnings growth pick up. That creates a powerful opportunity to collect dividends and potentially produce outsize stock gains.

Lawrence Rothman, CFA has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3100. How AI Is Raising The Bar For SaaS Companies

Ed Frederici is Chief Technology Officer at Appfire, a leading enterprise collaboration software company.

AI has revived the debate about whether software as a service (SaaS) can maintain its market position. But the better question is whether SaaS companies can meet the higher bar customers are setting for differentiation, outcomes and value.​

Pressure on public SaaS valuations and questions about AI-driven disruption are pushing organizations to prove their value faster. According to a McKinsey survey from August 2026, nearly nine in 10 organizations use AI in at least one business function, though many are still experimenting or piloting rather than scaling it across the enterprise. ​

Together, these shifts are pressuring SaaS companies from multiple directions. Externally, buyers have more options and higher expectations. Internally, companies must operationalize AI responsibly, rethink how roles evolve and manage the cost of adding AI to products.

These pressures are connected, but buyers feel market changes first. They have more products to evaluate, more AI claims to vet and less patience for software that fails to prove its value. ​

That is why competition comes first. With the ability to generate code becoming cheaper, AI lowers barriers to entry for SaaS products. Differentiation therefore becomes harder and more important. In a crowded market, buyers have less patience for products that are difficult to buy, onboard, use or maintain.​

The strongest SaaS companies will stand out by understanding customer workflows and removing friction. That means solving specific problems more effectively than competitors, embedding governance, risk and compliance (GRC) and business outcomes directly into workflows and making the product experience simple enough to drive adoption.​

Competition can signal healthy demand, but the challenge is proving why your product deserves to win. That requires quality, compliance, flexibility and a product experience that fits naturally into how people work. ​

Winning customers is only the first step. As AI becomes part of more SaaS products, companies also have to help customers adopt those capabilities with confidence.​​

Even as AI tools become more available, adoption is moving at different speeds. According to a 2025 survey by DeskPro, the tech sector is seeing a 92% adoption rate in using AI for support operations, while adoption in regulated sectors sits at 58% as they face different risk considerations, workflows and trust requirements. When it comes to scaling AI across an organization, you’re only as fast as your slowest team. ​​

That uneven adoption makes scaling difficult. Organizations cannot rely on isolated AI experiments if they want AI to become part of everyday work. They need clear use cases, governance, data standards and a shared understanding of where AI should and should not be used.

Without that foundation, AI remains fragmented instead of becoming an enterprise capability.​​

While hands-on experience often drives adoption faster than policy, organizations still need guardrails. As AI becomes core business infrastructure, leaders must hold it to the same expectations for accuracy, security, performance and reliability as any critical system. Those expectations also reshape the features organizations need from the people building and managing AI-enabled products.​

As AI increasingly handles operational work such as coding, testing, documentation and analysis, people remain central, though their roles are shifting.​

Software engineers will need strong judgment in review, architecture, security and system-level work. If AI writes more implementation, humans need to evaluate whether the solution is safe, scalable, maintainable and aligned with organizational standards. AI-generated code should still be reviewed with the same rigor as other contributions.​

Entry-level roles may change as AI takes on basic tasks, and new roles will emerge to manage AI across the business. The chief AI officer is one example, but it only helps if the mandate is clear. A leadership seat created for optics can overlap with the CTO or CIO and slow progress.

The role needs a defined problem to own, clear authority and measurable ROI. The same expectation applies to AI investments. As organizations expand AI across products and operations, SaaS providers must find ways to help leaders demonstrate that their investments deliver measurable value.​

AI can create an advantage, but also introduce cost. That creates several financial pressures:

• Balancing Innovation With Cost Control: AI infrastructure, model usage and compute costs can increase the cost of goods sold and put pressure on margins.

• Prioritizing High-Impact Use Cases: Not every AI feature will drive meaningful differentiation. Companies should focus on areas that deliver clear customer and business value

• Aligning Pricing With Delivered Value: Customers may pay for AI-enabled capabilities, but only when the value is clear.

• Maintaining Visibility Into ROI: Leaders need to track which investments deliver results, and then adjust when they do not.

• Avoiding Overextension: If an AI feature increases operating costs without improving the product experience or business outcome, it becomes a margin problem.​

The investment-return gap was already clear in 2025 when 85% of organizations had increased AI investment over the prior year, according to Deloitte, and 91% planned to increase it again. However, only 6% reported payback in under a year. ​

For SaaS developers looking to help organizations navigate these headwinds, the best starting point is to choose the highest-value use cases, estimate costs up front and connect the investment to customer or business value.

Some AI features will drive differentiation. Others will add cost without changing the customer outcome. SaaS products should give leaders visibility into which is which.​

AI is making software easier to build, but harder to differentiate. Success will depend less on who ships the most AI features to who solves customer problems most effectively, earning trust across the enterprise and delivering measurable business value.

SaaS is not disappearing. The bar for winning has simply risen.

#3097. ServiceNow vs. Snowflake: Which Tech Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

Both software leaders are growing fast, but one is weighed down by fears of AI disruption and the other by a steep price tag.

Should you invest in the operational brain of the modern office or the central engine of its data? We look at ServiceNow (NOW +0.78%) and Snowflake (SNOW +5.53%) to see which is the better buy.

ServiceNow specializes in automating workflows and digitizing business processes, making life easier for employees. Snowflake provides a cloud-native platform for storing, sharing, and analyzing massive amounts of data. Both companies are high-growth leaders in their respective categories, but they follow very different business models and possess distinct financial profiles.

ServiceNow operates an AI platform that connects people and processes by automating manual tasks across departments such as IT, HR, and customer service. The company serves roughly 8,700 enterprise customers, including more than 600 clients that generate over $5 million in annual contract value. It has expanded its reach through strategic alliances with major tech companies.

On a trailing-12-month basis through the September quarter, revenue reached nearly $15.4 billion, representing growth of approximately 22% compared with the prior fiscal year. The company remains consistently profitable, reporting net income of close to $1.4 billion for the period. This resulted in a net margin of roughly 9.4%, demonstrating the ability to generate significant earnings while continuing to invest in its global expansion and AI integration.

As of its September 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.6x, indicating that its total debt is low relative to its shareholders' equity. The current ratio, which measures the ability to cover short-term bills, is roughly 0.7x.

TTM free cash flow was roughly $4.7 billion, though note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 35.9% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Snowflake provides the AI Data Cloud, a platform that allows organizations to break down data silos and collaborate across multiple cloud environments. The company has grown its customer base to over 13,328 accounts, including nearly 800 of the Forbes Global 2000. It relies on a deep network of partners, including global systems integrators such as Accenture and Infosys, to help customers implement and scale their data strategies.

TTM revenue through Snowflake's July quarter reached nearly $5.4 billion, a growth rate of approximately 32% year over year. Despite this rapid top-line expansion, the company reported a net loss of close to $1.1 billion over the same period. This resulted in a net margin of roughly -20%, as the business continues to prioritize customer acquisition and product development over immediate GAAP profitability.

As of its July 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is roughly 1.3x, calculated by dividing total debt by shareholders' equity. Its current ratio is approximately 0.9x, providing a healthy cushion to meet short-term obligations.

TTM free cash flow through July was nearly $1.2 billion, but note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 130.9% of operating cash flow, meaning this non-cash add-back heavily inflates cash generation.

ServiceNow faces intense competition from legacy and AI-native vendors, including Salesforce and Oracle, which may put pressure on pricing. The company also addresses the complexities of integrating AI technology while maintaining high levels of data security.

Furthermore, ServiceNow navigates significant government contracting hurdles and the constant challenge of attracting and retaining highly skilled AI talent in a tight labor market.

Snowflake faces significant competitive pressure from large public cloud providers, such as Alphabet and Microsoft, that both host its platform and offer competing data tools. Its consumption-based revenue model introduces volatility, making it difficult for investors to forecast quarterly results with precision.

ServiceNow appears significantly more affordable on a price basis, trading at a much lower multiple for both its sales and future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

In this comparison, the Forward P/E compares the share price to future earnings estimates, while the P/S ratio compares market cap to sales over the past twelve months.

Both companies have steadily grown revenue at high rates, despite competition in their respective market. However, I would buy ServiceNow for a few reasons.

ServiceNow stock is down over worries that agentic AI tools will reduce demand for its software. But subscription revenue growth has remained fairly steady throughout 2026, and management's guidance calls for 21% year-over-year growth in constant currency for the full year.

It appears to have a successful path to positioning itself as the crucial governance and security layer for agentic AI. Its backlog grew 21% year-over-year last quarter, indicating demand for its offering remains strong.

Snowflake is also remaining resilient despite concerns about competition in its market. But I prefer to buy ServiceNow because of its lower valuation. Snowflake trades at high multiples of earnings and sales, while ServiceNow trades at a more average multiple of forward earnings despite above-average revenue growth and margins. I believe ServiceNow is likely undervalued and can outperform the market, possibly in 2027 and in the coming years.

John Ballard has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Accenture Plc, Alphabet, Microsoft, Oracle, Salesforce, ServiceNow, and Snowflake. #3096. SaaS Isn’t Dead: AI Is Changing When It Makes Sense To Build Your Own Software

Irina Shymko | CEO at Langate EMEA | Introducing innovations and transforming business in Europe/America | Lecturer, mentor, advisor.

​For years, the software decision for most small and midsize businesses was straightforward: buy, don’t build. The economics favored SaaS. Building a custom system meant assembling developers, business analysts, QA engineers and other specialists, then investing months of work before knowing whether the product would deliver enough value. Paying a subscription for an existing platform was the rational alternative.

The compromise was that businesses had to adapt their processes to software designed for thousands of other companies. The important shift is not that AI can generate code faster. It is that the cost of turning a well-defined business process into working software is falling. For businesses with specialized workflows, this changes what’s worth building and how they invest in technology.

A common response to an operational problem is to search for better software. Consider a company where an employee receives a customer request, checks data in a CRM, copies information into another system, calculates it in Excel, requests approval, updates the CRM and generates a document.

The company might blame the CRM. But the CRM may not be the problem. The expensive part is the human coordination happening between systems. Instead of replacing an entire platform, an AI-assisted team could build a lightweight operational layer that retrieves the necessary data through APIs, applies business rules, triggers human approval for defined exceptions and updates the relevant systems. That is a much smaller engineering problem.

This suggests a practical rule: Don’t start by asking what software to build. Start by finding an expensive workflow. One useful way to evaluate candidates is to estimate their annual cost: (frequency x time per instance x loaded labor cost) + (frequency x error rate x cost per error) + cost of delays.

A 20-minute manual process performed twice a month probably does not justify development. The same process performed hundreds of times per week may. Technology investment should be compared with the cost of the current process, not with zero.

​AI can generate interfaces, application logic, tests, documentation and assist with integrations and debugging. What it cannot do is resolve ambiguity in an unclear business process.

For example, a developer needs to know what should happen when an order reaches a particular status, which values to retrieve, when an approval is required and what actions to take next. AI can help translate those requirements into working software, but someone must first define the rules and exceptions.

This creates a new development equation: domain knowledge, technical literacy, AI acceleration and engineering judgment. A domain expert does not need to become a cloud architect, but basic technical literacy can make AI-assisted development far more effective. Engineering expertise remains essential as software moves toward production, particularly when security, sensitive data, complex integrations, reliability and scale enter the equation.​

AI is lowering not only development costs, but also the cost of testing whether an idea is worth building. Consider a midsize company evaluating employee benefits software. After researching the market, shortlisting two SaaS products and mapping them against its workflows, the team faced a familiar problem: another tool with per-user costs that would grow with the company, while about 5% of its functionality was needed.

Instead, the CTO translated the HR requirements into a focused internal system and built the first working version in three days using AI-assisted development. After two weeks of testing, the team could identify gaps, refine the system and prepare it for employee use.

The lesson is not to replace SaaS by default. It is that AI can make testing the alternative inexpensive enough to factor into the buy-versus-build decision, and provide evidence before a larger investment.

Cheaper development creates another risk: building what you should have bought. Most companies do not need proprietary authentication, cloud storage, email delivery or payment infrastructure. Mature services solve these problems. A more efficient architecture often combines them.

Buy the commodity. Build the differentiation. Connect the rest. For a logistics business, differentiation might be exception-handling logic. For a specialty healthcare organization, it might be a specific operational workflow. For a manufacturer, it could be quoting or production-planning logic.

APIs and managed cloud services can provide the commodity infrastructure while a smaller custom layer implements the business-specific logic. This is where AI can create disproportionate value for smaller companies, not by rebuilding their entire technology stack, but by making the unique 10% economically feasible to build.

Custom software still requires updates, security monitoring, infrastructure management and someone accountable for its long-term reliability. AI can reduce some of that effort, but it does not eliminate it. The business case should therefore compare the total cost of ownership, not just the initial build cost, with the long-term cost and constraints of SaaS.

For leaders considering this approach, the investment process can be simple:

1. Identify one repetitive, expensive workflow.

2. Quantify its current cost, including human effort, errors and delays.

3. Define the desired process independently of current software limitations.

4. Reduce the scope to the smallest version capable of testing the hypothesis.

5. Use AI-assisted development to shorten the build-test-learn cycle.

6. Measure adoption, time saved, errors reduced and business impact before expanding.

The result may be another SaaS purchase. It may be an integration between existing systems. Or it may justify building proprietary software. The point is not to build more. It is to make smaller technology bets and obtain evidence sooner.

SaaS became dominant partly because buying software was convenient while building it was expensive. AI does not make SaaS obsolete. But it is weakening the assumption that adapting the business to existing software will always be cheaper than adapting software to the business. For some small and specialized companies, that may fundamentally change where the next technology dollar should go.​

#3094. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#3093. Anthropic Just Took a Big Step Toward Its Public Debut. Here's What You Need to Know.

The Claude developer finally set a price range for its IPO, and it's lower than many investors expected.

Anthropic, the company behind the Claude family of AI models, has been one of the most anticipated IPOs in years. There has been speculation that it could break records set earlier this year by SpaceX (SPCX +5.81%) for valuation, the amount of money raised in the offering, or both.

Investors finally got some of the details they've been waiting for. Anthropic's latest draft prospectus indicates that the company and some of its existing investors plan to sell 825 million shares at a range of $70 to $78 per share.

Here's what the price range tells us about Anthropic's valuation, when the stock could start trading, and what else it means for investors.

At $70 to $78 a share, the IPO would raise $57.75 billion to $64.35 billion. It's worth noting that not all of that money goes to Anthropic, as 23.65 million shares (roughly 3% of the total) are being sold by existing shareholders.

Anthropic also lined up a separate $26 billion private placement with seven tech heavyweights, including Nvidia (NVDA -4.27%), Alphabet's (GOOGL +1.18%) Google, Microsoft (MSFT +2.75%), and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (TSM +3.51%). Including the placement, Anthropic could bring in as much as $90.4 billion. This would surpass the roughly $85.7 billion SpaceX raised.

It's also worth noting that this range is already a revision. A day earlier, a version of the filing sent to bankers showed about 867 million shares at $70 to $80.

Plus, underwriters will have the option to buy up to 123.75 million additional shares, which could add nearly $10 billion to the amount raised, based on the high end of the price range.

Based on about 16.9 billion fully diluted shares implied by Anthropic's amended filing, the range works out to roughly $1.19 trillion to $1.32 trillion. Once you add the new IPO shares and the shares going to the cornerstone investors, my estimate rises to about $1.27 trillion to $1.41 trillion.

For context, Anthropic was valued at $965 billion in its private funding round in May, so the IPO range is about 32% to 46% higher. But it's well below the $1.5 trillion to $2 trillion valuation reported by The Wall Street Journal in October, before the tech sell-off that followed the midterm elections cooled investors' appetite. SpaceX's IPO valued that company at more than $1.7 trillion, so as things stand now, Anthropic isn't likely to set a new valuation record.

One thing we can do after the latest version of Anthropic's S-1 is to start calculating some valuation metrics to help determine how cheap or expensive the stock could be.

The price-to-sales (P/S) ratio compares a company's market value with its revenue, and it's one of the most useful yardsticks for a company that isn't consistently profitable yet. Anthropic generated $17.0 billion of revenue in the third quarter, up 47% from the second quarter. Annualize that, and you get about $68 billion, which puts Anthropic at roughly 19 to 21 times sales at the IPO range.

That's expensive by most standards, but not outrageous for a company growing this fast. Anthropic's revenue in the first half of 2026 was $16.3 billion, up from $1.2 billion in the same period a year earlier. If it keeps growing anywhere near that pace, today's multiple will look much lower in hindsight.

Of course, growth like this rarely lasts. Anthropic also swung back to a $567 million operating loss in the third quarter, after reporting its first operating profit in the second quarter.

Anthropic's roadshow, the series of meetings where management pitches the stock to big institutional investors, is underway this week. Shares could start trading on the Nasdaq as soon as late next week, according to multiple sources.

We don't know the exact date yet. But companies usually publish a price range when the roadshow begins, roughly one to two weeks before the IPO prices. The final price is generally set the evening before the first trade, and it can come in above, within, or below the range, depending on demand. Anthropic has already trimmed its range once, so I wouldn't treat $70-$78 as final.

A few other things stand out to me. The cornerstone investors are also among Anthropic's largest suppliers and partners, signaling confidence in the company but potentially deepening conflicts of interest. Anthropic's IPO will also be the first major test of AI valuations since the recent tech sell-off, so its reception could set the tone for other AI companies hoping to go public. And Alphabet and Amazon (AMZN +2.94%) both already own stakes in Anthropic, which will soon have a public market price.

Having said all that, the price range gives investors something concrete to evaluate for the first time. At roughly 20 times annualized sales, Anthropic isn't cheap, but its valuation is more reasonable than the $2 trillion figure that was floating around just a few weeks ago.

It's also worth noting that the actual IPO price doesn't necessarily have to fall in this range, especially if interest is high. It's not uncommon for popular IPOs to end up pricing shares above the high end of the stated range.

Matt Frankel, CFP® has positions in Amazon. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Nvidia, Space Exploration Technologies, and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3090. AMD vs. Intel: Which Is the Better Artificial Intelligence (AI) Chip Stock to Buy Right Now?

Both companies have been experiencing solid growth in their data center businesses, but one of them seems like the better bet.

Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +13.26%) and Intel (INTC +5.49%) have benefited from healthy demand for their artificial intelligence (AI)-oriented chips, which are being deployed by major hyperscalers in data centers to handle both training and inference workloads.

Not surprisingly, both semiconductor stocks have delivered healthy gains to investors this year, despite the brutal sell-off they have experienced lately. While AMD shares have jumped 137% in 2026, Intel has clocked respectable gains of 60% as of this writing. However, their latest results clearly indicate that one of them is stealing a march over the other in the AI chip market.

We will examine AMD's and Intel's latest results and determine which of these two chipmakers is the better buy right now.

Intel's revenue from its data center and AI (DCAI) segment increased by 78% year over year in Q3 to $7.3 billion. What's worth noting is that the company's DCAI revenue growth has been accelerating in recent quarters.

However, AMD trumped Intel with a much stronger year-over-year increase of 97% in its data center segment to $8.5 billion in the previous quarter. So, AMD is now outselling Intel in the data center segment, and that's not surprising. After all, AMD sells both server central processing units (CPUs) and graphics processing units (GPUs). At the same time, Intel seems more inclined toward server CPUs after its Gaudi series of data center GPUs failed to find much favor among customers.

Looking ahead, AMD expects stronger growth in data centers, driven by the increasing adoption of its latest MI455X graphics cards, which it has begun shipping in volume to customers, as well as its Epyc server CPUs. AMD CEO Lisa Su remarked on the latest earnings call:

Meanwhile, Intel management noted on the latest earnings call that it expects server CPU industry shipments to increase by more than 30% in 2026 and 2027. AMD, therefore, is growing significantly faster than the end market, indicating it is taking market share from Intel.

Moreover, AMD's guidance clearly suggests that it is a better investment compared to Intel stock. The company anticipates a 66% year-over-year increase in revenue in the current quarter to $17 billion, at the midpoint of its guidance range. On the other hand, Intel's $15.5 billion revenue guidance points to a potential jump of just 13% from the year-ago period.

Even better, AMD is projecting revenue growth of more than 80% in 2027, which is more than 2x the company's long-term growth forecast. As such, it is evident that AMD is the better semiconductor stock to buy right now, especially given that it is cheaper than Intel on a valuation basis.

AMD stock trades at 30 times forward earnings, according to Morningstar. Intel is slightly more expensive at 35 times forward earnings. However, AMD's earnings per share (EPS) are projected to increase by 118% in 2027, well above Intel's estimated EPS growth rate of 20%.

Also, analysts have become significantly bullish about AMD's long-term EPS growth potential following its latest quarterly report.

INTC EPS LT Growth Estimates data by YCharts

The chart above indicates that even Intel's EPS could increase impressively over the long run, but AMD's lower valuation makes it the better buy. So, investors looking to buy one of these two AI stocks right now have an easy choice to make following the recent sell-off in the semiconductor sector.

#3089. Why Investigational Treatments Depend On Access, Not Just Breakthroughs

Dr. Michel van Harten, CEO of myTomorrows, helping patients discover and access treatments.

Picture this: A cancer therapy is in development, with a clinical trial actively enrolling patients who could benefit from it. But often the patients who need it most never hear about it or can’t navigate the system to get to it.​

An estimated 70% of Americans say they’re inclined to participate in clinical trials, yet fewer than 5% of adults living with cancer ever do. The gap between willingness and participation has nothing to do with science and everything to do with access.

What does it mean to innovate if the people you built something for can’t reach it? In clinical research, where the cost of inaction runs highest, that question deserves more attention than it gets.

The dominant narrative around medical progress celebrates the breakthrough, the lab discovery, the Phase III result and the FDA approval. The harder, less examined frontier is access to treatments still under investigation. Science produces breakthroughs. Infrastructure creates access to them. Access is what drives impact.

Certain advances in genomics, precision medicine and AI are increasing the number of investigational therapies reaching clinical development.​ Yet if patients cannot find, understand and navigate these opportunities, science risks outpacing the systems meant to connect it with the people it intends to serve.

The problem becomes visible at scale when looking at clinical trials. More than 80% of trials worldwide fail to enroll on schedule, with 55% of terminated trials citing low patient accrual as the leading cause of closure. The gap between “a trial exists” and “the right patient can reach it” is where the most consequential but least addressed work lies. Every month an eligible patient fails to reach an investigational treatment represents lost learning for researchers, slower development for sponsors and a missed opportunity for the patient.

Geography is one reason. Research shows 78 major cancer centers are involved in 94% of all U.S. cancer trials. Those centers tend to sit in socioeconomically advantaged areas with higher proportions of white residents. For patients living hours away, an enrolling trial can stay out of reach.

Racial and ethnic disparities compound the gap. In the trials behind the FDA’s 2025 novel drug approvals, Black participants enrolled at lower rates than white or Asian participants. In some drug programs, they made up 0% of those enrolled. These disparities reflect access failures, not scientific ones.

Four barriers consistently keep patients from accessing pre-approval treatments.

A trial cannot help anyone who doesn’t know it exists or understand what it involves. The European Health Literacy Survey found that almost half of respondents across Europe, a region with some of the world’s most developed healthcare systems, had limited health literacy.

This excludes more patients than many would assume. Knowing a trial exists is only the first step. Reaching it means navigating fragmented clinical trial databases, complicated eligibility criteria and referral pathways that vary by institution. That complexity can shut out patients as effectively as cost or geography.

This gap has roots that reach back decades. From 1932 to 1972, the U.S. Public Health Service’s Tuskegee Syphilis Study tracked untreated syphilis in hundreds of Black men, withholding treatment even after penicillin became a standard cure in the 1940s. That history correlates with lasting increases in medical mistrust and mortality within the same community.

Mistrust remains a key barrier to research participation among many Black patients, sustained by ongoing inequities and discriminatory encounters. When an institution with a history of failing a population delivers an innovation without involving that community, the innovation predictably fails to reach the people who may need it most.

Even patients who are informed, motivated and trusting still face hurdles relating to geography, cost, insurance, language, transportation and fragmented data systems. The early years of the HIV epidemic show how these barriers can be moved.

Many people with AIDS could not get into clinical trials, which were the main route to investigational drugs. In October 1988, around 1,500 ACT UP activists protested at FDA headquarters to demand faster research and approval of AIDS treatments. The following year, federal health officials accepted a “parallel track” approach, making innovative drugs available to patients who could not join trials without disrupting the trials themselves.

An access problem is a failure of the connective tissue between an innovation and the population it was designed to serve. Building that connective tissue means investing in five areas at once. These are not abstract categories. Each reflects a breakdown many health professionals have seen keep a treatment from the person who needed it.

1. Tools for discovery help people find and reach relevant investigational treatments and trials, instead of just learning they exist.

2. Trusted communication builds credibility early by involving intended users in shaping solutions and reaching them through the community institutions and peers they already rely on.​

3. Health literacy support, embedded across schools, clinics, hospitals and community centers, gives patients what they need to make informed decisions about their own care.

4. Reduced system fragmentation cuts the number of platforms, touchpoints and decision points a patient must navigate alone.

5. Dedicated patient navigators do the same, turning a complex system into something a person can find their way through, especially when they speak the patient’s language.

None of these fixes calls for a scientific advance—only a willingness to treat access as seriously as discovery.

Access should be a prerequisite for scientific progress, not an afterthought. Every investigational treatment that reaches the person who needs it is progress made real. That is the work worth measuring.

The next era of medical innovation will be defined less by the therapies we discover and more by how well we connect them with patients who need them. Access is not downstream of innovation. It is what allows innovation to matter.​​

#3087. Are These 6 Small-Cap ETFs Worth a Look Right Now?

Small-caps have made an impressive comeback over the past year. The fundamental picture suggests the rally can continue.

Ever since large-cap tech stocks starting melting down in October, small-caps have become outperformers again. And for good reason. Their combination of accelerating earnings growth, reasonable valuations, and fewer questions around artificial intelligence (AI) capex spending make this an intriguing entry point for this group.

There are dozens of ETFs focused on small-caps but I want to look at the major ones here:

The biggest difference that matters most in this category is the one between the iShares Russell 2000 ETF and the iShares Core S&P Small-Cap ETF.

The iShares Russell 2000 ETF holds essentially all stocks, profitable and unprofitable, within its target universe. The S&P 600 index, which the iShares Core S&P Small-Cap ETF tracks, has a screen that requires companies to be profitable in order to be included. That means it has a quality tilt that the others don't necessarily have.

The other two are built a little differently. The Vanguard Morningstar Small-Cap ETF has a median market cap of $10.7 billion, giving it a distinct larger company tilt. The Schwab U.S. Small-Cap ETF has a more modest median market cap of around $5 billion, making it more representative of what investors might more likely consider to be small-caps.

All four have a case for use as a core small-cap position. I like the iShares Core S&P Small-Cap ETF because of its quality tilt and I think that's a good strategy when shopping in this space. A large percentage of small-cap companies are unprofitable and that can lead to deeper drawdowns in bear markets.

The small-cap value category has become interesting lately. Normally, this is the segment where quality is an issue, where cheap stocks are cheap for a reason. But despite the issues with the Iran war, high inflation, and high interest rates, they've been staging an impressive rally.

Over the past year, the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +2.35%) is up roughly 15%. But the iShares Russell 2000 Value ETF and the Vanguard Morningstar Small-Cap Value ETF are up 27% and 24%, respectively.

They both buy cheaper small-cap stocks but use different methods for getting there.

The iShares ETF only uses the price/book (P/B) ratio to select stocks, has a high 29% allocation to financial stocks, and a forward price/earnings (P/E) ratio of around 15. The Vanguard ETF uses multiple traditional valuation metrics to target stocks, has roughly half of its portfolio in a combination of industrials, financials, and consumer discretionary stocks, and a P/E ratio of around 16.

The Vanguard Morningstar Small-Cap Value ETF would be considered the more diverse of the two portfolios, but the iShares Russell 2000 Value ETF is more of a pure small-cap play.

In terms of short-term potential, I think the broad small-cap ETFs have a better case than the small-cap value ETFs.

The expected earnings acceleration over the next two years should be a strong catalyst for a further rally. I'd expect broader exposure and its heavier growth tilt over value will potentially perform better.

Over the longer-term, I still like the iShares Core S&P Small-Cap ETF and its link to the S&P SmallCap 600 index. I think the profitability requirement when investing in small-caps is important and helps weed out some of the junk that may not survive long-term. Over time, quality tends to win and I think that's the best way to approach this category.

David Dierking has positions in Schwab Strategic Trust-Schwab U.s. Small-Cap ETF. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends iShares Core S&P Small-Cap ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3085. NerdWallet: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

NerdWallet's spending on long-term AI and software projects will weigh on near-term profits, a trade-off investors should balance against its cash generation.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, NerdWallet (NRDS -0.89%) reported total revenue of $240 million. Among major takeaways, lenders and insurance carriers are adjusting partner budgets, forcing the financial marketplace to diversify past traditional search traffic. Three things management said stand out.

A $17 million drop in auto insurance revenue pushed the company's third-quarter performance below its guidance range. The marketplace relies on a narrow carrier network, which limits its ability to capitalize on broader industry demand.

Changes in search engine algorithms have reduced reader traffic to educational content over the past three years. Search engine optimization (SEO), the process of attracting unpaid search engine traffic, accounts for a smaller share of total revenue today, while transactional marketplaces remain more insulated.

Management is prioritizing internal software and artificial intelligence (AI) tools to assist in-house agents. These multiyear projects reduce near-term non-GAAP operating income, an adjusted metric for operating profit that excludes certain stock compensation costs, but aim to improve user retention.

Cash flow generation provides the company with the resources to balance share repurchases against strategic product investments. Management is focusing on carrier partner expansion and marketing discipline across channels to broaden its audience reach.

Long-term value creation depends on transforming search traffic into repeat user relationships across personal finance decisions.

#3084. 18 Outdated Identity And Access Management Assumptions To Retire

At its core, identity and access management helps companies control who—or what—can access their systems and data. But as businesses adopt new technologies and authentication methods, keeping that access secure without making everyday work unnecessarily difficult is becoming more complicated.

The challenge isn’t just keeping up with new security technologies; it’s recognizing when familiar practices and long-held beliefs may be creating vulnerabilities. Below, members of Forbes Technology Council discuss outdated identity and access management assumptions tech leaders should reconsider as digital identities and authentication continue to evolve.

Retire the idea that verifying someone once at sign-up is enough. Fraudsters are finding ways to take over established, verified accounts and exploit the trust those accounts carry. A one-time “yes” shouldn’t mean lifetime trust. Verification needs to be continuous, combining device and network signals, behavioral biometrics, and risk-based orchestration to assess risk over time and ensure trust extends beyond the initial onboarding moment. - Kaarel Kotkas, Veriff

We need to retire the assumption that strong authentication alone secures identity. As machines and AI agents gain access, organizations must limit privileges, detect unauthorized changes and restore trusted identities and access policies after an attack. Identity resilience keeps businesses running and enables rapid recovery when preventive controls fail. - Robert Bobel, Cayosoft

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

The assumption that more complex authentication always means stronger security is a myth. Every unnecessary login, password reset or access hurdle introduces friction for users, costing organizations productivity while encouraging risky workarounds. Security that employees routinely circumvent isn’t effective security. The goal should be to make secure access the easiest path. - Fran Rosch, Imprivata

Retire the assumption that identities are human. Non-human identities, from service accounts to AI agents, now outnumber employees and often hold standing, overprivileged access with no clear owner. Treat every NHI as a governed identity: Assign ownership, enforce least privilege and review access continuously. - Damon Tompkins, Pathlock

Giving a service its own identity addressed the assumption that identities are human, but that is no longer enough. An orchestrator, several sub-agents and ordinary application code can now act from inside the same process using the same credentials. The question is not whether the caller is authenticated. It is which of the things inside it decided to make the call. - Aviv Mussinger, Kodem

Static trust is the identity assumption leaders should kill. In e-commerce, a customer browsing from a known device may pass frictionlessly, but a $5,000 refund, new device or delivery address change should trigger stronger verification. Risk-based access adjusts authentication to context and consequence. The payoff is lower fraud without sacrificing conversion. Adaptive identity is now part of enterprise resilience. - Sumit Srivastava

Retire the assumption that the identity provider is always available. If it fails, staff may be locked out of incident response, payroll or customer systems just when they are needed most. Define a separate emergency path for a few critical actions, keep it isolated and monitored, and rehearse its use and shutdown. Resilience means proving both that ordinary access stops and that recovery remains possible. Test emergency access with the primary identity service offline. - Jagadish Gokavarapu, Wissen Infotech

The assumption technology leaders should retire is that authentication establishes durable trust. Identity and access management is becoming as much a governance discipline as a security one. Organizations need clear boundaries around what people, applications and agents are permitted to do, as well as the ability to ensure those boundaries continue to hold as conditions change. - Jay Bavisi, EC-Council

Retire the assumption that an agent should inherit the permissions of the person it acts for. I can delete every entry on my calendar and pull salary data from our HR system. An agent acting for me should start with zero access, with its own identity and limits, until someone deliberately decides what it may do. That way, when something breaks, we know which agent did it and who is accountable. - Linus Hakansson, Gravitee

Who can access what is now a business risk decision, the same as financial controls. When a single stolen login can stop operations or expose customer data, it belongs on the leadership agenda. CEOs should know who owns identity in their company, how often access is reviewed, and how quickly it can be shut off when something goes wrong. - Asad Khan, TestMu AI

One outdated assumption is that administrators usually need standing privileges. Privileges are not always needed and are not needed for every command. Permanent admin rights or access to service accounts are exactly what attackers hunt for. Just-in-time access, granted for a specific task and expiring automatically, makes a stolen admin account worth far less. If someone holds the keys to the kingdom all the time, 24/7, “just in case,” that convenience is being paid for with risk. - Dan Sorensen, Nexus Security Advisors

As video and voice cloning rise, tech leaders must retire the assumption that they can verify identity over the phone or through a video conference. Instead, we have to start relying increasingly on technical signals that users are the persons they claim to be unless they are standing right in front of us. - Kevin Korte, Univention

The next access management question isn’t “who”; it’s “why.” An AI agent can be fully entitled to a resource and still be doing something it shouldn’t. Behavior is a signal, but it only shows what already happened. Intent is the purpose behind the action. Instead of asking, “Can this agent access this?” leaders should ask, “What is this agent trying to accomplish, and should that happen?” Access should not be given without understanding intent. - Sumit Dhawan, Proofpoint

The main assumption to rethink is how we manage credentials. The old-school practice was to generate credentials, which were rotated occasionally or stored in a vault. That approach assumed credentials would be used only occasionally and would remain largely static. Agents require us to rethink how we authenticate and authorize activity: It needs to be on a per-use basis. That means credentials must be generated just in time and/or there must be enforcement scoped on a per-action basis. - Alex Bovee, C1

Leaders must retire the assumption that device or network verification signals user intent. Possessing a registered device or passing a biometric check does not guarantee a human explicitly authorized the action, especially with the rapid rise of session hijacking and AI-driven prompt injection. Cybersecurity and identity management processes must evolve to continuously verify contextual user intent at the transaction level. - Mark Brown, The Mark of Security Ltd.

Retire the assumption that disabling an employee’s login closes all access they set up. Include service accounts and AI agents in offboarding, reassign ownership, and revoke credentials the employee could retain so you do not leave machine access under a former employee’s control. - Kayode Faturoti, Breet

Assuming traditional multifactor authentication methods like SMS codes or push notifications guarantee safety is a dangerous misconception in modern security. Adversary-in-the-middle and push-fatigue tactics routinely bypass these basic controls to capture session tokens. To truly lower account takeover risk, organizations must eliminate shared secrets entirely and transition to domain-bound, phishing-resistant standards like FIDO2 and passkeys. - Neil Lampton, TIAG

Retire the idea that one login proves identity for an entire session. Access should be continuously evaluated using context, device trust and behavior. Authentication is becoming an ongoing process, not a one-time checkpoint at the front door. - Michael Gargiulo, VPN.com

#3082. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.
