#3077. Social Security Faces a 22% Cut in 2032, and Both Parties Are Eyeing the $184,500 Cap

It may not be the best or most popular idea, but it's a simple one that could be implemented with relative ease.

Most investors know all too well that the United States' Social Security program is under serious strain. If nothing changes, the program's trustees believe a 22% reduction in its current retirement benefit payments will be required come 2032. This effectively gives Congress five years to fix the problem. Of course, each passing year of inaction makes the problem more challenging to solve.

Several solutions have been suggested by both sides of the political aisle, including Representative John Larson's and Senator Richard Blumenthal's Social Security 2100 Act, Representative Gwen Moore's Social Security Enhancement and Protection Act of 2025 (H.R. 3517), and the bipartisan bill co-authored by Senators Elizabeth Warren and Bernie Moreno. All three proposals are fairly similar in that they simply call for raising the limit on the amount of wage-based income subject to Social Security tax to fund the program.

It's an idea that continues to resurface not necessarily because it's the one that makes the most fiscal sense, but because it's the one that would be the easiest to turn into law and then implement.

If you currently have a job, then you already know Social Security taxes take a sizable bite out of your paycheck. Indeed, 6.2% of all of your wages are passed along to the Social Security Administration, while your employer pays an additional 6.2% of your pay into the program that you never see. The more you pay in, the more you get back when you claim benefits.

There's a limit to how much of your work-based taxable income is subject to Social Security taxation, however. Although you pay an ever-growing amount of income tax the more wages you earn, for this year, the government-run entitlement program only taxes you up to your first $184,500 (and $192,300 next year). Any income earned above and beyond that amount isn't taxed by the program.

There's a reason, of course. Just as there's a cap on the amount of work-based wages that are taxed for Social Security purposes, there's a limit to how much of a benefit recipients can receive. This year's maximum possible payments are $5,181 per month, limited by the amount of money these beneficiaries contributed to the program during their working years.

This is where most proposals to shore up Social Security's impending funding shortfall are aiming. By raising or outright eliminating the taxable cap, the country's highest earners will supply the program's trust fund with considerably more cash than it currently receives.

How much more isn't exactly clear, since reducing higher-earning individuals' incomes could affect the economy's overall health. A couple of credible estimates are surprisingly close to one another, though, underscoring the argument that these numbers are in the right ballpark. The Tax Policy Center says that simply eliminating the taxable income ceiling could increase receipts by a total of $2.5 trillion over the course of the next 10 years, while Tax Foundation pegs the figure closer to $3.2 trillion for the same time frame.

Those are huge numbers, to be sure. But they may still not be large enough. For perspective, the Social Security Administration disbursed $1.36 trillion in retirement benefits alone in 2025, according to USAFacts, drawing on $1.25 trillion in receipts and a shrinking reserve base that stood at $2.34 trillion as of the end of last year. Even the Tax Policy Center and the Tax Foundation agree that this approach to generating more income for the program wouldn't actually solve Social Security's solvency problem in the long run, although it could -- and likely would -- harm the domestic economy.

Still, the proposed legislation to raise or eliminate the taxable income cap could be a smart starting point, if only to force deeper discussions of what's really wrong here and what else needs to happen to fix these problems.

In this vein, while it's not quite as action-driven and immediate as the aforementioned proposed legislation, the bipartisan PROMISE Act (S. 4979) would require the Social Security Advisory Board to come up with a true solvency plan. Perhaps it or something like it might be a more effective means of driving necessary change.

Of course, with political gridlock likely in Washington, D.C., for at least the next two years, it remains to be seen what -- if anything -- will actually get done anytime soon.

The biggest takeaway for investors may simply be to recognize that you can't exactly count on Washington to get enough done on this front to matter in the near or distant future. You remain best served by saving as much as you can and building your own retirement nest egg, so you're less reliant on the government-run entitlement program that may or may not be fixed anytime soon.

The good news is, your returns on your own savings will likely be far better than those you'd be getting with your compulsory contributions to Social Security. The program effectively achieves only an average yearly return of 2% to 6% on your money, or 2% to 3% more than any given year's rate of inflation. Investors can do better than that in the stock market, as long as they are patient and properly diversified.

#3075. AI’s Last-Mile Problem: Why Working AI Tools Don’t Always Deliver Business Value

At one point in an AI project, everyone gets excited. The team has tested the idea, the results are promising and the conversation turns to what it could mean beyond the group that built it. But expanding a controlled test across the business raises new questions about workflows, ownership and what needs to change.

Technology rarely slows progress. The harder questions emerge after the proof of concept: Who owns the process? How does the solution fit into existing workflows? How will the organization maintain it as the technology changes?

These questions are part of what is being called the last mile of AI. The organization has proven that something can work. The challenge is making it work consistently enough, and broadly enough, to become part of how the business operates.

Consider a customer service team that tests AI to summarize interactions and identify follow-up actions. During the pilot, a few employees use the tool successfully, and the team sees that it can reduce the time spent documenting calls.

That result matters, but scaling the idea requires different decisions. How does the summary enter the customer record? Who reviews it? What happens when the output is incomplete or incorrect? Is the workflow still efficient when the entire team uses it? How will the organization measure whether the time savings continue after the initial enthusiasm fades?

Moving from a promising experiment to something the business can rely on means figuring out how it fits into how people actually work, who needs to be involved and what has to change for it to work beyond the pilot.​

One reason AI initiatives struggle to move beyond experimentation is that organizations often add AI to existing processes without rethinking them.

A finance team might use AI to summarize a monthly report but still require the same manual steps, reviews and handoffs as before. A sales team might use AI to draft proposals but still route every proposal through a workflow designed for a fully manual process.

In those situations, the technology may be working exactly as intended while the business captures only a fraction of its potential value.

Moving through the last mile often requires asking a more fundamental question: If this capability is now available, should the process itself work differently? That does not mean rebuilding an entire function around AI. It means identifying where AI changes the work sequence, removes unnecessary steps or lets people spend more time on judgment-based decisions.

The most valuable implementation creates a better way of working by connecting AI to the surrounding process.

Ownership becomes especially important when an AI initiative moves from experimentation into daily operations.

During a pilot, responsibility often sits naturally with the team that created it. Once the initiative becomes part of a broader workflow, that ownership can become less clear. Technology may own the platform. A department may own the process. Employees may be responsible for reviewing outputs. Leadership may be responsible for the business result.

Without clear accountability, small issues can quickly become organizational problems.

A good implementation defines ownership before the solution scales. Someone should be accountable for the business outcome, someone should understand how the workflow is maintained and employees should know where human judgment remains necessary. Clear responsibilities help teams address problems while keeping the initiative focused on its intended outcome.

This is also where measurement becomes more useful than enthusiasm. If an AI initiative is expected to reduce processing time, improve response rates, reduce errors or increase capacity, someone should be responsible for knowing whether that result continues after implementation. AI doesn’t create lasting value just because employees use it. The organization has to own what the use is supposed to accomplish and have a way to determine whether it is actually delivering that value.

An AI solution can perform well in isolation and still struggle inside the business.

A tool that produces a useful output in a standalone environment may create little value if employees have to move information manually between systems, duplicate work or maintain another process to use it.

That is why the last mile often has more to do with integration than invention. The question is no longer just whether AI can produce a useful result. It is whether that result can become part of the systems and workflows employees already rely on.

For example, an AI system that identifies important information from customer conversations becomes far more useful when that information can flow into the systems where sales, service or operations teams already work. The goal is to make the improved workflow part of how work already gets done, with technology supporting the process rather than creating another layer employees have to manage.

The closer AI is to the actual operating process, the more likely its value is to persist.

Organizations often talk about scaling AI as though it means giving more employees access to a successful tool. In practice, scaling is a much broader operational decision.

Before expanding an AI initiative, leaders should understand whether the process is repeatable, ownership is clear, the necessary systems are connected and the organization can keep measuring the intended outcome. They also need to account for change. AI capabilities will evolve, models will improve, vendors will change their products and a workflow that works today may need adjustment six months from now.

That is why successful AI implementation requires more than proving that a technology works. It requires building an operating model that can adapt as the technology changes, while keeping the original business objective at the center of those decisions.​

#3063. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3061. Nvidia and Broadcom Are Scaling AI Data Centers With This Growth Stock. Here's Why It Could Soar.

The AI trade is suffering, but don't make the mistake of overlooking Alphabet's long-term growth trends.

Nvidia (NVDA -4.27%) and Broadcom (AVGO +8.78%) are two leading semiconductor companies that are benefiting immensely from the artificial intelligence infrastructure boom. Both companies have seen their sales and earnings skyrocket over the past several years, and their prices have followed suit, with Nvidia up 303% and Broadcom gaining 230% over the past three years.

And part of their success has come from selling processors to Alphabet (GOOGL +1.18%) (GOOG +0.73%) as the company expands its AI cloud computing footprint.

Here's how these three companies are intertwined, and why Alphabet's stock could soar amid rising cloud computing revenue and the development of its own processors.

Alphabet is spending a lot of money -- between $195 billion and $205 billion this year -- to expand its AI infrastructure footprint. And some of that money flows directly to Nvidia and Broadcom.

Alphabet is an important Nvidia customer, buying up some of the company's GPUs for its data centers and then using those processors to rent out AI compute capacity through its Google Cloud services.

Nvidia doesn't disclose its largest customers, but Alphabet is likely a top customer, and its GPU purchases are estimated to account for double-digit percentages of Nvidia's sales.

Alphabet's deals with Broadcom are more direct. Alphabet co-designs its own AI Tensor Processing Units with Broadcom for use in some of its data centers and to sell to customers. The two companies recently extended their TPU partnership through 2031, and are in the middle of a major deal worth about $21 billion to sell their co-designed TPUs to Anthropic.

While developing TPUs with Broadcom puts Alphabet somewhat in competition with Nvidia, the current scale of processor usage is far below Nvidia's dominant position and could take years to have any significant impact on Nvidia, if at all.

Alphabet recently reported its third-quarter 2026 results (which ended Sept. 30), and investors were not happy. Its share price fell immediately after the results were published and wiped out $500 billion of the company's value.

Alphabet shareholders are worried that the company's immense spending on AI won't pay off in the long run, and have latched onto the fact that free cash flow was $9.1 billion negative in the quarter. They were rightly concerned about that, but it's worth mentioning that $5.2 billion of that negative free cash flow came from a one-time European Commission fine.

Importantly, investors overlooked one of Alphabet's most important growth stories: Google Cloud's soaring revenue. Google Cloud sales more than doubled in the third quarter to $33.7 billion. That's a good indicator that Alphabet's AI spending is being put to good use. This segment is also highly profitable, generating $11.1 billion in operating profit with operating margins of 36%.

What's more, Google Cloud has a massive $561 billion backlog, half of which Alphabet's management said will be realized as revenue over the next two years.

All of this is important in the context of Alphabet's growth story because AI cloud services are rapidly becoming must-have products among all tech companies and many businesses. Goldman Sachs estimates that global AI cloud revenue will reach $2 trillion by 2030, and Alphabet is already a top-three player, with 13% market share (trailing Amazon and Microsoft).

It's not just cloud computing revenue that will accelerate over the coming years. Alphabet just began selling its co-designed TPUs with Broadcom to other companies in the second quarter, and they could be a significant revenue generator very soon. Analysts at Wells Fargo believe Alphabet's TPU sales could reach as high as $99 billion next year.

And if all that wasn't enough to convince you that Alphabet stock could be worth buying right now, consider that its share price looks like a downright bargain. Alphabet stock has a trailing price-to-earnings ratio of just 14.5, which is far below the current tech sector average P/E ratio of 34.

While some investors are worried about AI slowing down amid a new crop of politicians soon taking office, I believe the sell-off has more to do with fear. AI compute needs are rising, and it's likely that companies and politicians will find some middle ground to find a solution.

Considering all the above, Alphabet stock still looks like a very good deal right now.

#3056. Plug Power: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Refresh cycles with Walmart and European energy-security demand are the catalysts that could decide whether the margin recovery holds into 2027.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 9, 2026, Plug Power (PLUG +3.45%) announced that its quarterly net cash usage fell to approximately $28 million, marking its lowest cash burn of the year. The company's focus on operational discipline and gross margin recovery is starting to show in its core material handling and electrolyzer segments, which split water into hydrogen fuel. Three things management said stand out.

Operational discipline helped reduce quarterly cash consumption while avoiding equity dilution from its ATM (at-the-market) equity issuance program, which lets a business sell shares gradually into the public market.

Deployments of GenDrive hydrogen fuel cell units more than doubled year over year, setting up high-volume replacement opportunities with major enterprise customers.

Geopolitical shifts in Europe are pushing customers toward locally produced green hydrogen, making energy independence a central driver of large-scale equipment orders.

Equipment deployment schedules are ramping up into year-end, and Plug Power relies on manufacturing volume leverage, service reliability, and fuel plant stability to expand margins. Management is pursuing positive EBITDAS (earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, amortization, and stock-based compensation) in the fourth quarter through strict cost controls and volume expansion.

The company's strategic focus stays fixed on executing its contracted pipeline while keeping cash needs flat in the near term.

#3054. I Still Think Micron Stock Is a Buy. Here's the Price at Which I'd Sell It.

At about 4 times expected earnings, how high could this memory stock go before it gets too expensive?

Shares of memory chipmaker Micron Technology (MU +6.25%) have had a tough few months. The stock closed at a record $1,213.56 on June 25 and is around $715 as I write -- still about 40% off that high.

After a slide like that, most investors wonder when to buy. I've called the stock a buy more than once this fall, and I still think it's worth buying at today's price.

But when a company's profits can swing as much as Micron's, I think it's just as helpful to decide beforehand when the stock would be too expensive to hold.

For me, that level is around $1,050 a share, or about 47% over $715. Here's how I got there.

Micron's non-GAAP (adjusted) earnings were $75.52 per share in fiscal 2026, which ended Sept. 3. For fiscal 2027, analysts' consensus estimates call for about $176 -- more than twice that record.

At roughly $715, shares cost about 4 times those expected earnings, an extremely low price-to-earnings ratio for any growth stock. In other words, investors don't seem to trust these profits to last.

And the $176 might prove too high. Management's forecast for the fiscal first quarter of 2027 calls for $38.15 in adjusted earnings per share, plus or minus $1. To hit $176, Micron would have to average around $46 a share in the three quarters after that, or about 21% over the guide. But growth is slowing. Adjusted earnings per share climbed 33% sequentially in the fiscal fourth quarter of 2026 (the period ending Sept. 3), and the guide implies a gain of around 14% in fiscal Q1. What's more, Micron's DRAM prices climbed by a high-teens percentage sequentially in fiscal Q4, down from a low-60s percentage gain in fiscal Q3.

To be fair, management expects sequential revenue growth in every quarter of fiscal 2027 and called fiscal Q1 the low for gross margin this fiscal year. But it's still a lot of growth to rely on from a product whose price can drop fast.

Micron's own history shows the valuation investors have paid when memory profits were booming. The stock closed at $62.62 on May 29, 2018, its peak for that cycle. That was about 5 times what the company ultimately earned on an adjusted basis in fiscal 2018 ($11.95 per share).

In its next boom, the stock closed at $97.36 on Jan. 14, 2022, or around 12 times what it later earned on an adjusted basis in fiscal 2022 ($8.35 per share).

The gap seems to boil down to how high profits had risen. Micron's adjusted gross margin was 59% in fiscal 2018, versus 46% in fiscal 2022.

And investors had good reason to doubt those profits. After all, adjusted earnings per share dropped 47% to $6.35 in fiscal 2019, and fiscal 2023 saw an adjusted loss of $4.45 per share.

Today's profits look much higher than either. Micron's adjusted gross margin reached 87% in fiscal Q4, up from 46% a year before. I'd lean closer to 2018's price-to-earnings ratio and use around 6 times fiscal 2027 earnings.

I'd go a bit over 2018's level because Micron arguably has more protection this time. It has signed 26 multiyear strategic customer agreements, with customers agreeing to $32 billion in financial commitments, primarily cash deposits.

Six times $176 comes to about $1,056, which I'd round down to $1,050. That's roughly 13% below the stock's June record close, so Micron wouldn't have to hit a new high for me to think it's too expensive.

My sell price tracks the estimate. Every $10 added to expected fiscal 2027 earnings per share would boost it by $60, so a strong fiscal Q1 report could raise it. If estimates fell to around $153 (the fiscal Q1 guide times four), my price would drop to about $920.

That price-to-earnings ratio could change too. If Micron's agreements covered much more of its revenue than they do today, I could justify paying more per dollar of earnings. And if DRAM prices start falling from quarter to quarter, I wouldn't wait for $1,050 at all.

For now, at around 4 times expected earnings, selling looks premature to me. Sure, there's no guarantee Micron hits $1,050 before profits start falling. I'd just keep close tabs on DRAM prices in the meantime.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Micron Technology. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3052. OraSure (OSUR) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Ebola demand lifted sales, but cash flow breakeven timing slipped.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

OraSure Technologies, Inc. (OSUR +2.62%) reported its results for the third quarter of 2026, noting a return to growth driven by expanded diagnostics and sample management portfolios. Management indicated that core business performance was supported by stabilization in international markets and demand for syndemic testing in the United States. The company is currently navigating the regulatory process for its molecular self-testing product while launching new diagnostic tools for Ebola and urine-based STI collection. Strategy for the upcoming year involves prioritizing operational efficiency and manufacturing consolidation to support a path toward positive operating cash flow.

Operator: Good day, and welcome to the OraSure Technologies, Inc. 2026 Third Quarter Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Please note, this call may be recorded. I would now like to turn the call over to Mike Vallie, Investor Relations. Please go ahead.

Mike Vallie: Good afternoon, and welcome to OraSure Technologies Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Call. Participating in the call for OTI are Carrie Eglinton Manner, our President and Chief Executive Officer; and Ken McGrath, our Chief Financial Officer. As a reminder, today's webcast is being recorded, and the recording can be found on our Investor Relations website. Before we begin, you should know that this call may contain certain forward-looking statements, including statements with respect to revenues, expenses, profitability, earnings or loss per share, cash flows and other financial performance, product development, performance, shipments and markets, business plans, regulatory filings and approvals, expectations and strategies. Actual results could be significantly different.

Factors that could affect results are discussed more fully in OraSure's SEC filings, its annual report on Form 10-K for the year ended December 31, 2025, its quarterly reports on Form 10-Q and its other SEC filings. Although forward-looking statements help to provide more complete information about future prospects, listeners should keep in mind that forward-looking statements are based solely on information available to management as of today. We undertake no obligation to update any forward-looking statements to reflect events or circumstances after this call. In addition, the call will also discuss certain non-GAAP financial measures.

A reconciliation of these measures to the most directly comparable GAAP measures is included in our earnings press release issued today, which is available on the Investor Relations section of our website. With that, I'm pleased to turn the call over to Carrie.

Carrie Eglinton Manner: Thanks, Mike, and thank you to everyone for joining us. Today, I'll discuss some highlights from Q3 and provide updates on our key priorities for the balance of 2026 and our outlook for 2027. Q3 was another solid quarter of execution and our return to growth. We delivered 14% year-over-year growth and 1% sequential growth, meaningfully expanded gross margins and reduced operating spend, results driven by continued execution of our strategy. Continuing now with more on each of our portfolios. In International Diagnostics, our Q3 performance reflected our approach to stabilize, diversify and localize our business.

We are building on our long-standing leadership in HIV testing while expanding our portfolio and strengthening partnerships that broaden our reach and enable us to address critical public health needs across Africa, Latin America and other international markets. Starting with Ebola. As discussed last quarter, in July, we received FDA emergency use authorization for our OraQuick Ebola 2.0 rapid antigen test. The test detects all 4 Ebola virus species known to cause human disease, including Bundibugyo, the species responsible for the current outbreak. Since the outbreak began, its scale and geographic reach have increased dramatically. As of November 3, WHO reports that there are now more than 10,600 confirmed cases and nearly 5,200 deaths.

Transmission has spread across multiple provinces in the Democratic Republic of the Congo and across international borders, including the first recorded cases in Kenya's history as well as the case in Nigeria. Against this backdrop, the need for rapid decentralized diagnostic capacity is clear. Rapid testing can play an important role alongside surveillance, contact tracing, isolation and other public health measures to identify cases quickly and help interrupt chains of transmission. Our test is particularly well suited for point-of-need deployment with its low infrastructure needs, speed and ease of use. Following our FDA EUA, we have quickly fulfilled initial commercial orders for Ebola 2.0. We believe, however, that the more meaningful catalysts for broader adoption are still ahead of us.

One important catalyst will be the results of the BRIDGE Access Platform study, which we previously discussed. Led by Africa CDC and WHO with Unitaid support, the BRIDGE Access Study is independently evaluating diagnostic products for the current Bundibugyo outbreak with the evidence intended to inform Africa CDC recommendations as well as national and regional decisions regarding registration and introduction of diagnostic tests. This is an important example of how the public health environment in Africa is evolving post-pandemic and following the international aid cuts of 2025. African countries are increasingly focused on building greater self-reliance and locally led health security infrastructure.

Africa CDC itself has made local manufacturing, reduced dependency and greater health sovereignty central elements of its health security agenda. That evolution means adoption increasingly occurs through African-led country-specific pathways for evaluation, registration, procurement and deployment. These include new processes that are taking time even during an urgent outbreak, but they also reinforce the importance of the investments we have been making to localize our presence and capabilities in the region. To best serve Africa, OraSure has launched local assembly in Uganda, where our partner is already expanding access to our HIV products and has the potential to add Ebola and other products in the future. Nigeria is our next planned market for launching our localized approach in Africa.

We believe we are well positioned as the Ebola response moves into its next phase. CDC currently assesses the most likely near-term scenario as a very large, sustained outbreak with high-intensity transmission in multiple areas, underscoring the immediate and continued need for a range of effective diagnostic tools. Our product can play an important role on the front lines and porous borders of this outbreak. Our manufacturing capability is ready, and we bring decades of experience and partnerships to readily serve the region's needs. Beyond this outbreak, we believe rapid point-of-need diagnostics can become an increasingly important component of outbreak preparedness and response and that OraSure is ready and able to play a meaningful role as these pathways mature.

Moving to sickle cell disease. We continue to support growing awareness and expansion of newborn screening across Africa, where Africa CDC estimates that 300,000 to 400,000 babies are born with sickle cell disease each year. This momentum is driving increased demand for Sickle SCAN and our successful integration of BioMedomics is enabling us to leverage our international commercial infrastructure to broaden access in markets with significant unmet need. We are also building partnerships with national and local health programs to expand point-of-need testing. Our recently announced partnership in Nairobi is one example, focused on scaling Sickle SCAN across community health centers and bringing testing closer to patients.

At the same time, our participation in the World Coalition on Sickle Cell Disease connects OTI with leaders across government, health care and patient advocacy to advance earlier diagnosis and strengthen testing pathways. We believe these relationships and joint efforts can help create the conditions for broader adoption of solutions like Sickle SCAN over time. In summary, the ongoing progress of HIV, early momentum of Sickle SCAN, the potential of Ebola plus the launch of our partners' near-shoring operations reflect the continued evolution of our international diagnostics strategy. We are successfully and increasingly pairing differentiated point-of-need diagnostics with localized capabilities and strong partnerships to expand access, diversify our business and unlock new growth priorities.

Over time, we believe this approach can continue to broaden our global reach, strengthen our competitive position and enhance the durability of our growth. Switching to U.S. Diagnostics. We delivered strong year-over-year growth driven by strength in HCV, syphilis and the continued underlying demand for HIV testing. Our syndemic testing strategy continues to be an important differentiator for us. With rapid testing solutions for HIV, HCV and syphilis, we give customers a broader set of tools to reach populations that face multiple interconnected infectious disease risks. We're very pleased with the progress of this strategy and the clinical and economic value it can deliver for our customers and the communities they serve.

Turning to a regulatory process update on our InteliQuick CT/NG test on the Sherlock platform, intended as a first-of-its-kind rapid molecular self-test. We continue to work towards our resubmission with the FDA following our announcement of withdrawal in August. We are currently working within the pre-sub process, the aim of which is to align on the best path to regulatory access. We have valuable experience and feedback from our prior submission and are leveraging that and this pre-sub process to evaluate a range of pathways that can bring this important innovation to market. We remain confident in the product's performance, the impact it can have on patients and our ability to navigate the regulatory landscape.

We will provide updates as we have more to share. Now moving to our Sample Management Solutions portfolio. Growth from key commercial customers, along with the timing of orders, contributed to a strong Q3 with growth of more than 20%, both year-over-year and sequentially. As announced last quarter, we received FDA clearance for our Colli-Pee Dx urine collection kit in June. We believe this technology can help expand access to STI testing through a more convenient and private approach to sample collection. We commercially launched the product in the third quarter and have been very encouraged by initial sales.

At the same time, we are excited by the pipeline of potential customers who see real opportunities for the technology, both the chemistry and the device, with many evaluating the workflow required to incorporate self-collected urine into their testing programs. This customer engagement reinforces our confidence in the opportunity for Colli-Pee Dx to support expanded access to STI testing over time. In Q3, we delivered strong growth in our SMS portfolio. Looking forward, our robust portfolio, the increased anticipated contributions from Colli-Pee and its NucleoPrecision chemistry, along with our partnerships with IVD platform manufacturers, reinforce our confidence in the long-term outlook for Sample Management Solutions and its ability to contribute to our return to growth strategy.

Overall, as we've outlined, OTI delivered a solid third quarter. We further demonstrated our return to growth, drove meaningful gross margin expansion and continued to take cost out of the business. These results reflect the progress we are making in executing our strategy and positioning the business for sustainable growth and long-term value creation. With that, I'll turn the call over to Ken to discuss our financial results and guidance.

Kenneth McGrath: Thanks, Carrie. Total revenue in the third quarter was $30.9 million, growing 14% year-over-year and approximately 1% on a sequential basis. Diagnostic products generated $16.8 million of revenue in Q3, representing 16% year-over-year growth. The increase was driven by higher international HIV revenue, reflecting stabilization in our African markets and customer order timing, improved HCV performance supported by U.S. funding dynamics and continued growth from Sickle SCAN. Sample Management Solutions revenue in Q3 was $12.5 million, a 21% year-over-year increase, driven by key commercial customer growth and timing of specific commercial orders. Our Q3 GAAP gross margin increased 173 basis points to 45.2% from 43.5% in the third quarter of last year.

And non-GAAP gross margin in Q3 increased to 45.9% from 44.2%. The gross margin expansion was driven by operational efficiencies, resulting in improved overhead absorption, partially offset by geographic mix. While margins may move quarter-to-quarter based on product mix and order timing, we believe the absorption gains we have built this year will continue to further improve as we scale the business. Turning to operating expenses. As expected, we saw a material sequential improvement in our operating spend. In the third quarter, R&D expense was $7.6 million, down $1.8 million sequentially as a result of the tapering of development-related spend.

At the same time, we continue to invest in innovation, including advancing the early development of HPV testing as a potential future application for Colli-Pee in collaboration with leading global molecular diagnostic partners. Sales and marketing remained relatively flat at $6.6 million as we leverage our existing capabilities to invest in new growth within our portfolio. And finally, as expected, G&A expense declined to a more normalized level in the third quarter. Total G&A was $12 million, a significant $2.4 million reduction coming as a result of lower nonrecurring items, including legal and professional service costs. Noncash stock compensation expense in the third quarter was $2.3 million, and depreciation and amortization expense was $2.2 million.

And our GAAP operating income in Q3 was a loss of $13 million, and our non-GAAP operating loss was $10.2 million. Moving to our balance sheet. We ended the quarter with 0 debt and total cash and cash equivalents of $148.2 million. Operating cash flow in the third quarter was negative $10.9 million. Consistent with our balanced capital deployment strategy, we continue to evaluate organic and inorganic opportunities that can accelerate our profitable growth in high-value markets and leverage our existing capabilities. This guidance represents full year revenue between $119.5 million and $121.5 million and implies a full year growth rate between 3.8% and 5.6%, with 4.7% at the midpoint and as we expect gross margin in Q4 to be similar to or slightly below Q3.

As I mentioned earlier, while quarterly margins may fluctuate due to revenue mix, improved manufacturing efficiencies and continued focus on our underlying margin profile are driving fundamental improvement and providing a stronger base to build from going forward. Looking ahead to 2027. Regarding cash flow from operations. At the beginning of the year, we laid out a path to achieving cash flow from operations breakeven, entering 2027. That plan contemplated a combination of continued improvement in the underlying business and contributions from several growth opportunities. As the year progressed, the timing and mix of those growth opportunities have changed. Our original plan included a meaningful contribution from CT/NG molecular self-testing.

Following our decision to withdraw that submission, we had identified other opportunities, including significant potential demand for Ebola rapid testing that we believe could offset much of the contribution within the original time frame. As Carrie discussed, we continue to see attractive opportunities in Ebola and across the broader portfolio. But as the timing of these opportunities has evolved, we have decided to take a more conservative approach to our estimates, and we now expect to reach cash flow from operations breakeven in the second half of 2027. Continuing our conversation on 2027.

In alignment with the execution of our strategy, we expect full year revenue growth to accelerate in 2027 compared with 2026, and we'll provide additional insights into our 2027 outlook on our fourth quarter earnings call. With that, I'll turn the call back to Carrie to conclude.

Carrie Eglinton Manner: Thanks, Ken. Looking to the balance of 2026 and into 2027, we are focused on continued execution of our strategy and our return to profitable growth. During the third quarter, we demonstrated the results of our strategy, supported by our simplified portfolio, operating discipline, strengthened production and manufacturing capabilities and advanced innovation pipeline. Together, these drivers translate to revenue and operating margin expansion, positioning OraSure to deliver sustainable long-term value for our customers, partners and shareholders. And I'm wondering if you're able to, whatever extent you're willing to disclose, if there was a meaningful contribution yet in terms of revenue from either Ebola or Colli-Pee and if there's any meaningful contribution from either of the 2 factored into the Q4 guide?

Carrie Eglinton Manner: We appreciate the deeper dive. We don't -- we're not guiding by product. What we would say is what we had shared, which is we're really encouraged by the initial orders. Product ramp-ups, you know how these go. There's adoption and really trying to move into those repeat orders. So we're very encouraged by the initial orders, enough -- encouraged enough to talk about them and to -- I think that's part of the confidence in what we shared on 2027, which we've often just sort of shared quarterly.

I think what we're trying to indicate is that we do feel like that momentum is strong enough for us to point to that -- those increases for a return to growth this year that we believe we're demonstrating and a commitment that following Q4 earnings, we plan to give more visibility, though we are sharing that we see an acceleration of that growth. And I'd reiterate what Ken had said. To your point, while we're not going to talk specifics on the guide, we are encouraged to say it's a part of the acceleration of growth that we're referring to for '27 in total.

John Wilkin: That makes sense. Fair enough. And then lastly, I just want to ask you, if you're able to delve any more into just what the contracting process is looking like for Ebola. And I know you mentioned a couple of countries in the pipeline that you're going to be launching into. Maybe just any -- if you can size like the magnitude of those opportunities at all or what the time lines look like for getting those in place and getting the products starting to sell through?

Carrie Eglinton Manner: Yes. Part of the reason we shared more of the Ebola background is that major catalyst that I talked about, the BRIDGE Access Study, obviously, we were hoping that you have seen the results of that sooner. That study was announced in May. Inclusion in that was in June. The descriptions of it were moving at the speed of the outbreak. And considering that this is the fastest-growing outbreak to date, the need for diagnostics and rapid testing is so clear that I think everybody is expecting that to be a major catalyst to drive that contracting. So quite frankly, I would have expected that already. We are not unusual in waiting for this.

I mean I know the U.S. government is -- we work with BARDA and CDC, and we're all waiting on that as a catalyst to sort of open up the regional ordering. What I would say is that like we've emphasized, we've got decades-long relationships. We will serve needs that are already contracting. But this is a matter of new processes in place that were not sort of well defined. But fundamentally, we feel very well positioned to work with these partners who we support, who we partner with today. And we've been very encouraged. They've been figuring out funding and implementation paths for all of the rest of their diagnostic needs.

We believe that, that will be the case with Ebola as well. And there are really strong partnerships that we plan to continue to build on. So while we don't know the timing precisely, we do expect that before the end of the year. And as soon as we know more, we'll be sharing that.

Operator: I'm showing no further questions. I'd like to turn the call back over to Carrie Eglinton Manner for any closing remarks.

Carrie Eglinton Manner: Thank you. Thank you for your interest and participating in our call. We look forward to our offline discussions as well as following up next quarter. Thanks so much. With that, we'll close.

Operator: Thank you for your participation. You may now disconnect. Good day.

#3048. AppLovin: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

AppLovin's architecture overhaul is still in progress, so the payoff from the new AI models remains unproven for long-term holders.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, AppLovin (APP +4.80%) announced that revenue jumped 55% year over year to $2.18 billion. The company is generating massive cash flow while expanding its ad engine beyond mobile gaming into broad e-commerce categories. Three things management said stand out.

Engineers began rolling out a structural architectural update to Axon, the deep learning algorithm that predicts user actions and matches ads to mobile app users. Early performance metrics show advertisers spent more money profitably, particularly in newer non-gaming consumer categories where historical data is sparse.

Mobile gaming has traditionally generated most of the business, but non-gaming e-commerce brands are rapidly ramping up spend on the platform. Management sees this consumer vertical providing an extended runway as new advertisers validate performance through return on ad spend, a metric that measures ad revenue against ad spend.

AppLovin converted roughly 65% of its top line into free cash flow this quarter, giving management the firepower to repurchase $1.4 billion of stock. Retiring shares at this pace amplifies per-share returns while net leverage, which measures debt relative to adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, remains near zero.

Ad spend is surging across mobile games and e-commerce, and leadership is signaling total comfort with its compounding trajectory. The company maintains an asset-light model, high profit-to-cash conversion, and expanding ad inventory on connected television, which serves streaming video ads to smart televisions.

Management is focusing near-term execution on finishing the architecture transition, attracting new e-commerce advertisers, and scaling connected television campaigns.

#3043. Via: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Over 95% of Via's code is now written with AI, a productivity gain that may matter more to margins than to new contracts.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 9, 2026, Via (VIA +12.80%) announced revenue grew 31% year over year to $144 million as customer contracts expanded. Broadening software adoption across municipal transit agencies and disciplined spending are moving the transit technology provider toward net profitability. Three takeaways from management's comments illustrate how the business is evolving.

When Via manages an entire transit network, it replaces pen-and-paper systems with software across fleet scheduling, maintenance, and dispatching. These end-to-end contracts yield immediate efficiency gains for transit agencies, so Via doesn't have to rebuild its sales effort for every new city.

Rising fuel prices and vehicle ownership costs are pushing commuters toward public transportation. U.S. voters recently approved ballot initiatives that will generate $30 billion in local funding. That unlocks fresh expansion opportunities for Via's transit software.

Automated engineering and customer support tools are lowering internal operating expenses across the business. Software automation helped raise adjusted gross margin to 40.5%. The company doesn't need to bloat headcount to ship new features faster.

Revenue expanded 31% year over year in the third quarter while operating expenses grew at a much slower rate. Regional customer density creates flywheel effects that lower sales costs in neighboring cities, while internal software automation accelerates product delivery. Management is tracking sales pipeline conversion and gross margin consistency.

Expanding into school transportation extends Via's core platform into adjacent municipal markets. Converting a record pipeline while locking in profitability defines the primary operational focus heading into next year.

The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Via Transportation. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3030. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#3029. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#3027. Warriors guard Will Richard sidelined by torn ligament in left thumb

SAN FRANCISCO — Second-year Golden State Warriors guard Will Richard has a torn ligament in his left thumb that will sideline him for an undetermined stretch.

The Warriors listed Richard on the injury report Sunday with a torn ulnar collateral ligament and he was out for Monday's game against New Orleans at Chase Center as the team concludes a six-game homestand. Golden State didn't have practice or media availability Sunday, so there wasn't an immediate timetable for Richard's recovery or a known treatment plan.

Richard has been providing key minutes as both a starter and a reserve as Golden State has won seven straight games since an 0-3 start. He made two starts this past week, against Phoenix and Miami.

Stephen Curry has credited the Warriors figuring out their roles to grab some momentum after dropping the first three games.

"Everybody having an impact, throw Will Richard in there, too, he's had three straight games really where he's turned up the energy defensively for us," Curry said after a 116-113 win over the Suns on Monday. "Defensively he's just been a pest out there."

The 23-year-old guard was a second-round draft pick last year out of Florida after he led the Gators to a national championship.

Richard is averaging 5.1 points, 1.8 rebounds and 1.0 assists while shooting 47.5% from the floor and 42.9% from 3-point range.

Sacramento Kings forward Keegan Murray dealt with the same injury early last season, underwent surgery and missed the first 15 games.

Out — Gary Payton II (left knee inflammation)

With both Richard and Payton out, the Warriors perimeter defense is going to be severely compromised tonight. Fortunately, they're facing a below-average Pelicans offense which is also missing some key players.

Out — Jimmy Butler III (right ACL repair surgery)Out — Moses Moody (left patellar tendon repair surgery)Out — Kristaps Porziņģis (not with team)

The standard trio. Butler is progressing nicely, but these three will likely be a staple of this section of the injury report until Porziņģis is traded.

Available — Gui Santos (left knee tendinitis)

Hey, some good news! Santos is still dealing with some knee pain, but thankfully is able to play.

Williamson has battled health issues his entire career, so it's not surprising to see him get a rest day, especially since the Pelicans played (and traveled) last night. It's great seeing him healthy, though, as he's appeared in all 10 games prior to this one, and is averaging 33 minutes per night.

Out — Trey Murphy III (bilateral patellar tendinopathy)

We know the Warriors have had their sights set on a Murphy trade for a while. Unfortunately for their scouts — but fortunately for their prospects of winning tonight — they won't get to see him in this game.

Out — Yves Missi (left knee contusion/bone bruise)

The Dubs have also been linked to Missi, as a potential target to replace the retiring Porziņģis. The third-year center was injured in the second game of the season, and hasn't played since.

Out — Trendon Watford (right ankle sprain)

Watford has been a solid bench contributor in his first season in the bayou, so NOLA's bench will be shortened by his absence tonight.

Enjoy the game, Dub Nation. It tips off at 7:00 p.m. PT on NBA TV and NBC Sports Bay Area.

#3020. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#3016. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#3014. Lakers’ bench comes up big in road win over Hawks

For the second night in a row, the Lakers picked up a tough road win while shorthanded, this time beating the Hawks 124-114 on Monday to extend their winning streak to six games.

Austin Reaves (illness) and Quentin Grimes (right finger sprain) were both out for the game, placing a heavier burden on the Lakers' role players to step up. The response came in the form of a season-high 49 bench points.

LA scored 29 points off 20 Hawks turnovers and, again, won the points in the paint battle, 52-42.

The Hawks grabbed the lead and momentum early in the first period. Onyeka Okongwu led them with seven points. LA was 3-7 from the field. Ziaire Williams got the Lakers' first basket in a triple. Luka Dončić made his first shot attempt, but had three of the team's turnovers.

At the 6:53 mark, the Hawks were up eight.

Luka inched closer to his usual double figures in the first quarter, pushing his total to seven. With Austin Reaves out, Cameron Carr saw minutes in the rotation. He had two rebounds.

The first ended with back-to-back dunks from Carr and Jarred Vanderbilt, both assisted by Luka, and a triple from Sandro Mamukelashvili. LA was up by nine.

Los Angeles was cooking early in the second period with Williams and Mamu both knocking down triples. Collin Sexton also connected with Walker Kessler twice for dunks.

LA was on an extended 28-9 scoring run. Every Laker off the bench, minus Adou Theiro, had scored.

Sexton continued to give the purple and gold a huge boost. He now had nine points with four assists. He and Kessler helped give Los Angeles a 19-point lead with 6:34 left.

CJ McCollum and Nickeil Alexander-Walker brought some life to the Hawks, combining for 12 points to shave points off the lead. After being down by as much as 20, Atlanta crawled back on the shoulders of Jalen Johnson to cut the deficit to 11 at halftime.

Williams began the third period with the first four points. Atlanta, though, still had momentum and were still slowly chipping away at LA's lead. They got within six, with Dyson Daniels heating up and scoring five points.

Both teams turned on the offensive jets, scoring at a ridiculous high level.

Alexander-Walker got hot for Atlanta, scoring nine points. Luka and Mamu combined for 11 points. Los Angeles was shooting 46% from the field and the Hawks were close behind with 44%

The Lakers let Atlanta gain steam again as they knocked down three triples. They got to within three, but Luka converted on a midrange jumper, giving LA a five-point lead at the end of the third.

Mamu continued his stellar play by knocking down a 3-pointer to start the final frame. He had an impressive 19 points off the bench. The Hawks cut the deficit all the way down to two thanks to Aaron Wiggins scoring five points.

Los Angeles answered immediately with four straight points to take a six-point lead with 8:22 left.

The three ball was proving to be important for both teams as the Lakers saw Sexton make another, but Aaron Wiggins and McCollum drained two for the Hawks, getting the team to within four with 4:29 left.

Los Angeles erupted for a wonderful 9-2 scoring run on the shoulders of Mamu, Luka, Williams, LaRavia and Matisse Thybulle, putting the finishing touches on a great performance.

Luka finished with 32 points, eight rebounds, 10 assists and five steals. Kessler ended with 21 points, 10 rebounds and four assists. Sexton had 18 points and six assists off the bench.

Mamu logged 23 points with eight rebounds and three assists. Williams pitched in with 12 points. Thybulle had six points, all of which came at moments the team needed big shots.

Carr ended with two points and three rebounds. Thiero had two rebounds, an assist and a steal.

The Lakers' next game will be against the Atlanta Hawks on Monday at 4:00 PM PT.

#3009. Chiefs News: 3 Chiefs players labeled as ‘Day-to-Day’

Fans of the Kansas City Chiefs do not take the injury status of "day-to-day" at face value. It's been that way since safety Eric Berry held that status for what felt like the entire 2018 season.

So it's hard to take much away from head coach Andy Reid labeling three Chiefs players — quarterback Patrick Mahomes (elbow), wide receiver Xavier Worthy (hamstring) and right tackle Jaylon Moore (ankle) — as "day-to-day" during his press conference on Monday. Still, it's an update.

Join fellow Chiefs fans to react to every explosive play and game-changing moment in real time.

Download the SB Nation app on iOS or Android.

Download the SB Nation app on your mobile device! For Apple devices, click here. For Android devices, click here.

Chiefs QB Patrick Mahomes day-to-day with elbow injury | ESPN

Chiefs coach Andy Reid said Monday that quarterback Patrick Mahomes is considered day-to-day with his right elbow injury, an injury he sustained in Sunday's win over the New York Jets. Reid shared that Mahomes was in the Chiefs' training facility Monday morning to receive treatment for his injury.

Following the game, an MRI scan didn't reveal any major injury to Mahomes' elbow, sources told NFL Network's Ian Rapoport.

"It was more positive news than negative news on Patrick," Reid said Monday. "It looks like it's just a matter of [getting] some rehabbing in there and getting rid of some of the soreness. We'll just see where it goes from there. He's in good spirits."

Former Chiefs player was roasted for his take on kicker Harrison Butker | The Kansas City Star

At 11:21 a.m. on Sunday, roughly 40 minutes before kickoff of the Jets game, ex-Chiefs wideout Gehrig Dieter shared this message on X: "I'm calling it now. Butker going to go on a generational run. He won't miss another kick the entire season."

Ninety minutes later, that generational run ended before it began as Butker missed a 51-yard attempt — after missing from 56 before a penalty gave him another shot. That led fans to believe his tenure with the Chiefs was nearly over, although coach Andy Reid said after the game he wasn't ready to announce a change.

Cover 32: What every team should do at NFL trade deadline, plus game balls and gaffes from Week 9 | CBS Sports

Jacksonville has a reason to listen. One factor that could make the Jaguars flinch and trade away a player like Thomas is the need to recoup draft capital, a point Fowler raised during the discussion.

ESPN's Adam Schefter outlined the compensation headed to the New Orleans Saints in the Jaguars' recent trade for defensive tackle Davon Godchaux. The Saints are receiving a conditional 2028 sixth-round pick. However, that pick could turn into a 2027 sixth-round pick, depending on the conditions attached to the deal.

"It's part of it, you know," he told reporters after the game. "I'm booing myself sometimes. People are passionate. Fans are passionate. They want to win games. They want me to make kicks. I want to make kicks. It's part of it. That's what you sign up for. I've got to do better."

The 10-year veteran is ranked the sixth-most-accurate kicker in NFL history, with an average of 87.1 percent of field goals made. But he's been struggling significantly this season and currently leads the league with seven missed field goals (over eight games).

The news comes three days after Aiyuk and his new agent, Drew Rosenhaus, met with the 49ers' brass at the team's business offices in Santa Clara, Calif., with the receiver saying he was 100 percent healthy and seeking reinstatement. Aiyuk had been on the reserve/left squad list — where players are not paid and do not count against the team's roster numbers — since December and had not been to the team's facility since.

The 49ers have made it clear Aiyuk will never play for them again. Asked about the roster exemption Monday afternoon, coach Kyle Shanahan replied, "It means absolutely nothing to our team. … That's all administrative stuff to me."

While Monday's transactions were undoubtedly costly for the franchise, the Jaguars feel ready to go for it and make a legitimate postseason run. Whether that is enough for them to run the table in the AFC and make what would be the team's first Super Bowl remains to be seen.
