#3008. Warriors have no urgency to trade Porzingis, give up draft picks

The Golden State Warriors would like to trade Kristaps Porzingis by December 15. However, they're not about to sacrifice draft capital to do it for a long-term contract.

There's not a ton of pressure on the Warriors to trade Porzingis, who isn't being paid as he decides to "step away" from basketball, which he allegedly loves. He and the NBA Players Association aren't planning to challenge to Warriors taking him off the payroll, which saves Joe Lacob about $250K per game. He may not play this season, for the Warriors or anyone else, but that doesn't mean his contract is an albatross.

According to Clutch Points, the Warriors have had trade talks regarding Porzingis with the Chicago Bulls, New Orleans Pelicans, and Milwaukee Bucks. They're reportedly interested in 26-year-old center Jalen Smith of the Bulls, who is making $9.4M in the last year of his contract. It's not clear who on the Pelicans' roster has interested the Warriors, but with the Bucks, it's center Myles Turner, who is signed through 2029 for $82.6M.

The Bucks want the Warriors to give them draft picks in a Turner trade, which would have to include another player (Moses Moody?) heading to Milwaukee, as the Warriors can't take back more money than they send out. The Warriors think that taking on that much of Turner's salary means they should be getting a draft pick, not the Bucks.

Turner is having a wretched season for the Bucks, who are in a weird rebuild limbo since trading Giannis Antetokounmpo this summer. They got four players back from the Miami Heat, drafted two players in the lottery, and have veterans Tyler Herro, Kyle Kuzma, and Caris Levert on expiring deals. They're 1-9 and since they don't have their own first-round pick until 2031, the Bucks don't even have incentive to avoid the lottery penalty for having one of the league's worst records.

The 30-year-old Turner is averaging 7.1 points and 5.7 rebounds this season, though he is blocking 2.5 shots per game. His effort seems to be lacking, as seen in a blowout loss to the Boston Celtics Sunday.

What Turner can provide, assuming he's trying, is a rare combination of rim protection and outside shooting. He's a career 36.4% three-point shooter and has blocked 2.1 shots per game for his career. Turner was the pivot man on an Indiana Pacers team that was a Tyrese Haliburton Achilles injury from winning a title. It's easy to see how he's a logical replacement for Porzingis, with the bonus that he's played at least 71 games the last three seasons.

But his contract is n0t good. It's even worse for the Bucks, since they waived-and-stretched Damian Lillard's contract to afford the deal, and Dame Time now costs them $22.5M on the salary cap for each of the next four seasons. By contrast, Porzingis is on the books for only $3M next season, isn't get paid anything right now, and might take a buyout on the $17M+ remaining on this year's deal.

The key calendar date for the Warriors is Dec. 15, because of the NBA's "reaggregation" rule. Players acquired before that date can be combined with other players/contracts until the trade deadline. This came up for the Warriors in Dec. 2024, when they traded De'Anthony Melton for Dennis Schroder just before the reaggregation deadline, then aggregated Schroder's salary in the blockbuster trade for Jimmy Butler.

That's why a player like Smith could be more appealing for the Warriors than Smith, as he could provide temporary help while still remaining highly moveable in a later trade. The Warriors could conceivably take back their old friend Buddy Hield in such a trade, as his salary and Smith's are still less than Porzingis' total money — and Hield can be waived next summer for just over $3M.

There's a lot of possibilities for what the Warriors do with Porzingis. But giving up future draft picks to ditch him does not seem to be one of them.

→ Baca artikel asli

#3003. Ravens news: Eric DeCosta gets mixed grades for Jacoby Brissett trade

Welcome back to Loose Feathers, a regular round-up of the latest news and analysis about the Baltimore Ravens. In today's edition, we cover a weak trade market for some of the Ravens' veterans, grades for Eric DeCosta's acquisition of Jacoby Brissett, and an assessment of first-year offensive coordinator Declan Doyle, Let's dive in:

Apologies to the Ravens fans hoping to flip some of the team's highly-paid, underperforming veterans for draft picks at the deadline. If Baltimore is holding out any hope of getting Lamar Jackson back, they will not trade away his left tackle, and neither Andrews nor Humphrey can be seen as upgrades right now. All three were listed under "no real market" in Jeff Zrebiec's recent trade deadline round-up.

One name remains: Roquan Smith. His contract seems untenable heading into the offseason, but another team could be interested in upgrading their linebacker corps this season. Getting rid of his future salary could help Baltimore's tight future cap picture and maybe even free up the money to re-sign Trenton Simpson. Some would even argue that a Simpson-Teddye Buchanan duo might actually be an improvement over Smith's up-and-down (mostly down) play this year.

But the Ravens love Smith. They loved him coming out of Georgia in 2018, and they loved him enough in Chicago to send a second-round pick to the Bears. And again, Jackson is still in the picture, albeit on the margins. With no clarity to be had before Tuesday's deadline, Smith seems likely to stay put.

It's worth noting that trading Smith might be less of an option in the offseason. Linebackers tend to have more value during the season when teams are desperate to make up for injuries and/or push all-in. But in the offseason, teams will have more (and cheaper) options at linebacker, allowing them to focus on premium positions instead. The same is true of running back. This might be the best time to trade Smith and Derrick Henry, but the Ravens will not pull the trigger.

It's hard to say whether there will be any interest in Smith. He's still an effective player, but he's 29 now, he's not making the same number of impact plays and he still has another year left on a big-money contract.

A purge of some veteran defensive players seems overdue, and moving on from Smith would save them $8 million in cap space, which would come in handy this offseason.

However, shipping off the team's vocal leader and defensive signal-caller could make it seem like the Ravens are waiving the white flag on the season. They probably wouldn't get much in return, either.

I'm mostly unmoved by this deal. Brissett does give first-year coach Jesse Minter and offensive coordinator Declan Doyle a higher floor on offense. Still, it's hard to see the Ravens contending in any meaningful capacity, especially with the disappointing way Minter's defense is playing. General manager Eric DeCosta gave up marginal draft capital, so it's a fine bet to try and salvage something from a season slipping away.

The Ravens lost 33-10 to the Jaguars on Thursday night with Fagnano (and also some Derrick Henry in the Wildcat) at quarterback. In all likelihood, Baltimore's season is over with Jackson currently on injured reserve and the team at 4-5. Ravens coach Jesse Minter has said Jackson's injury is "potentially" season-ending. But Brissett gives the team at least a sliver of hope of staying afloat long enough to hand the reins back to Jackson with a shot at the playoffs.

But make no mistake, those chances are extremely slim. Even knowing Brissett is about to take over at QB (and assuming Jackson has, say, a 30% chance of coming back in games starting in Week 14, ESPN's Football Power Index gives Baltimore only a 3% chance of reaching the playoffs. If Jackson is indeed able to come back, that number goes up, but it's still a long shot. The cost here is low. By ESPN's draft pick valuations, the swap works out to the equivalent of an early sixth-round pick. Considering the team's upside if it does get Jackson back, I think that is easily worth it for Baltimore.

Head Coach Jesse Minter gave a positive update Monday on All-Pro safety Kyle Hamilton, who left Thursday night's game against the Jacksonville Jaguars with a hip injury.

"I think everything looks fairly good," Minter said. "We'll find out as the guys come back in tomorrow (Tuesday). But I wouldn't expect that to be something where … hopefully he doesn't miss time."

Minter was also optimistic about cornerback Nate Wiggins (illness) and safety Jaylinn Hawkins (knee) returning to action this week. Wiggins did not play against the Jaguars, while Hawkins has missed the last three games.

"There's definitely optimism there," Minter said regarding Hawkins rejoining practice. "I think (Nate) will be okay. It was an unfortunate timing of an illness."

#3001. Player Grades: Cavs vs Hornets – James Harden reaches DEFCON 1

The Cleveland Cavaliers lost to the Charlotte Hornets 124-112 in a game that trailed by as much as 30 points.

This is, without a doubt, one of the most alarming and concerning games the Cavs have played in the last five seasons.

Grades are based on our usual expectations for each player.

Thomas and the Cavs' youth were responsible for some of the only inspiring basketball that was played tonight.

They weren't perfect, but Thomas led the way with an energetic half that showed most of the team's issues are centered on not trying hard enough.

Thomas shot 7-12 from the floor. His effort and shot-making allowed the Cavs reserves to cut the deficit to within single digits.

Mitchell has cleaned up his offensive process in recent games to strike a solid balance between playmaking and scoring. His approach offensively wasn't the issue tonight. It was his defensive effort, and the fact that this team has lost all semblance of an identity around him.

Allen was awesome in the first quarter of this game. He made his return with a bang, detonating on a lob pass for the first play of the game. He finished the opening quarter with 9 points and 4 rebounds on a perfect 4-4 shooting. He even drilled a three-pointer.

It's not Allen's fault that everything went down the drain.

9 points, 2 rebounds, 3 assists, 2 steals, 1 block

Mobley is expected to be capable of transcending all of this. I can't give him a pass for fading into the background when the team is spiraling out of control. He's one of the players who is supposed to stabilize things. This wasn't it.

11 points, 5 assists, 3 rebounds, 4 turnovers

Harden has not looked good this season. Far from it. He's hit a new low defensively, and he's nowhere near good enough on offense right now to justify his minutes. This is a DEFCON 1 situation that can't be ignored.

The Cavs traded for Harden, and then extended him, understanding that the league has been rapidly moving away from his style of play. His slow-paced, heliocentric offense could only carry you so far even in his prime. At 37, Harden isn't showing that he'll be able to adapt his game to no longer being a one-man wrecking crew.

Watson has not been able to find his niche yet. Most of his scoring comes outside of the team's system — and he is not providing enough defensively to tip the scales.

Hezonja is hitting some speed bumps recently but is still someone who I'd feel comfortable giving more minutes to. He plays hard, and he probably has more offensive production in the tank if Cleveland can figure out where he fits.

I've given the Cavs flak for signing Bryant as soon as free agency began last summer. That's only because I think it was worth doing your due diligence and searching for a potential upgrade. It is not to disrespect or invalidate Bryant as a stone-cold veteran who plays every possession like it's life or death.

Bryant plays with a sense of urgency and is a fine role model for the younger players on this team.

Proctor was part of the inspiring group that brought this game back into reach. Effort and energy matter. Looking like you care about basketball matters. The intention that Proctor plays with on each possession was enough to string together stops and scores. Aka, winning basketball.

What a turn of events it's been for Minix. This wasn't a barn burner by any means; he shot just 1-of-4, but Minix deserves a ton of credit for brushing off the fact that this team waived him last week and only brought him back due to necessity. He's played hard and was a positive tonight, given the circumstances.

Omier is another two-way prospect who had a chance to get some run tonight. He made his impact through hustle, and as is the theme, that was enough to make him a positive in stark contrast to the starters.

Porter showed signs of life after a fairly troubling start to the season. He got downhill and looked effective as a ball-handler again. The Cavs would love it if he could get back to being a plus defender and creator.

#2997. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2993. Giants news: Trade deadline day, Cam Skattebo should be ‘full-go’, more

Good morning, New York Giants fans! Here are the stories you need to know as the Giants get set to face off with the Washington Commanders this Thursday.

"I thought we had good players," Harbaugh said as he was leaving Lincoln Financial Field as the first Giants head coach to win in this building since Tom Coughlin in 2013.

"I didn't really know the personality of the team, but I think what I learned is these guys have what it takes. They're tough-minded, they've got big hearts and they're willing to do what it takes. Having what it takes means you're willing to do what it takes, and they've been willing to do it. They work, they believe and they don't flinch."

All of that's enough to give any playoff-starved fan of Big Blue a sugar high. And yet, they'll tell you this isn't about going all in on anything, happy as they are with where the team is. No, under Harbaugh, and with holdover GM Joe Schoen, this is now their default setting. Which, they'll tell you, has little to do with adjusting any sort of preseason expectation.

"No, that's not how we look at winning," Harbaugh texted me from the team bus, heading back to New Jersey on Sunday night. "You're never ONE thing away. This is not addition and subtraction. The math is running on multiple levels of formulas and algorithms and humanity. You have to assess your options and opportunities. Seek value. What makes your team better shorter- and long-term and avoid what hinders your team, even in possibility. It's a case-by-case decision regarding options. The work is on finding and creating the options. That's where the stones have to get turned over.  We are building day-by-day for what's coming tomorrow and in the future BOTH."

The Giants ended their 13-game losing streak at Lincoln Financial Field in Philadelphia with an inelegant but effective 19-11 victory over the Eagles, providing further separation from first place (the Giants are 5-2-1) and last place (the Eagles are 2-7) in the NFC East.

Is finding help stopping the run more important than adding a running back at the trade deadline?

Giants Are Serious Suitors for RB Jonathan Taylor. The Giants could be an aggressive suitor for multiple reasons. They're in the driver's seat in the NFC East, and their lead ball-carrier, Cam Skattebo, missed the previous game with a hamstring injury. Furthermore, this season, when healthy, Skattebo has had ball-security issues, and the Giants benched him in Week 6. He has four fumbles in seven outings (six starts).

Also, keep in mind that head coach John Harbaugh had workhorse running back Derrick Henry in his last two years with the Baltimore Ravens. Taylor isn't the physically imposing ball-carrier that Henry is, but the three-time Pro Bowler is a trustworthy running back who can handle a big workload on the ground and help Big Blue establish a physical offensive identity. Verdict: Buy

In the aftermath of Sunday's 19-11 victory over the Eagles, the Giants' first at Lincoln Financial Field since 2013, D.J. Reader added to the list of what makes Reese special: the intense purpose with which he approaches each snap.

"Arvell proved yet again what I already knew, and I see it before every game, man," Reader said. "He's got this look in his eyes, he's just ready to play. He's gonna be special. He is special. I don't know any young player that can do the things he does. I'm gonna be honest with you: you'll have to find him. Maybe the kid in Seattle [safety Nick Emmanwori]. Because, you don't find a kid that can cover the way he can cover. Come down and fill gaps in the run game. Rush the passer. Doesn't miss tackles. He's serious, man, and it's special to watch. It's very special."

Linebacker Arvell Reese needed only 2.29 seconds to sack Philadelphia's Joe Milton in the second quarter. It was the fastest time to a sack for a Giants player in the past 10 seasons when Reese got a free run at Milton and forced a fumble. It was part of a monster game for the impressive rookie, who had 12 tackles, three sacks, three quarterback hits, three tackles for loss and the forced fumble.

During his postgame press conference, Milton said the Giants "played great today" and specifically credited their approach.

"They had great defensive calls and great players over there," Milton told reporters. "What they did today was just pressure and play over the top." He added that it was "great calls by the defensive coordinator" and offered a shout-out to the unit for stopping Philadelphia and making plays.

#2991. No Austin Reaves, no problem. First-place Lakers beat Hawks for sixth consecutive win

Austin Reaves took the baton from Luka Doncic when Doncic sat out Sunday, and when the roles were reversed Monday, Doncic passed it to his teammates.

Doncic had 32 points and 10 assists in a 124-114 win over the Atlanta Hawks as the Lakers relied on their depth to secure their sixth consecutive win and stay atop the Western Conference despite a short, tired bench.

One night after sitting out because of lower-back tightness, Doncic struggled with his shot but reached double-digit assists for the third time this season. He also had eight rebounds and five steals. Lakers center Walker Kessler had 21 points with 10 rebounds, his sixth double-double in seven games, and forward Sandro Mamukelashvili had what coach JJ Redick called "his best game as a Laker" with 23 points and eight rebounds off the bench to help overcome the absences of Reaves and Quentin Grimes.

Read more: Austin Reaves steps up in Luka Doncic's absence to lead Lakers over Pistons

Reaves, who scored 43 points in a thrilling win at Detroit on Sunday, didn't play because of illness. Grimes suffered a sprained finger on his right hand against Detroit, although Redick doesn't anticipate the injury keeping the guard sidelined for long. X-rays were negative, Redick said, and Grimes could return as soon as Wednesday in Milwaukee.

The Lakers (10-2) didn't look like a team playing on consecutive nights. They blitzed the Hawks (7-5) with 13 unanswered points at the end of the first quarter and ran the lead to 20 midway in the second quarter. Kessler and guard Collin Sexton connected on back-to-back alley-oops during the quarter to inject life into a shorthanded second unit.

"It's building resiliency," Kessler said. "JJ talks about it, going through adversity and understanding that to be a championship team, you've got to respond to that kind of stuff."

Reaves' absence was compounded by Grimes' injury, decimating the team's ballhandling unit. Redick and general manager Rob Pelinka had worked to stock the roster with guards to ward off the possibility of leaving too much responsibility to Doncic. On Monday, it all fell to Doncic and Sexton.

Lakers star Luka Doncic drives between Atlanta's Dyson Daniels, right, and Luguentz Dort in the first quarter Monday. (Kevin C. Cox / Getty Images)

Doncic, who came in averaging a league-high 40.1 points per game, leaned on teammates early with five assists in the first quarter. Sexton got going in the second quarter with eight points and three assists, all on lobs to Kessler, his former teammate with the Utah Jazz and fellow Atlanta native. Sexton finished with 18 points and six assists.

The 6-foot-1 guard signed during free agency when the Lakers wanted to stock the bench with play-making, shooting and defensive intensity. Sexton, playing on his fourth team in three seasons, has delivered on every one.

"He just checks a lot of boxes," Redick said. "For us, it was easy. He expressed interest, and obviously we wanted him, and it's worked out."

Sexton went to high school 15 miles from State Farm Arena. He estimated he had 400 to 500 people in the stands rooting for him. He stayed on the court for more than an hour after the game taking photos with the hundreds who waited.

In front of his city, Sexton wanted to win, no matter what his role would be within the Lakers' shorthanded rotation.

"Whenever those guys are in or out, I have to adjust," Sexton said. "And I feel like adjusting is something I've been doing my entire career."

The Lakers relied on their two youngest players for support. Second-year forward Adou Thiero, who made his season debut only two games ago after struggling with left knee swelling, had two points, two rebounds, an assist and a steal.

Rookie Cameron Carr earned his first rotation minutes and even started the fourth quarter. Carr, whose father, Chris, played in the NBA and famously lost the 1997 dunk contest to Kobe Bryant, scored his first NBA points, appropriately, on a dunk. Doncic snatched a pass from the Hawks and hit Carr on an outlet pass. The 21-year-old rookie gathered for an emphatic, two-handed slam.

The first-round pick had two points and three rebounds. After the game, Doncic made sure to grab the game ball as a memento for the rookie.

#2984. What are the worst NFL trade deadline deals ever? These 7 top the list

Every team trading for a player ahead of the NFL trade deadline is trying to improve its roster. Not every team that makes such a move is successful.

The NFL has seen many midseason trades throughout its history. That's been especially true over the last decade, as the league's decision to move the trade deadline to its current spot after Week 9 has correlated with a higher volume of moves.

While most transactions ahead of the annual cut-off have ended up forgotten, only a few have gone down as all-timers – either for better or worse. Seven trades in particular have aged especially poorly. Many of them have come within the last decade as trade volume has increased.

Here are seven of the worst midseason trades in NFL history, including one widely considered among the worst in sports history:

NFL TRADE DEADLINE: Everything to know before cut-off, including date, time, potential targets

Week 3: Indianapolis Colts kicker Spencer Shrader (3) celebrates with long snapper Luke Rhodes (46) after Shrader kicked a 58-yard field goal against the Houston Texans at Lucas Oil Stadium. The Colts debuted their "Anvil Strike" uniform - featuring an unfamiliar gray uniform and glossy, metallic blue helmet with the familiar horseshoe logo in white. The Colts won, 19-17, for their first win of the season.

Week 3: Buffalo Bills quarterback Josh Allen (17) hands off to running back James Cook III against the Los Angeles Chargers at New Highmark Stadium. For the game, the Bills debuted their "Nickel City" alternate uniforms - the most striking element being the blue "Charge Helmet," adorned simply with an oversized cutout of the team's familiar logo. The Bills won the game, 24-16.

Week 2: Los Angeles Rams quarterback Matthew Stafford (9) hands the ball to running back Kyren Williams (23) during a 28-6 win on "Monday Night Football" against the New York Giants at SoFi Stadium. The Rams debuted their new "Classic Sol" uniforms, an homage to the team's look during its 1951 championship season.

Week 2: Arizona Cardinals quarterback Jacoby Brissett (7) hands off to running back Jeremiyah Love against the Seattle Seahawks at State Farm Stadium. The Cardinals wore their desert-themed "Rivalries" uniforms in the 31-7 loss to the Seahawks. Arizona's "Rivalries" look was one of the best to debut in 2025.

Week 2: The Baltimore Ravens' Trey Hendrickson (91) reacts after a sack against the New Orleans Saints at M&T Bank Stadium. The Ravens wore their "White Noise" uniforms, just one of the team's new uniform combinations that will debut during the 2026 season. The Ravens lost the game, 24-17.

Week 2: New York Jets wide receiver Garrett Wilson (5) celebrates after scoring a touchdown against the Green Bay Packers at MetLife Stadium. The Jets debuted their "White Out" uniforms for their home opener, which ended in a 20-17 overtime loss.

Week 3: Indianapolis Colts kicker Spencer Shrader (3) celebrates with long snapper Luke Rhodes (46) after Shrader kicked a 58-yard field goal against the Houston Texans at Lucas Oil Stadium. The Colts debuted their "Anvil Strike" uniform - featuring an unfamiliar gray uniform and glossy, metallic blue helmet with the familiar horseshoe logo in white. The Colts won, 19-17, for their first win of the season.

Week 3: Buffalo Bills quarterback Josh Allen (17) hands off to running back James Cook III against the Los Angeles Chargers at New Highmark Stadium. For the game, the Bills debuted their "Nickel City" alternate uniforms - the most striking element being the blue "Charge Helmet," adorned simply with an oversized cutout of the team's familiar logo. The Bills won the game, 24-16.

Week 2: Los Angeles Rams quarterback Matthew Stafford (9) hands the ball to running back Kyren Williams (23) during a 28-6 win on "Monday Night Football" against the New York Giants at SoFi Stadium. The Rams debuted their new "Classic Sol" uniforms, an homage to the team's look during its 1951 championship season.

Week 2: Arizona Cardinals quarterback Jacoby Brissett (7) hands off to running back Jeremiyah Love against the Seattle Seahawks at State Farm Stadium. The Cardinals wore their desert-themed "Rivalries" uniforms in the 31-7 loss to the Seahawks. Arizona's "Rivalries" look was one of the best to debut in 2025.

Week 2: The Baltimore Ravens' Trey Hendrickson (91) reacts after a sack against the New Orleans Saints at M&T Bank Stadium. The Ravens wore their "White Noise" uniforms, just one of the team's new uniform combinations that will debut during the 2026 season. The Ravens lost the game, 24-17.

Week 2: New York Jets wide receiver Garrett Wilson (5) celebrates after scoring a touchdown against the Green Bay Packers at MetLife Stadium. The Jets debuted their "White Out" uniforms for their home opener, which ended in a 20-17 overtime loss.

Spoiler alert: this is the first of three trades on this list to feature a former Panther underachieving with his new team.

As a first-round rookie in 2014, Benjamin made a strong impression with his 1,008 yards and nine touchdowns. He missed all of 2015 after tearing his ACL in training camp, then returned in 2016 to the tune of 941 more yards and seven more touchdowns.

Before the 2017 season, the Panthers exercised Benjamin's fifth-year option. He rewarded his team with another 1,000-yard season pace through eight games and going into the trade deadline. But in a fascinating move for the then-playoff-contending Panthers, who were 5-3 after Week 8, they traded Benjamin to Buffalo in a move that reportedly came together three minutes before the trade deadline.

Panthers officials explained it was to eliminate redundancy with the skill set of Devin Funchess, a 2015 second-rounder, and give the team more speed options. For the Bills, they got a big, downfield target for then-quarterback Tyrod Taylor after their 5-2 start inspired hope for an end to their 17-year playoff drought.

Benjamin never reached the heights he had in Carolina after heading to Buffalo. He tore his meniscus in his second game with the Bills and only played six games for the team in 2017. Benjamin caught 16 passes for 217 yards and a touchdown. He also had one, nine-yard catch in the playoffs.

Twelve games into his second season with the Bills, Benjamin had an NFL-low 37% catch rate: 23 catches on 62 targets. Buffalo released him on Dec. 4, 2018.

But the 2024 trade that sent Mingo to the Cowboys certainly has not gotten off to a good start. The Ole Miss product was a second-round pick by the Carolina Panthers in the 2023 NFL Draft and had a mediocre rookie season.

Despite having the second-most targets of any Carolina wideout (85), Mingo caught just about half (43), which was third-most on the team and barely ahead of running back Chuba Hubbard's 39 catches on 44 targets. His 51.8% catch percentage was the fifth-lowest among wideouts with at least 40 targets and the lowest among those with more than 65. Mingo also struggled with contested catches, creating separation on routes and generating yards after catch.

Mingo's 2024 season started worse, with an even lower catch rate (46.2%) on fewer targets (26) and still disappointing production (121 yards) through nine games. Yet Carolina managed to bundle their second-year wideout – who still hadn't recorded a touchdown in his career – with a seventh-round selection for a fourth-round pick at the 2024 deadline.

With the Cowboys in 2024, Mingo recorded five catches on 16 targets for 46 yards, and still no touchdowns.

Carolina used their fourth-round pick to select running back Trevor Etienne – who showed promise in limited touches as a rookie last year. Mingo began the 2025 season on injured reserve with a PCL sprain and saw five targets in six games. So far in 2026, Mingo has appeared in eight games, tallying three catches on eight targets for 30 yards.

The 2024 trade deadline did not age well for most of the season's buyers. Meanwhile, the Panthers came away as relative winners.

Where the Cowboys' trade for Mingo was bad for several reasons – it was an overpay, Dallas was 3-5 at the time and missed the playoffs, Mingo has never been a top contributor – at least the receiver is still with the team. Such is not the case for Johnson. He didn't even make it through his second month with Baltimore.

At first blush, the rich (Ravens) appeared to get richer with the trade. Carolina was 1-7 and in clear "sell" mode. Baltimore was 5-3, just had a 100-yard game from Rashod Bateman and a 100-yard game from Zay Flowers in back-to-back weeks. General manager Eric DeCosta still felt the team needed one more offensive weapon to take it over the top, and Johnson seemed like the fit.

The Ravens sent a fifth-round pick to the Panthers for Johnson and a sixth-rounder on Oct. 29, 2024, and by Dec. 29 that same year, Johnson was off the team. On Dec. 4, the team suspended the wideout for one game for conduct detrimental to the team – refusing to enter a game because he was cold. Johnson never played again for the Ravens – the team waived him on Dec. 20, one day before his scheduled return from a suspension.

Johnson appeared in one more game that year – for the Houston Texans, the team that claimed him off of waivers – but was waived by Houston before the playoffs and never played another NFL snap.

With the pick return, the Panthers selected tight end Mitchell Evans out of Notre Dame. To date, he has 29 catches for 298 yards and two touchdowns in his two NFL seasons.

Chase Claypool enjoyed two seasons of solid production with the Pittsburgh Steelers after the team made him its second-round pick in 2020. Across 2020 and 2021, the Notre Dame product had 121 catches for 1,733 yards and 11 touchdowns.

In his third season, Claypool's role in the Steelers' offense diminished slightly after the team selected receiver George Pickens in the second round of the 2022 draft.

After Week 8 of the 2022 season, the Chicago Bears were 3-5 after a loss to the Dallas Cowboys on the road. Seeking a boost for its passing offense, Chicago traded its 2023 second-round pick for Claypool, matching the Packers' offer for the Steelers wideout.

The British Columbia, Canada native never played a full season for the Bears. In seven games in 2022, he caught just 14 passes for 140 yards. The next year, Claypool played in just three games, tallying four receptions for 51 yards and his only Bears touchdown before expressing frustration about how the team was using him to the media. He never played another snap for the Bears before they traded him to Miami in Week 5.

Chicago went on to lose every game after trading for Claypool in 2022, and the second-round pick it gave up became the first pick on the second day of the 2023 draft. Pittsburgh drafted cornerback Joey Porter Jr. with the selection, and he went on to finish fifth in the Defensive Rookie of the Year voting.

NFL TRADE DEADLINE HISTORY: What are the best midseason deals ever? These 9 top the list

Trent Richardson tallied 950 yards and 11 touchdowns on the ground, 367 receiving yards, and one touchdown catch as a rookie in 2012. At the time, he seemed the lone bright spot in an otherwise brutal year for the Cleveland Browns. First-round quarterback and 28-year-old rookie Brandon Weeden had the fourth-worst passer rating in the NFL. Cleveland finished 5-11 and in last place in the AFC North.

So, when the Browns traded Richardson just two games into the 2013 season, it was a bit of a shock to the NFL world. The shock faded once the Indianapolis Colts got their hands on Richardson in his second year in exchange for a first-round pick.

By Week 12, in early December, Indianapolis had already demoted Richardson to a backup role after nine games, averaging fewer than three yards per carry and just two touchdowns.

The Alabama product was only slightly better in his first full year with the Colts in 2013, and the team waived him the following offseason. He never played another regular-season snap in the NFL.

Thanks to a solid second year from Colts quarterback Andrew Luck, the pick Indianapolis sent to Cleveland ended up in the back third of the first round. That year, the Browns traded up from that 26th pick to take another swing at a franchise quarterback: Johnny Manziel.

NFL TRADE DEADLINE CANDIDATES: Will the Jets spring another November surprise?

In 1973, a 33-year-old Hadl played in his first season with the Los Angeles Rams after 11 years with the San Diego Chargers. By the end of the year, the former Kansas Jayhawk earned a sixth Pro Bowl nod to go along with his first-team All-Pro honors (a career first) and second-place finish in MVP voting behind running back O.J. Simpson.

The following year, Hadl started the Rams' first five games before being benched in the fifth game in favor of James Harris, who became the first African-American quarterback to start an NFL playoff game later that year.

Seeing an opportunity to bring in a battle-tested veteran quarterback, the Green Bay Packers sent five draft picks (two first-round picks, two second-round picks, and a third-rounder) to Los Angeles in a midseason trade for Hadl in 1974. The deal only came about because a similar trade attempt for New Orleans Saints quarterback Archie Manning fell through.

Hadl was a disaster in Green Bay. During his one-and-a-half years with the Packers, he led the team to a 7-12 record while throwing just nine touchdowns to 29 interceptions.

Meanwhile, the Rams went on to win six straight NFC West division titles and made a Super Bowl appearance with the help of some of those draft picks. Both cornerbacks Monte Jackson and Pat Thomas, second-round picks for the Rams in 1975 and 1976, earned multiple Pro Bowl nods with Los Angeles.

The Minnesota Vikings' trade for Herschel Walker is among the worst trades in NFL history, midseason or otherwise. It even has its own Wikipedia page.

Walker had established himself as Dallas' best player by 1988, his third season as a pro.

The year before, the former Georgia Bulldog led the NFL with 1,606 yards from scrimmage on 269 touches and earned Pro Bowl and second-team All-Pro nods. In 1988, he rushed for 1,514 yards and five touchdowns and caught 53 passes for 505 receiving yards to put him at 2,019 scrimmage yards. It was enough for another Pro Bowl appearance, second-team All-Pro honors, fifth place in MVP voting, and a sixth-place finish for Offensive Player of the Year.

It was enough for a Vikings team fresh off two straight playoff appearances to make a blockbuster deal for the running back five weeks into the next year. In October 1989, Minnesota received Walker and three future draft picks for five players, three draft picks, and additional picks conditional on the Cowboys cutting those five players.

Ultimately, Dallas didn't play any of the players it acquired in the trade. As a result, the Cowboys got the Vikings' first- and second-round picks in 1990, 1991, and 1992, as well as Minnesota's sixth-round pick in 1990 and third-round pick in 1992.

The Cowboys eventually used those picks to make several additional trades, landing five other players, including running back Emmitt Smith and safety Darren Woodson, to build a roster that dominated the 1990s. Dallas won three Super Bowls between 1992 and 1995.

Meanwhile, Walker played just two full seasons for the Vikings and never exceeded 850 rushing yards in a season. Minnesota missed the playoffs in all but that first year, 1989, with its new running back.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: What are the worst NFL trade deadline deals ever? These 7 top the list

#2982. Hornets humiliate the Cavs 124-112

The Cleveland Cavaliers, in desperate need of a get-right game, put together one of their worst performances in recent (regular season) memory by getting blown out by the Charlotte Hornets 124-112 on Monday night. The Cavs fall a game under .500 before heading to New York on Wednesday to play the defending champion Knicks on national television.

Instead of using tonight's game as a momentum builder, the Cavs were laughed out of the arena by the woeful Hornets. Cleveland trailed by as many as 33 points against the NBA's worst offense in Charlotte, a team that was already missing several key players. Grayson Allen (27 points on 8-of-14 shooting) and Naz Reid (25 points, 13 rebounds) thoroughly outplayed Cleveland's starters, resulting in a 16-point deficit at the half. That ballooned to 27 points at the end of the third quarter, leading to none of the Cavs' starters returning to the game after that.

Jarrett Allen returned to the starting lineup, finishing with 12 points and seven rebounds in 19 minutes. Donovan Mitchell and James Harden had 11 points each and combined to shoot 7-20 from the floor. Harden in particular was disastrous and had some of the worst defensive possessions you will ever see from an NBA player. Evan Mobley had an exceptionally ineffective game, too, with nine points and four turnovers. Harden and Mobley had a combined +/- of -46 tonight, which passes the eye test.

Not only did Charlotte's starters outplay the Cavs, but Cleveland's backups did too. Craig Porter Jr., Thomas Bryant, Thomas, and Norchad Omier brought the Cavs within eight points late in the fourth quarter before running out of steam. But the reserves showed a lot of heart, and considerably more effort and energy than Cleveland's starters.

The Cavs were out-rebounded 49-32 overall, but didn't get their first offensive rebound until the third quarter, losing that battle 17-3. Cleveland actually ended up with fewer turnovers and shot better from the field than Charlotte, but the final score didn't reflect that.

This is the type of loss that reverberates through an organization, especially one that is expensive and on a fixed timeline like the Cavs. There is a heightened sense of urgency to right the ship, but Cleveland remains exceptionally inconsistent and underwhelming. Despite quality wins against Minnesota and Philadelphia, the Cavs feel like a team teetering on the edge of the abyss. Losses like tonight's are what push things over.

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2974. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney
